closest to a chosen endpoint of
\ell_{i}
.
The existence of such an interface is synonymous to
A_{10}^{\square}(\ell_{i})
.
Condition next on this interface, and bound the probability of existence of the second primal arm.
The conditioning induces both positive and negative information on the remaining edges, but
the information favorable to the existence of a primal path is at every scale around
\ell_{i}
at a macroscopic distance from
\ell_{i}
.
This suffices to obtain the extra polynomial term using (
2.2
).

To bound the probabilities of the events
A_{10}^{\square}(\ell_{i})
, define
\mathbf{N}
to be the number of disjoint clusters crossing
{\rm Ann}(R,2R)
from inside to outside. Then

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\phi_{\Lambda_{2R}}^{\xi}[A_{10}^{\square}(\ell_{i})]\leq\phi_{\Lambda_{2R}}^{\xi}[\mathbf{N}]\leq C_{1},

(3.2)

where the first inequality is a deterministic bound, and the second uniform bound on the expectation of
\mathbf{N}
which is a standard consequence of Theorem
2.1
sketched below:
observe that there exists
c_{2}>0
such that for every
k\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\phi_{\Lambda_{2R}}^{\xi}[\mathbf{N}\geq k+1|\mathbf{N}\geq k]\leq 1-c_{2}.

(3.3)

Indeed, conditionally on the
k
first clusters (in clockwise order around
\partial\Lambda_{R}
starting from some arbitrary point), observe that the complement
\Omega
in
{\rm Ann}(R,2R)
of these clusters is a subset of
{\rm Ann}(R,2R)
with free boundary conditions on the part of the boundary that lies strictly inside
{\rm Ann}(R,2R)
; on the rest of the boundary, the boundary conditions are dominated by wired ones. Then, a dual path disconnecting
\partial\Lambda_{R}
from
\partial\Lambda_{2R}
in
\Omega
occurs with probability at least
c_{2}>0
by Theorem
2.1
.
This proves (
3.3
), which in turn implies (
3.2
).

Combining (
3.1
) and (
3.2
), and adding a factor
2
to account for the existence of small primal petals, we find

\phi_{\Lambda_{2R}}^{\xi}[\mathscr{F}\text{ exists but is not\penalty\ $\eta$-well-separated}]\leq 2C_{0}\eta^{c_{0}}\,\sum_{i=1}^{k}\phi_{\Lambda_{2R}}^{\xi}[A_{10}^{\square}(\ell_{i})]\leq 2C_{0}C_{1}\eta^{c_{0}}.

Fixing
\eta
small enough concludes the proof.

\square

Definition 3.3
(Double four-petal flower domain)

Fix
1\leq r<R
.
We say that there exists a double four-petal flower domain between
\Lambda_{r}
and
\Lambda_{R}
if

•

the outer flower domain
\mathscr{F}_{out}
explored from
\Lambda_{(rR)^{1/2}}
to
\Lambda_{R}

exists, is
1/2
-well-separated and has exactly four petals
P_{1}^{out},\dots,P_{4}^{out}
;

•

the inner flower domain
\mathscr{F}_{in}
explored from
\Lambda_{(rR)^{1/2}}
to
\Lambda_{r}

exists, is
1/2
-well-separated and has exactly four petals
P_{1}^{in},\dots,P_{4}^{in}
;

•

P_{1}^{in}
is connected to
P_{1}^{out}
and
P_{3}^{in}
to
P_{3}^{out}
in
\omega\cap\mathscr{F}_{in}^{c}\cap\mathscr{F}_{out}^{c}
;

•

P_{2}^{in}
is connected to
P_{2}^{out}
and
P_{4}^{in}
to
P_{4}^{out}
in
\omega^{*}\cap\mathscr{F}_{in}^{c}\cap\mathscr{F}_{out}^{c}
.

The advantage of the double four-petal flower domain is that it can be explored from
\partial\Lambda_{(rR)^{1/2}}
towards the inside and outside,
and limits the interaction between the configurations in
\mathscr{F}_{in}
and
\mathscr{F}_{out}
.

Lemma 3.4

For any
\eta>1\penalty\
, there exists
c=c(\eta)>0
such that
for any
p\in(0,1)
, any
R<L(p)
large enough and any boundary conditions
\xi
on
{\rm Ann}:={\rm Ann}((1-\eta)R,(1+2\eta)R)

\displaystyle\phi_{{\rm Ann}}^{\xi}[\text{there exists a double four-petal flower domain between\penalty\ $\Lambda_{R}$ and\penalty\ $\Lambda_{(1+\eta)R}$}]>c.

Proof

We recommend taking a look at Figure
2
. Set
R^{\prime\prime}:=(1+\eta)R
and
R^{\prime}:=(RR^{\prime\prime})^{1/2}
.
Consider the rectangles

\displaystyle{\rm Rect}_{1}

\displaystyle:=[R,R^{\prime\prime}]\times[0,\eta R],

\displaystyle{\rm Rect}_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=[R,R^{\prime\prime}]\times[-\eta R,0],

as well as their rotations
{\rm Rect}_{2},{\rm Rect}_{3},{\rm Rect}_{4}
and
{\rm Rect}^{\prime}_{2},{\rm Rect}^{\prime}_{3},{\rm Rect}_{4}^{\prime}
by angles of
\pi/2,\pi,3\pi/2
.

Define the event
E
that
{\rm Rect}_{1},{\rm Rect}^{\prime}_{2},{\rm Rect}_{3},{\rm Rect}_{4}^{\prime}
(resp.
{\rm Rect}^{\prime}_{1},{\rm Rect}_{2},{\rm Rect}^{\prime}_{3},{\rm Rect}_{4}
) are crossed by a primal (resp. dual) path from
\partial\Lambda_{R}
to
\partial\Lambda_{R^{\prime\prime}}
. Theorem
2.1
implies the existence of
c_{0}>0
such that

\phi^{\xi}_{\rm Ann}[E]\geq c_{0}.

(3.4)

Let
F
be the event that there exist exactly two clusters in
{\rm Ann}(R,R^{\prime})
crossing from
\partial\Lambda_{R}
to
\partial\Lambda_{R^{\prime}}
, and exactly two clusters in
{\rm Ann}(R^{\prime},R^{\prime\prime})
crossing from
\partial\Lambda_{R^{\prime}}
to
\partial\Lambda_{R^{\prime\prime}}
. We claim that

\phi^{\xi}_{\rm Ann}[F|E]\geq c_{1}.

(3.5)

When
F\cap E
occurs, both flower domains
\mathscr{F}_{in}
and
\mathscr{F}_{out}
exist, have four petals and are
1/2
-well separated.
Thus, (
3.4
) and (
3.5
) imply directly the result, and we focus next on its proof.

Let
\Gamma_{1}
be the bottom boundary of the cluster of
\{R\}\times[0,\eta R]
in
{\rm Rect}_{1}\cup{\rm Rect}_{1}^{\prime}
,

\Gamma_{2}
be the left boundary of the cluster of
[0,\eta R]\times\{R\}
in
{\rm Rect}_{2}\cup{\rm Rect}^{\prime}_{2}
,

\Gamma_{3}
be the top boundary of the cluster of
\{-R\}\times[-\eta R,0]
in
{\rm Rect}_{3}\cup{\rm Rect}_{3}^{\prime}
and

\Gamma_{4}
be the right boundary of the cluster of
[-\eta R,0]\times\{-R\}
in
{\rm Rect}_{4}\cup{\rm Rect}^{\prime}_{4}
.
Notice that each
\Gamma_{i}
may be explored by a standard procedure, starting from the common inner corner of
{\rm Rect}_{i}
and
{\rm Rect}_{i}^{\prime}
.
Moreover,
E
implies that each
\Gamma_{i}
is contained in
{\rm Rect}_{i}\cup{\rm Rect}^{\prime}_{i}
and ends on
\partial\Lambda_{R^{\prime\prime}}
.

Condition now on a realisation of
\Gamma_{1},\dots,\Gamma_{4}
with the properties above.
By Theorem
2.1
, one may construct with uniformly positive probability four primal paths and four dual ones as in Figure
2
, namely:
two primal paths connecting
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
in the top-right corners of
{\rm Ann}(R,R^{\prime})
and
{\rm Ann}(R^{\prime},R^{\prime\prime})
, respectively;
two primal paths connecting
\Gamma_{3}
and
\Gamma_{4}
in the bottom-left corners of the same annuli;
two dual paths connecting the dual vertices adjacent to
\Gamma_{2}
and
\Gamma_{3}
in the top-left corners and
and two more between the dual vertices adjacent to
\Gamma_{4}
and
\Gamma_{1}
in the bottom-right corner of these two annuli.
When all these paths exist, both
E
and
F
occur. This concludes the proof of (
3.5
) and therefore the whole argument.

\square

Figure 2:
In grey and darker grey (respectively): the rectangles
{\rm Rect}_{i}
and
{\rm Rect}_{i}^{\prime}
. We depicted in red some possible realization for the paths
\Gamma_{1},\dots,\Gamma_{4}
. In blue, possible realizations of paths and dual paths enforcing the event in blue.

3.2
Boosting pair of boundary conditions

The goal of this section is to study how changes of boundary conditions impact crossing probabilities.

Definition 3.5

A boosting pair of boundary conditions for a flower domain
\mathscr{F}

is a pair of boundary conditions
(\xi,\xi^{\prime})
such that

•

\xi
and
\xi^{\prime}
are compatible with
\mathscr{F}
,

•

\xi\leq\xi^{\prime}
,

•

there exist two primal petals of
\mathscr{F}
that are wired together in
\xi^{\prime}
but not in
\xi
.

In a slight abuse of notation, we will henceforth also call a pair
(\zeta,\zeta^{\prime})
of boundary conditions on
\mathscr{F}
boosting
if there exists a boosting pair
(\xi,\xi^{\prime})
of boundary conditions on
\mathscr{F}
(in the sense of the definition above) such that
\zeta\leq\xi<\xi^{\prime}\leq\zeta^{\prime}
.

Recall from Section
2.2
that
A_{R}
is the event that there exists a circuit in
{\rm Ann}(R,2R)
surrounding
0
.
The object of this section is to prove the following theorem.

Theorem 3.6

Fix
q\in(1,4]
. For every
\eta>0
, there exists
\delta=\delta(\eta,q)>0
such that the following holds.

(i)

For every
p\in(0,1)
, every
R\leq L(p)
,
every
\eta
-well-separated inner flower domain
\mathscr{F}
on
\Lambda_{2R}
,
every boosting pair
(\xi,\xi^{\prime})
of boundary conditions on
\mathscr{F}
,
and every
\eta
-regular quad
(\mathscr{D},a,b,c,d)
of size
R
,

\displaystyle\phi^{\xi^{\prime}}_{\mathscr{F}}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]

\displaystyle\geq\phi^{\xi}_{\mathscr{F}}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]+\delta,

(3.6)

\displaystyle\phi^{\xi^{\prime}}_{\mathscr{F}}[A_{R}]

\displaystyle\geq\phi^{\xi}_{\mathscr{F}}[A_{R}]+\delta.

(3.7)

(ii)

For every
p\in(0,1)
, every
R\leq L(p)
,
every
\eta
-well-separated outer flower domain
\mathscr{F}
on
\Lambda_{R}
,
every boosting pair
(\xi,\xi^{\prime})
of boundary conditions on
\mathscr{F}
,
and every
\eta
-regular quad
(\mathscr{D},a,b,c,d)
of size
R
translated in such a way that it is contained in
{\rm Ann}(2R,4R)
,

\displaystyle\phi^{\xi^{\prime}}_{\mathscr{F}}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]

\displaystyle\geq\phi^{\xi}_{\mathscr{F}}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]+\delta,

(3.8)

\displaystyle\phi^{\xi^{\prime}}_{\mathscr{F}}[A_{R}]

\displaystyle\geq\phi^{\xi}_{\mathscr{F}}[A_{R}]+\delta.

(3.9)

In light of the RSW theory, the crossing probabilities
\phi^{\xi}_{\mathscr{F}}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]
and
\phi^{\xi^{\prime}}_{\mathscr{F}}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]
are bounded away from
0
and
1
by constants depending only on
\eta
.
Above we are concerned with the amount by which such crossing probabilities increase when the boundary conditions change from
\xi
to
\xi^{\prime}
.
Indeed, the theorem states that the increase is positive, uniformly in the scale, the quad to be crossed and the boosting pair of boundary conditions.
The rest of the section is dedicated to proving Theorem
3.6
.

The following lemma is the cornerstone for the proof.
For a quad
(\mathscr{D},a,b,c,d)
, let
{\rm mix}
be the boundary conditions on
\mathscr{D}
corresponding to the partitions containing
(ab)
,
(cd)
and singletons, and
{\rm mix}^{\prime}
be the one containing
(ab)\cup(cd)
and singletons.

Lemma 3.7

For every
q>1
,
p\in(0,1)
, and every quad
(\mathscr{D},a,b,c,d)
, we have

\phi_{\mathscr{D}}^{\rm mix^{\prime}}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]=\frac{q}{1+(q-1)\phi_{\mathscr{D}}^{\rm mix}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]}\phi_{\mathscr{D}}^{\rm mix}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})].

(3.10)

Notice that the ratio in the right-hand side above is always larger than
1
, and considerably so when
\phi_{\mathscr{D}}^{\rm mix}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]

is far from
1
.

Proof

Let
w(\omega):=(\tfrac{p}{1-p})^{|\omega|}q^{k(\omega^{\rm mix})}
and
w^{\prime}(\omega):=(\tfrac{p}{1-p})^{|\omega|}q^{k(\omega^{\rm mix^{\prime}})}
and observe that

w(\omega)=\begin{cases}w^{\prime}(\omega)&\text{ if }\omega\in\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D}),\\
qw^{\prime}(\omega)&\text{ if }\omega\notin\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D}).\end{cases}

Now, set

\displaystyle Z[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\omega\in\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})}w(\omega)\quad\ ,\quad Z[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})^{c}]:=\sum_{\omega\notin\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})}w(\omega),

\displaystyle Z^{\prime}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\omega\in\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})}w^{\prime}(\omega)\quad,\quad Z^{\prime}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})^{c}]:=\sum_{\omega\notin\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})}w^{\prime}(\omega).

Then

\displaystyle{\phi^{\rm mix^{\prime}}_{\mathscr{D}}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]}=\frac{Z^{\prime}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]}{Z^{\prime}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]+Z^{\prime}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})^{c}]}

\displaystyle=\frac{Z[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]}{Z[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]+\tfrac{1}{q}Z[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})^{c}]}

\displaystyle=\frac{\phi^{\rm mix}_{\mathscr{D}}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]}{\phi^{\rm mix}_{\mathscr{D}}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]+\tfrac{1}{q}(1-\phi^{\rm mix}_{\mathscr{D}}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})])},

which is the desired equality.

\square

Figure 3:
The domain
\mathscr{D}
with the four marked points
a,b,c,d
in grey. The outer flower
\mathscr{D}
is in black. In this case,
P_{3}
is assumed to be connected to
P_{7}
in
\xi^{\prime}
but not in
\xi
.
The red depicts the double flower-domain
(\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out})
which is explored first.
Then, conditionally on the realization of
(\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out})
, the conditions for the event
E
to occur are depicted in blue (note that the blue connections from
\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}
to
\mathscr{F}
do not necessarily need to cross the arcs
(ab)
and
(cd)
of
\mathscr{D}
).
At the time
\tau
of the procedure, the red and blue parts have been revealed and only the inside of
\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}
is unexplored.
Then, the event
\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})
depends on the connection inside
\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}
between its primal petals,
which, with positive probability, are connected in
\omega^{\prime}
, but not in
\omega
(see the green paths).

Proof of Theorem
3.6

We will focus on inner flower domains; the case of outer flower domains is very similar.
Let
\mathscr{F}
be an
\eta
-well-separated inner flower domain on
\Lambda_{2R}
and let
(\xi,\xi^{\prime})
be a boosting pair of boundary conditions.
Write
P_{1},\dots,P_{2k}
for the petals of
\mathscr{F}
in counter-clockwise order, indexed in such a way that
P_{1}
is primal.
Fix
i
and
j
odd such that
P_{i}
is wired to
P_{j}
in
\xi^{\prime}
but not in
\xi
.
Below,
c_{0},\dots,c_{4}
will denote strictly positive constants depending only on
\eta
.

We start by proving (
3.6
). We recommend to take a look at Figure
3
.
Fix an
\eta
-regular quad
(\mathscr{D},a,b,c,d)
of size
R
and translate everything in such a way that the box
\Lambda_{\eta R}

is included in
\mathscr{D}
.
Consider the event
E
that there exists a double four-petal flower domain
(\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out})
between
\Lambda_{\eta R/4}
and
\Lambda_{\eta R/2}

and that

•

P_{1}^{\rm out}
and
P_{3}^{\rm out}
are connected to
P_{i}
and
P_{j}
in
\mathscr{F}\cap\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}
, respectively;

•

P_{1}^{\rm out}
and
P_{3}^{\rm out}
are not connected to each other, nor to any other primal petal of
\mathscr{F}
in
\mathscr{F}\cap\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}
;

•

P_{1}^{\rm out}
and
P_{3}^{\rm out}
are connected to the arcs
(ab)
and
(cd)
in
\mathscr{D}\cap\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}
respectively.

•

P_{2}^{\rm out}
and
P_{4}^{\rm out}
are dually-connected to the arcs
(bc)
and
(da)
in
\mathscr{D}\cap\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}
.

Theorem
2.1
and Lemma
3.4
give

\phi^{\xi}_{\mathscr{F}}[E]\geq c_{0}>0.

(3.11)

Consider the coupling
\mathbb{P}_{\bf T}
between
\phi^{\xi}_{\mathscr{F}}
and
\phi^{\xi^{\prime}}_{\mathscr{F}}
,
obtained by first exploring
(\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out})
in
\omega
,
then revealing all the edges in
\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}\cap\mathscr{F}
, then all those inside
\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}
.
Let
\tau
be the stopping time corresponding to the end of the second step of this exploration.

Suppose that
\omega_{[\tau]}\in E
.
Then
\mathscr{D}
is crossed in
\omega
if and only if the petals
P_{1}^{\rm in}
and
P_{3}^{\rm in}
are connected inside
\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}
.
Write
\zeta<\zeta^{\prime}
for the two boundary conditions on
\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}
which are coherent with the flower domain structure
(with
P_{1}^{\rm in}
wired to
P_{3}^{\rm in}
in
\zeta^{\prime}
, but not in
\zeta
).
Then the boundary conditions induced by
\omega_{[\tau]}
and
\xi
on
\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}
are
\zeta
.
Moreover, since
\omega^{\prime}\geq\omega
and due to the wiring of
P_{i}
and
P_{j}
in
\xi^{\prime}
,

\omega_{[\tau]}^{\prime}
induces the boundary conditions on
\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}
that dominate
\zeta^{\prime}
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\phi^{\xi^{\prime}}_{\mathscr{F}}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]-\phi^{\xi}_{\mathscr{F}}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{\bf T}[\omega^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D}),\,\omega\notin\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}_{\bf T}\Big[\big(\phi_{\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}}^{\zeta^{\prime}}[P_{1}^{\rm in}\leftrightarrow P_{3}^{\rm in}]-\phi_{\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}}^{\zeta}[P_{1}^{\rm in}\leftrightarrow P_{3}^{\rm in}]\big)\mathbf{1}_{\omega_{[\tau]}\in E}\Big]

\displaystyle\geq c_{1}\mathbb{P}_{\bf T}[\omega_{[\tau]}\in E]\geq c_{1}\,c_{0}>0,

where
c_{1}>0
is given by Lemma
3.7
and Theorem
2.1
.
This concludes the proof of (
3.6
).

We turn to (
3.7
) and refer the reader to Figure
4
.
Write
z
for the point
(3R/2,0)
.
Consider the coupling
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{T}}
between
\phi^{\xi}_{\mathscr{F}}
and
\phi^{\xi^{\prime}}_{\mathscr{F}}
obtained by first exploring the
double flower domain
(\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out})
in
\omega
between
\Lambda_{R/16}(z)
and
\Lambda_{R/8}(z)
,
then the configurations inside
\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}
and finally those in
\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}
.
If no double flower domain exists, reveal all remaining edges in arbitrary order.
Write
\tau_{1}
for the stopping time marking the end of the exploration of
(\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out})
,
and
\tau_{2}
the stopping time after further exploring
\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}
.

Condition on a pair of configurations
(\omega_{[\tau_{1}]},\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau_{1}]})
such that the double flower domain exists,
and write
\zeta<\zeta^{\prime}
for the two boundary conditions on
\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}
that are coherent with the flower domain structure.
The configuration
\omega
inside
\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}
is sampled according to a convex combination of
\phi_{\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}}^{\zeta}

and
\phi_{\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}}^{\zeta^{\prime}}
.
Indeed, the coefficient
\lambda
for the latter measure is given by
the probability that
P_{1}^{\rm out}
is connected to
P_{3}^{\rm out}
in
\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}
, including via the boundary conditions
\xi
.
Similarly, the law of
\omega^{\prime}
inside
\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}
dominates a convex combination of
\phi_{\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}}^{\zeta}

and
\phi_{\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}}^{\zeta^{\prime}}
, with the coefficient
\lambda^{\prime}
for the latter given by
the probability that
P_{1}^{\rm out}
is connected to
P_{3}^{\rm out}
in
\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}
, including via the boundary conditions
\xi^{\prime}
.

As shown in the first point of the proof with the event
E
,
there is a uniformly positive probability
c_{2}>0
for
P_{1}^{\rm out}
and
P_{3}^{\rm out}
to be connected
in
\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}
to
P_{i}
and
P_{j}
respectively, but not to each other.
Thus,
\lambda^{\prime}\geq\lambda+c_{2}
.
Applying Lemma
3.7
and Theorem
2.1
we find

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{T}}[P_{1}^{\rm in}\xleftrightarrow{\omega^{\prime}\cap\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}}P_{3}^{\rm in}\text{ but }P_{1}^{\rm in}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu/\hfil$\crcr$\displaystyle\xleftrightarrow{\omega\cap\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}}$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\hfil\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu/\hfil$\crcr$\textstyle\xleftrightarrow{\omega\cap\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}}$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu/\hfil$\crcr$\scriptstyle\xleftrightarrow{\omega\cap\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}}$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu/\hfil$\crcr$\scriptscriptstyle\xleftrightarrow{\omega\cap\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}}$\crcr}}}}P_{3}^{\rm in}\,|\,\omega_{[\tau_{1}]},\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau_{1}]}\text{ s.t.\penalty\ $(\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out})$ exists}]

\displaystyle\geq(\phi_{\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}}^{\zeta^{\prime}}[P_{1}^{\rm in}\xleftrightarrow{\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}}P_{3}^{\rm in}]-\phi_{\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}}^{\zeta}[P_{1}^{\rm in}\xleftrightarrow{\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}}P_{3}^{\rm in}])(\lambda-\lambda^{\prime})

\displaystyle\geq c_{3},

for some
c_{3}>0
.

Write
F
for the event that
(\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out})
exists and that
P_{1}^{\rm in}
is connected to
P_{3}^{\rm in}

inside
\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}
in
\omega^{\prime}
, but not in
\omega
.
This event is measurable in terms of the configurations at the stopping time
\tau_{2}
.
Finally, write
H
for the event that in
\omega
,

•

in
\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}\cap{\rm Ann}(R,2R)
,

P_{1}^{\rm out}
is connected to
P_{3}^{\rm out}
by a primal path,

•

P_{2}^{\rm out}
is connected to
\Lambda_{R}
by a dual path, and

•

P_{4}^{\rm out}
is connected to
\Lambda_{2R}^{c}
by a dual path.

Notice that, if
H
occurs, the primal path connecting
P_{1}^{\rm out}
to
P_{3}^{\rm out}
needs to “wind around”
\Lambda_{R}
.
Thus,
H
may be understood as the connection between
P_{1}^{\rm in}
and
P_{3}^{\rm in}
in
\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}
being “pivotal” for
A_{R}
(for
\omega
).
By Theorem
2.1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{T}}[\omega\in H\,|\,(\omega_{[\tau_{2}]},\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau_{2}]})\text{ such that\penalty\ $F$ occurs}]>c_{4},

for some
c_{4}>0
.
Moreover, when
F
and
H
occur, then
A_{R}
occurs for
\omega^{\prime}
, but not for
\omega
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\phi^{\xi^{1}}_{\mathscr{F}}[A_{R}]-\phi^{\xi^{0}}_{\mathscr{F}}[A_{R}]=\mathbb{P}_{\bf T}[\omega\in H\text{ and }F]\geq c_{3}\,c_{4}>0,

which is the desired conclusion.

\square

Figure 4:
A depiction of the annulus configuration in the proof of (
3.7
). In black, the flower domain
\mathscr{F}
. The red part corresponds to the double flower-domain
(\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out})
explored at time
\tau_{1}
. The blue part corresponds to what is explored at time
\tau_{2}
, i.e. a connection in
\omega^{\prime}
between two wired petals of
\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}
together with a dual connection in
\omega^{*}
between two free petals. Then after time
\tau_{2}
, we construct the event
H
(in green).

Remark 3.8

The proof of (
3.7
) may appear contradictory,
as we are first arguing that
P_{1}^{\rm out}
and
P_{3}^{\rm out}
may appear wired in
\omega^{\prime}
but not in
\omega
,
then we focus on the event
\omega\in H
which ensures that
P_{1}^{\rm out}
and
P_{3}^{\rm out}
are connected in both
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}
.
The reader should keep in mind that the configurations
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}
in
\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}
are sampled before sampling the configurations in
\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}
,
and their laws are obtained by averaging over the possible configurations in
\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}
.

Alternatively, one may imagine a coupling where
(\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out})
is explored first,
then the configurations in
\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}
are revealed, then those in
\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}

and finally the configurations in
\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}
are resampled.
In this context, we are investigating the situation where,
in the first sampling of the configurations in
\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}
,

P_{1}^{\rm out}
and
P_{3}^{\rm out}
are connected to
P_{i}
and
P_{j}
, respectively, but not to each other,
then, in the sampling inside
\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}
,
P_{1}^{\rm in}
and
P_{3}^{\rm in}
are connected in
\omega^{\prime}
, but not in
\omega
,
and finally, in the second sampling in
\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}
,
H
occurs for
\omega
.

3.3
Crossing quads produce boosting pairs

This section is concerned with the following result which roughly states that
conditioning on the existence of the crossing of a quad has the same effect as a boosting pair of boundary conditions.

Proposition 3.9

For any
\eta>0
, there exists a constant
c=c(\eta,q)>0
such that the following holds.
Fix
p
and let
(\mathscr{D},a,b,c,d)
be an
\eta
-regular discrete quad at scale
R\leq L(p)
.
Then there exists a coupling via decision trees
\mathbb{P}
of
\phi_{p}
and
\phi_{p}[\cdot\,|\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]
, and a stopping time
\tau
such that, when
\tau<\infty
,

\mathscr{F}_{\tau}=\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus e_{[\tau]}
is a
1/2
-well-separated outer flower domain on
\Lambda_{2R}
,
and the boundary conditions induced by
\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau]}
on
\mathscr{F}_{\tau}
are a boost of those induced by
\omega_{[\tau]}
.
Finally,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\tau<\infty]\geq c.

Remark 3.10

We may replace
\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})
by
A_{R/2}
in the statement above.
Indeed, if we set
x=(0,3R/4)
, observe that
A_{R/2}
induces a vertical crossing of the
\eta
-regular discrete quad
\Lambda_{R/4}(x)

(with
a,b,c,d
the corners of
\Lambda_{R/4}(x)
, starting with the top-left one).
As a consequence, the measure
\phi_{p}[\cdot\,|A_{R/2}]
dominates
\phi_{p}[\cdot\,|\mathscr{C}(\Lambda_{R/4}(x))]
.
Now, apply the decision tree provided by Proposition
3.9
to couple in an increasing fashion the measures

\phi_{p}
,
\phi_{p}[\cdot\,|\mathscr{C}(\Lambda_{R/4}(x))]
and
\phi_{p}[\cdot\,|A_{R/2}]
; call
\omega\leq\omega^{\prime}\leq\omega^{\prime\prime}
the resulting configurations.
Then, if
\tau<\infty
,
the boundary conditions induced by
\omega^{\prime\prime}_{[\tau]}
on
\mathscr{F}_{\tau}
dominate those induced by
\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau]}
,
and hence are a boost of those induced by
\omega_{[\tau]}
.

The proof of Proposition
3.9
is based on the following lemma,
which allows us to “lengthen” crossings at a small cost.
For an
\eta
-regular discrete quad
(\mathscr{D},a,b,c,d)
at some scale
R
and for some
m\leq\eta R/4
,
define two modified quads
(\mathscr{D}^{+},a^{+},b^{+},c^{+},d^{+})
and
(\mathscr{D}^{-},a^{-},b^{-},c^{-},d^{-})
as follows (see Figure
5
for an illustration).
The domains
\mathscr{D}^{+}
and
\mathscr{D}^{-}
are formed by the union of
\mathscr{D}

with the set of edges of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\mathscr{D}
that are at a
\ell^{\infty}
distance at most
m
from
the arcs
(ab)
and
(cd)
(respectively
(bc)
and
(da)
),
but at least distance
m
from
a
,
b
,
c
and
d
.
The point
a^{+}
is the first point of
\partial\mathscr{D}^{+}
in counter-clockwise order after
a

that is at a distance
m
from
\partial\mathscr{D}
; the point
b^{+}
is the last such point before
b
.
The points
c^{+}
and
d^{+}
are defined similarly in terms of
c
and
d
respectively.
A similar definition applies to
a^{-}
,
b^{-}
,
c^{-}
and
d^{-}
.
Notice that
(\mathscr{D}^{+},a^{+},b^{+},c^{+},d^{+})
and
(\mathscr{D}^{-},a^{-},b^{-},c^{-},d^{-})
are both discrete quads and that

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D}^{+})\subset\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})\subset\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D}^{-}).

Figure 5:
Left:
For the lowest crossing
\Gamma
of
\mathscr{D}
not to be connected to
(a^{+}b^{+})
,
there needs to exist a dual path from
\Lambda_{m}(x)
to the arc
(d^{+}a^{+})
in the unexplored region above
\Gamma
.

Middle:
At the time
\tau_{2}<\infty
, when
\Gamma^{+}
and
\Gamma^{-}
have been revealed,
the region of
\mathscr{D}^{+}\setminus\mathscr{D}
above
\Gamma^{+}
and that of
\mathscr{D}^{-}\setminus\mathscr{D}
left of
\Gamma^{-}
are unexplored.

Right:
The unexplored regions may be used to connect
\Gamma^{+}
to the primal petals of
\mathscr{F}
in
\omega

and
\Gamma^{-}
to the dual petals of
\mathscr{F}
in
\omega^{*}
. This ensures that the boundary conditions induced by
\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau]}
on
\mathscr{F}

are a boost of those induced by
\omega_{[\tau]}
.

Remark 3.11

The choice of
m\leq\eta R/4
and the fact that
\mathscr{D}
is
\eta
-regular guarantee that
\mathscr{D}^{+}\setminus\mathscr{D}
and
\mathscr{D}^{-}\setminus\mathscr{D}

are at a distance at least
\eta R/4
of each other and are each made of two separate connected components. In particular, the complement of
\mathscr{D}^{+}\cup\mathscr{D}^{-}
is connected.

Lemma 3.12

For any
\eta>0
, there exist
C=C(\eta)>0
,
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(\eta)>0
such that the following holds.
For any
p\in(0,1)
,
R\leq L(p)
,
m<\eta R/4

and any
\eta
-regular discrete quad
(\mathscr{D},a,b,c,d)
at scale
R\leq L(p)
.

\displaystyle\phi_{p}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]-\phi_{p}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D}^{+})]

\displaystyle\leq C(m/R)^{\varepsilon},

(3.12)

\displaystyle\phi_{p}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D}^{-})]-\phi_{p}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]

\displaystyle\leq C(m/R)^{\varepsilon}.

(3.13)

Proof

We will focus on the first inequality; the second one is the same inequality applied to the dual model.
Since
\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D}^{+})\subset\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})
, we are searching for an upper bound on

\phi_{p}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})\setminus\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D}^{+})]
.
When
\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})
occurs, let
\Gamma
be the “lowest” crossing of
\mathscr{D}
, that is the open path closest to the arc
(bc)
,
with endpoints
x\in(ab)
and
y\in(cd)
. Write
\mathsf{Under}(\Gamma)
for the set of edges of
\mathscr{D}
between
\Gamma
and
(bc)
.

If
\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})\setminus\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D}^{+})
occurs, then at least one of the following four events needs to occur

(i)

x
is at a distance at most
\sqrt{mR}
from
a
;

(ii)

x
is at a distance at least
\sqrt{mR}
from
a
, but
\Gamma
is not connected to
(a^{+}b^{+})
in
\mathscr{D}^{+}
;

(iii)

y
is at a distance at most
\sqrt{mR}
from
d
;

(iv)

y
is at a distance at least
\sqrt{mR}
from
d
, but
\Gamma
is not connected to
(c^{+}d^{+})
in
\mathscr{D}^{+}
.

Next we bound the probability of each of the four events described above.

If the event in (i) occurs, there exists a primal arm, namely
\Gamma
, contained in
\mathscr{D}
from
\Lambda_{\sqrt{mR}}(a)
to distance
\eta R
; and therefore using (
2.2
) we get

\phi_{p}[(i)\text{ occurs}]\leq\pi_{1}(p;\sqrt{mR},\eta R)\leq C(\eta)(m/R)^{\varepsilon}.

If the event in (ii) occurs, then there exists a dual-open path from
\Lambda_{m}(x)
to
(d^{+}a^{+})
contained in
\mathscr{D}^{+}\setminus\mathsf{Under}(\Gamma)
; see Figure
5
(left diagram).
It is a standard consequence of Theorem
2.1
(the near-critical RSW) that there exist
\varepsilon,C>0
such that,
for any realisation of
\Gamma
such that (i) fails,

\displaystyle\phi_{p}[\Lambda_{m}(x)\xleftrightarrow{\omega^{*}\cap(\mathscr{D}^{+}\setminus\mathsf{Under}(\Gamma))}(da)\,|\,\Gamma\text{ and }\omega\text{ on }\mathsf{Under}(\Gamma)]\leq C(m/R)^{\varepsilon}.

Indeed, notice that the conditioning above induces both positive and negative information on
\mathsf{Under}(\Gamma)^{c}
.
However, due to the
\eta
-regularity of
\mathscr{D}
, the information that is favorable to the existence of dual connections is at every scale around
x
at a macroscopic distance from

\Lambda_{m}^{c}(x)\cap(\mathscr{D}^{+}\setminus\mathsf{Under}(\Gamma))
; see Figure
5
(left diagram).

The bounds above also apply to the events in (iii) and (iv). When combined using a union bound, we find

\phi_{p}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})\setminus\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D}^{+})]\leq 4C(m/R)^{\varepsilon}.

\square

Remark 3.13

Alternatively, one may use bounds on the probability of the three-arm event in the half-plane to prove the above.
We prefer the strategy above as it adapts easily to fractal quads of bounded extremal distance;
the cases (i) and (ii) (and (iii) and (iv), respectively) should then be distinguished using extremal distance rather than the geometric average of
m
and
R
.
Above, the quad
\mathscr{D}
is assumed to be
\eta
-regular simply for convenience.

Proof of Proposition
3.9

Fix
\eta>0
and let
C=C(\eta)
and
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(\eta)
be the constants given by Lemma
3.12
for this value of
\eta
.
Below,
c_{0},\dots,c_{3}
denote strictly positive constants depending only on
\eta
.

For
p
and
R
with
R\leq L(p)
, let
m=c_{0}R
be such that

C(m/R)^{\varepsilon}<\tfrac{1}{4}\inf_{\mathscr{D}}\phi_{p}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]\phi_{p}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})^{c}],

where the infimum is taken over all
\eta
-regular quads
\mathscr{D}
at scale
R
.
By Theorem
2.1
, the infimum is uniformly positive, and therefore
c_{0}>0
above does indeed depend only on
\eta
.

Fix
\mathscr{D}
as in the statement of the proposition.
Then, in any increasing coupling
\mathbb{P}
of
\phi_{p}[\cdot]
and
\phi_{p}[\cdot\,|\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]
(note that
\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})
occurs automatically for
\omega^{\prime}
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\omega^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D}^{+}),

\displaystyle\,\omega\notin\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D}^{-})]

\displaystyle\geq 1-\phi_{p}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]-\phi_{p}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D}^{-})\setminus\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]-\phi_{p}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D}^{+})^{c}\setminus\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})^{c}\,|\,\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]

\displaystyle\geq\phi_{p}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})^{c}]-C(m/R)^{\varepsilon}-\frac{C(m/R)^{\varepsilon}}{\phi_{p}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]}

\displaystyle\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\phi_{p}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})^{c}]\geq c_{1}>0.

Next, we create an increasing coupling
\mathbb{P}
between
\phi_{p}
and
\phi_{p}[\cdot\,|\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]
using a specific decision tree described below.
Start by exploring the lowest crossing
\Gamma^{+}
in
\mathscr{D}^{+}
from
(a^{+}b^{+})
to
(c^{+}d^{+})
(that is the crossing closest to
(b^{+}c^{+})
)
in the larger configuration
\omega^{\prime}
.
Write
\tau_{1}
for the stopping time when this crossing is found; set
\tau_{1}=\infty
if no such crossing exists.
If
\tau_{1}<\infty
, explore the “right-most” dual crossing
\Gamma^{-}
in
\mathscr{D}^{-}
from
(b^{-}c^{-})
to
(d^{-}a^{-})

(that is the one closest to
(c^{-}d^{-})
) in the configuration
\omega^{*}
;
define
\tau_{2}
for the stopping time when this crossing is found (if
\tau_{1}=\infty
or no such crossing is found, set
\tau_{2}=\infty
).
Notice that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\tau_{2}<\infty]=\mathbb{P}[\omega^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D}^{+}),\omega\notin\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D}^{-})]\geq c_{1}.

Assuming that
\tau_{2}<\infty
, the explored edges
e_{[\tau_{2}]}
are those of
\mathscr{D}^{+}
below
\Gamma^{+}
and those of
\mathscr{D}^{-}
right or
\Gamma^{-}
.
In particular, the edges of
\mathscr{D}^{+}\setminus\mathscr{D}
that are above
\Gamma^{+}
,
as well as those of
\mathscr{D}^{-}\setminus\mathscr{D}
that are left of
\Gamma^{-}
are unexplored. See Figure
5
(middle diagram).

Next, explore the double four-petal flower domain
(\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out})
between
\Lambda_{3R/2}
and
\Lambda_{2R}
;
let
\tau_{3}
be the stopping time marking the end of this exploration
(with
\tau_{3}=\infty
if no double four-petal flower domain exists or if
\tau_{2}=\infty
).
Due to Lemma
3.4
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\tau_{3}<\infty\,|\,(\omega_{[\tau_{2}]},\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau_{2}]})\text{ s.t. }\tau_{2}<\infty]\geq c_{2}.

Finally, reveal the configurations in the unexplored regions of
\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}
and write
\tau_{4}
for the stopping time marking the end of this stage.
Let
H
be the event that
P_{1}^{\rm in}
and
P_{3}^{\rm in}
are connected by paths of
\omega^{\prime}\cap(\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}^{c}\setminus\mathscr{D})
to
\Gamma^{+}

and
P_{2}^{\rm in}
and
P_{4}^{\rm in}
are connected by paths of
\omega^{*}\cap(\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}^{c}\setminus\mathscr{D})
to
\Gamma^{-}
.
Due to Theorem
2.1
(see Figure
5
, right diagram),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[H\,|\,(\omega_{[\tau_{3}]},\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau_{3}]})\text{ s.t. }\tau_{3}<\infty]\geq c_{3}.

Special care should be taken as the primal connections occur in
\omega^{\prime}
while the dual ones occur in
\omega
.
This may be easily overcome by considering connections in pre-determined disjoint regions
3
3

3

One may be tempted to ask for both the primal and dual connections to occur in the same configuration, for instance in
\omega
.
This would be conceptually simpler,
but would require a stronger RSW result, as the sections of
\Gamma^{+}
outside of
\mathscr{D}
are wired in
\omega^{\prime}
, but not in
\omega
. This stronger RSW result is true for
q<4
(see
[
DMT20
]
), but it is expected to be wrong for
q=4
.
.

Now, if
\tau_{3}<\infty
and
H
occurs,
then
P_{1}^{\rm out}
and
P_{3}^{\rm out}
are disconnected in
\omega\cap\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}^{c}
,
but are connected in
\omega^{\prime}\cap\mathscr{F}^{c}_{\rm out}
.
Set
\tau=\tau_{4}
if the above occurs and
\tau=\infty
otherwise.
Then
\tau
satisfies the requirements of the proposition and

\mathbb{P}[\tau<\infty]\geq c_{1}c_{2}c_{3}>0.

\square

We conclude this section by the following lemma, which is straightforward application of Theorem
2.1
.
This lemma will be useful in the future sections.

Lemma 3.14

For any
\eta>0
, there exists a constant
c=c(\eta)>0
such that the following holds.
Fix
p
and
\mathscr{F}
an
\eta
-well-separated outer flower domain on
\Lambda_{R}
for some
R\leq L(p)
.
Let
\xi,\xi^{\prime}
be a boosting pair of boundary conditions on
\mathscr{F}
and
x
be a point of
{\rm Ann}(2R,4R)
.
Then, there exists a coupling via decision trees
\mathbb{P}
of
\phi_{\mathscr{F},p}^{\xi}
and
\phi_{\mathscr{F},p}^{\xi^{\prime}}

and a stopping time
\tau
such that, when
\tau<\infty
,

\mathscr{F}_{\tau}=\mathscr{F}\setminus e_{[\tau]}
is a
1/2
-well-separated inner flower domain on
\Lambda_{R/4}(x)
,
and the boundary conditions induced by
\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau]}
on
\mathscr{F}_{\tau}
are a boost of those induced by
\omega_{[\tau]}
.
Finally,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\tau<\infty]\geq c.

The proof is similar (yet much easier) to the one of Theorem
3.6
.

Proof

Write
P_{i}
,
P_{j}
for two petals of
\mathscr{F}
that are wired in
\xi^{\prime}
, but not in
\xi
.

Start by exploring the double four-petal flower domain
(\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out})
between
\Lambda_{R/4}(x)
and
\Lambda_{R/2}(x)
.
If no such double flower domain exists, set
\tau=\infty
and proceed in an arbitrary way.
If
(\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out})
exists, continue by revealing the configurations in
\mathscr{F}\cap\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}
.
Write
H
for the event that in
\omega\cap\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}
,

P^{\rm out}_{1}
is connected to
P_{i}
,
P^{\rm out}_{3}
is connected to
P_{j}
, but that
P^{\rm out}_{1}
and
P^{\rm out}_{3}

are not connected to each other, nor to any other petal of
\mathscr{F}
.
Theorem
2.1
implies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[H\,|\,\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}]\geq c,

(3.14)

where
c>0
depends only on
\eta
.
If
H
occurs, set
\tau
to be the stopping time at which the configurations in
\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}
have been revealed;
otherwise set
\tau=\infty
.

Then due to Lemma
3.4
and (
3.14
),
\mathbb{P}[H]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!c
.
Finally, when
\tau<\infty
,
the boundary conditions induced by
\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau]}
on
\mathscr{F}_{\tau}=\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}
are indeed a boost of those induced by
\omega_{[\tau]}
, since
P^{1}_{\rm in}
is connected to
P^{3}_{\rm in}
in
\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau]}
, but not in
\omega_{[\tau]}
.

\square

4
Properties of the mixing rate

Fix
q\in(1,4]
and
\eta>0
for the whole of this section;
all constants, including those in
\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!
and
\asymp
, may depend on
\eta
.
In this section, we always work with a single edge-parameter
p\in(0,1)
,
and therefore omit it often from the measure
\phi_{G,p}
for notational convenience.

4.1
No coupling induces boosting boundary conditions

The main result of this subsection is be the following;
it is the cornerstone to the other results proved later in this and other sections.

Theorem 4.1

For any
\eta>0
,
p
, and
4r<R\leq L(p)
.

(i)

Let
\mathscr{G}
be an
\eta
-well-separated inner flower domain on
\Lambda_{R}
and
\xi,\xi^{\prime}
be a boosting pair of boundary conditions on
\mathscr{G}
.
There exists an increasing coupling
\mathbb{P}
of
\phi_{\mathscr{G},p}^{\xi}
and
\phi_{\mathscr{G},p}^{\xi^{\prime}}
on
\mathscr{G}\setminus\Lambda_{r}
via decision trees,
and a stopping time
\tau
with the following property.
When
\tau<\infty
,
\mathscr{F}_{\tau}=\mathscr{G}\setminus e_{[\tau]}
is a
1/2
-well-separated inner flower domain on
\Lambda_{r}
,
and the boundary conditions induced by
(\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau]})^{\xi^{\prime}}
on
\mathscr{F}_{\tau}
are a boost of those induced by
\omega_{[\tau]}^{\xi}
.
Moreover, if we write
\zeta
and
\zeta^{\prime}
for the boundary conditions induced by
\omega^{\prime}
and
\omega
on
\partial\Lambda_{r}
, then

\mathbb{P}[\tau<\infty]\asymp\mathbb{P}[\zeta\neq\zeta^{\prime}].

(4.1)

(ii)

Let
\mathscr{G}
be an
\eta
-well-separated outer flower domain on
\Lambda_{r}
and
\xi,\xi^{\prime}
be a boosting pair of boundary conditions on
\mathscr{G}
.
There exists a increasing coupling
\mathbb{P}
of
\phi_{\mathscr{G},p}^{\xi}
and
\phi_{\mathscr{G},p}^{\xi^{\prime}}
on
\mathscr{G}\cap\Lambda_{R}
via decision trees,
and a stopping time
\tau
with the following property.
When
\tau<\infty
,
\mathscr{F}_{\tau}=\mathscr{G}\setminus e_{[\tau]}
is a
1/2
-well-separated outer flower domain on
\Lambda_{R}
,
and the boundary conditions induced by
(\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau]})^{\xi^{\prime}}
on
\mathscr{F}_{\tau}
are a boost of those induced by
\omega_{[\tau]}^{\xi}
.
Moreover, if we write
\zeta
and
\zeta^{\prime}
for the boundary conditions induced by
\omega^{\prime}
and
\omega
on
\partial\Lambda_{R}
, then

\mathbb{P}[\tau<\infty]\asymp\mathbb{P}[\zeta\neq\zeta^{\prime}].

(4.2)

Remark 4.2

In the previous theorem we sample edges only outside
\Lambda_{r}
in Case (i), and only inside
\Lambda_{R}
in Case (ii), but, of course, once this is done, one may use an arbitrary coupling inside
\Lambda_{r}
or outside
\Lambda_{R}
to obtain couplings in the whole flower domain
\mathscr{G}
. We stated the previous theorem in this setting to be able to reuse the coupling in applications we have in mind.

The first point of the theorem should be understood as follows.
We reveal the configurations
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}
in the coupling
\mathbb{P}
starting from the outside and moving inwards;
while doing this, we follow the difference between the boundary conditions that the configurations induce on the unexplored region.
If this difference survives until the whole of
\mathscr{G}\setminus\Lambda_{r}
is revealed,
then there is a positive probability that it survives as a significant difference, namely in the form of a boosting pair of boundary conditions on a well-separated flower domain.

The second point is analogous, with the revealment starting inside and moving outwards.

Proof of Theorem
4.1

We will only prove point (i); the proof of point (ii) is identical.
Fix
p\in(0,1)
,
4r\leq R\leq L(p)
and
\mathscr{G}
,
\xi
and
\xi^{\prime}
as in the statement.
All constants below are independent of
r

R
,
\mathscr{G}
,
\xi
and
\xi^{\prime}
, unless explicitly stated.
When referring to connections in configurations
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}

with laws
\phi_{\mathscr{G},p}^{\xi}
and
\phi_{\mathscr{G},p}^{\xi^{\prime}}
, respectively,
we will implicitly include connections that use the boundary conditions.
In other words, we omit the superscript in the notation
\omega^{\xi}
and
(\omega^{\prime})^{\xi^{\prime}}
.

First, for any increasing coupling
\mathbb{P}
between
\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi}
and
\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi^{\prime}}
obtained by a decision tree,
and any stopping time
\tau
with the properties of the theorem,

\mathbb{P}[\tau<\infty]\leq\mathbb{P}[\zeta\neq\zeta^{\prime}]
.
Indeed, the requirement that the boundary conditions induced by
\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau]}
on
\mathscr{F}_{\tau}
are a boost of those induced by
\omega_{[\tau]}
,
imposes that the boundary conditions induced by
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}
on
\Lambda_{r}
are distinct.
The rest of the proof is dedicated to the converse bound.

Assume for simplicity that
R=4^{k}r
for some integer
k\geq 2
.
By monotonicity, it suffices to treat the case where
\xi
and
\xi^{\prime}
are identical,
with the exception of two petals that are wired together in
\xi^{\prime}
, but not in
\xi
.
Assume this is the case, and write
P_{1}
and
P_{3}
for the two primal petals of
\mathscr{G}
that are wired together in
\xi^{\prime}
, but not in
\xi

(contrary to what the notation suggests,
P_{1}
and
P_{3}
need not be separated by a single dual petal).

Below, we describe an increasing coupling
\mathbf{P}
of

\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi}
and
\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi^{\prime}}
on
\mathscr{G}\cap\Lambda_{r}^{c}
obtained through a decision tree.
The actual coupling of the theorem is a slight variation of
\mathbf{P}
that we will describe at the end of the proof.

At any time
t
, write
\mathscr{F}_{t}
for the connected component of
\Lambda_{r}
in
\mathscr{G}\setminus e_{[t]}
.
We will abusively consider that all edges of
\mathscr{G}
that are not revealed at time
t

and which are not part of
\mathscr{F}_{t}
, are revealed instantaneously.
Thus, from now on, we have
\mathscr{F}_{t}=\mathscr{G}\setminus e_{[t]}
.
We will identify
\omega_{[t]}
and
\omega_{[t]}^{\prime}
to the boundary conditions they induce on
\mathscr{F}_{t}
.

Write
T
for the first time
t
when
\omega_{[t]}
and
\omega^{\prime}_{[t]}
induce the same boundary conditions on
\mathscr{F}_{t}
,
and
T=\infty
if the boundary conditions are never the same.
If
T<\infty
, the configurations
\omega,\omega^{\prime}
in
\mathscr{F}_{T}
are identical, regardless of the decision tree used after
T
.
Thus, when this occurs, reveal the rest of the edges using lexicographical order.

The coupling proceeds in several stages numbered
j=0,\dots,k
.
If at any point
T
occurs, the procedure described below stops, and the revealment by lexicographical order is used.
Stage
j
corresponds to revealing information in
\Lambda_{4^{k-j}r}^{c}:=\mathscr{G}\setminus\Lambda_{4^{k-j}r}
.
We will write
\tau_{j}
for the stopping time that marks the end of stage
j
.
At time
\tau_{j}
, the revealed edges are those of the cluster of
P_{1}
and
P_{3}
in
\omega^{\prime}\cap\Lambda_{4^{k-j}r}^{c}
,
any edges of
\Lambda_{4^{k-j}r}^{c}
adjacent to this cluster,
as well as any edges separated from
\Lambda_{r}
by the two categories of edges mentioned above.

Let us now describe precisely the revealment algorithm.

Revealment algorithm

Stage 0:

Reveal the cluster of
P_{1}
and
P_{3}
inside
\omega^{\prime}\cap\Lambda_{R}^{c}
in arbitrary order.
Let
\tau_{0}
be the stopping time marking the end of this stage.

Stage
j+1
:
Fix
j\geq 0
and assume the coupling defined up to time
\tau_{j}
(see Figure
6
for an illustration). Stage
j+1
is itself formed of two steps.
As already mentioned, this is only valid when
T>\tau_{j}
.
Write
\rho=4^{k-j-1}r
.
In
\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau_{j}]}
, the boundary of
\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{j}}
is formed of dual arcs outside of
\Lambda_{4\rho}

with endpoints on
\partial\Lambda_{4\rho}
,
along with points on
\Lambda_{4\rho}
that are connected in
\omega^{\prime}_{\tau_{j}}
to
P_{1}
or
P_{3}
.
Call the latter points the “wired” points of
\partial\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{j}}
.
Since
T
has not yet occurred, there exists at least one wired point on
\partial\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{j}}
.

Figure 6:
Red solid lines represent open edges in
\omega^{\prime}
, while blue ones represent open edges in
\omega
.
Dotted lines represent closed (or equivalently open dual edges) in the configurations corresponding to their color.

Left:
At the end of stage
j
, the revealed edges are those of the cluster of
P_{1}
and
P_{3}
in
\omega^{\prime}\cap\Lambda_{4\rho}^{c}

and its boundary.
Then, by time
\tau_{j+1/2}
, the interfaces in
\omega
starting at the wired vertices previously exposed are explored until they touch
\Lambda_{2\rho}
.
At this time, the unexplored region is a flower domain that is likely well-separated.

Middle:
Assuming that the primal petals of
\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{j+1/2}}
are all wired together in
\omega
,
we turn our attention to the set
\mathscr{A}
of points that lie on dual petals of
\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{j+1/2}}
, but which are connected to
P_{1}
or
P_{3}

in
\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau_{j+1/2}]}
. If this set has a positive probability to be connected to
\Lambda_{\rho}
, then we call
\tau_{j+1/2}
promising.

Right:
For
\tau_{j+1/2}
promising, we may connect two separate regions of
\mathscr{A}
to the two primal external petals of a double flower-domain at a smaller scale. Then these petals will be connected in
\omega^{\prime}
but not in
\omega
.

First, reveal all interfaces in
\omega\cap\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{j}}\cap\Lambda_{2\rho}^{c}
that start at wired points of
\partial\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{j}}
.
This may be done by tracking the left and right boundaries of the clusters of each wired point of
\partial\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{j}}

until they finish on
\partial\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{j}}
or they reach
\partial\Lambda_{2\rho}
.
Write
\tau_{j+1/2}
for the stopping time that marks the end of this step.
Formally, if
T
occurs before time
\tau_{j+1/2}
, set
\tau_{j+1/2}=\infty
.

Next, explore the cluster of
P_{1}
and
P_{3}
inside
\omega^{\prime}\cap\Lambda_{\rho}^{c}
and write
\tau_{j+1}

for the stopping time marking the end of this stage (set
\tau_{j+1}=\infty
if
T
occurs before the end of this stage). In fact, we will sometimes require that the clusters are explored in a certain order (see the coupling
{\rm P}
defined by Lemma
4.4
for details).

After stage
k
:
Assuming that
T>\tau_{k}
, reveal all remaining edges in lexicographical order.

Each stopping time
\tau_{j+1/2}

will be declared promising or not (see the precise definition below), depending on the configuration at that stage
and on some constant threshold
\delta>0
to be fixed below.
Fix
0\leq j<k
and assume
\tau_{j+1/2}<\infty
(otherwise
\tau_{j+1/2}
is not promising).
Then
\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{j+1/2}}
is either a simply connected domain containing
\Lambda_{2\rho}

and
\omega_{[\tau_{j+1/2}]}
induces free boundary conditions on it,
or it is an inner flower domain on
\Lambda_{2\rho}
and
\omega_{[\tau_{j+1/2}]}
induces coherent boundary conditions on it.
The former occurs when none of the revealed interfaces in the first step of stage
j
reaches
\Lambda_{2\rho}
.

First, we analyse the case where
\omega_{[\tau_{j+1/2}]}
induces free boundary conditions
\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{j+1/2}}
.
Write
\mathscr{A}
for the set of vertices on
\partial\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{j+1/2}}
that are connected to
P_{1}\cup P_{3}

in
\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau_{j+1/2}]}
.
Notice that, due to the exploration procedure,
for any edge
uv
with
u\in\partial\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{j+1/2}}
and
v\in\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{j+1/2}}^{c}
,

v
is connected in
\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau_{j+1/2}]}
to
P_{1}
or
P_{3}
.
Thus,
\mathscr{A}
is exactly the set of vertices
u\in\partial\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{j+1/2}}

for which there exists an explored edge
uv
with
v\in\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{j+1/2}}^{c}
that is open in
\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau_{j+1/2}]}
.
In particular, in the boundary conditions induced by
\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau_{j+1/2}]}
on
\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{j+1/2}}
,
all vertices of
\mathscr{A}
are wired together and all other boundary vertices are free
(that is they are not wired to any other boundary vertices).

In this case, we call
\tau_{j+1/2}

promising
if

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{j+1/2}}}^{\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau_{j+1/2}]}}[\mathscr{A}\leftrightarrow\Lambda_{\rho}]\geq\delta.

Next, we turn our attention to the case where
\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{j+1/2}}
is a flower domain.
Write
\mathscr{A}
for the set of points on the dual petals of
\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{j+1/2}}

that are connected to
P_{1}
or
P_{3}
in
\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau_{j+1/2}]}
.
By the same argument as in the previous case, in the boundary conditions imposed by
\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau_{j+1/2}]}
on
\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{j+1/2}}

there exists a single wired component formed of
\mathscr{A}
along with all primal petals of
\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{j+1/2}}
.

If
\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{j+1/2}}
is not
\delta
-well-separated, then we say that
\tau_{j+1/2}
is not promising.
If
\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{j+1/2}}
is
\delta
-well-separated
and contains at least two primal petals that are not wired in
\omega_{[\tau_{j+1/2}]}
, then we call
\tau_{j+1/2}
promising.
Finally, if
\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{j+1/2}}
is
\delta
-well-separated and all its primal petals are wired together in
\omega_{[\tau_{j+1/2}]}
,
we call
\tau_{j+1/2}
promising if and only if

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{j+1/2}}}^{\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau_{j+1/2}]}}[\mathscr{A}\leftrightarrow\Lambda_{\rho}]\geq\delta.

For formal reasons, set
\tau_{-1/2}=0
and call it promising.
We now state two results that are instrumental in the proof of the theorem.
Roughly speaking, the first one states that if
\tau_{j+1/2}
is not promising, then it is very likely for
T
to arise before
\tau_{j+1}
.
The second lemma states that as soon as
\tau_{j+1/2}
is promising, then there exists a coupling guaranteeing a fairly good probability that the flower domain on
\partial\Lambda_{r}
is
1/2
-well-separated and the boundary conditions in
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}
induced on this domain correspond to a boosting pair.

Lemma 4.3

For any
\varepsilon>0
, we may choose
\delta=\delta(\varepsilon)>0
(independent of
r
,
R
,
p
,
\mathscr{G}
,
\xi
and
\xi^{\prime}
) so that
for every
0\leq j<k
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}[\text{$\tau_{j+1/2}$ not promising and\penalty\ $T>\tau_{j+1}$}\,|\,\omega_{[\tau_{j}]},\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau_{j}]}]<\varepsilon.

Lemma 4.4

For any
\delta>0
, there exists
c(\delta)>0
with the following property.
Fix some
-1\leq j\leq k-1

and a realisation of
\tau_{j+1/2}
,
\omega_{[\tau_{j+1/2}]}
,
\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau_{j+1/2}]}
and
\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{j+1/2}}

for which
\tau_{j+1/2}
is promising.
Then there exists an increasing coupling

{\rm P}
of
\phi_{\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{j+1/2}}}^{\omega_{[\tau_{j+1/2}]}}
and
\phi_{\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{j+1/2}}}^{\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau_{j+1/2}]}}
via decision trees,
and a stopping time
\sigma
with the following property.
When
\sigma<\infty
,
\mathscr{F}_{\sigma}
is a
1/2
-well-separated inner flower domain on
\Lambda_{r}
,
and the boundary conditions induced by
\omega^{\prime}_{[\sigma]}
on
\mathscr{F}_{\sigma}
are a boost of those induced by
\omega_{[\sigma]}
.
Moreover,

\displaystyle{\rm P}[\sigma<\infty]\geq c(\delta)\pi_{4}(p;r,4^{k-j}r).

(4.3)

Remark 4.5

It is essential in the second lemmata that
c(\delta)
is allowed to depend on
\delta
, but that
it only appears as a multiplicative constant in (
4.3
).
Indeed, the upper bound in (
4.3
) depends on the ratio between the
scales of
\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{j+1/2}}
and
\mathscr{F}_{\sigma}
in a way that is uniform in
\delta
.

Before proving the two lemmas, let us conclude the proof of the theorem.
Fix
\varepsilon>0
so that
\pi_{4}(p;r,4^{j}r)\geq(2\varepsilon)^{j}
for all
j\geq 0
with
4^{j}r\leq L(p)
.
Due to (
RSW
),
\varepsilon
may be chosen independently of
r
or
p
.
Let
\delta=\delta(\varepsilon)
be the quantity given by Lemma
4.3
for this value of
\varepsilon
.
Below,
c_{0}
and
c_{1}
stand for strictly positive constants that may depend on
\varepsilon
,
but not on
r
,
R
or
\mathscr{G}
.

First observe that, due to Lemma
4.4
and Theorem
3.6
,
there exists
c_{0}>0
such that for any
1\leq j\leq k
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}[T=\infty]\geq\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi^{\prime}}[\mathscr{C}(\Lambda_{r})]-\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi}[\mathscr{C}(\Lambda_{r})]\geq c_{0}\,(2\varepsilon)^{j}\,\mathbf{P}[\text{$\tau_{k-j-1/2}$ promising}].

(4.4)

Indeed, the first inequality follows from the more general observation that
\mathbf{P}[T=\infty]

bounds the distance in total variation between the restrictions of
\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi^{\prime}}
and
\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi}
to
\Lambda_{r}
.
For the second inequality,
consider the increasing coupling of
\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi^{\prime}}
and
\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi}
obtained by following
\mathbf{P}

up to the stopping time
\tau_{k-j-1/2}
, then, if
\tau_{k-j-1/2}
is promising,
using the coupling
\rm P
of Lemma
4.4
and applying Theorem
3.6
.

Next, we claim that there exists some
\ell\geq 1
such that
if
T=\infty
, then with positive probability there exists one promising stopping time among the last
\ell
ones.
It is essential here that
\ell
is independent of
R/r
. Indeed, fix
\ell\geq 1
, and observe that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}[T=\infty\text{ and\penalty\ $\tau_{k-1/2},\dots,\tau_{k-\ell+1/2}$ not promising}]

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{j=\ell}^{k}\mathbf{P}[T=\infty;\text{ \penalty\ $\tau_{k-1/2},\dots,\tau_{k-j+1/2}$ not promising;\penalty\ $\tau_{k-j-1/2}$ promising}]

\displaystyle\quad\leq\sum_{j=\ell}^{k}\varepsilon^{j-1}\mathbf{P}[\text{$\tau_{k-j-1/2}$ promising}]

\displaystyle\quad\leq c_{0}\,\mathbf{P}[T=\infty]\,\sum_{j=\ell}^{k}2^{-j}\leq c_{0}\,2^{-\ell+1}\,\mathbf{P}[T=\infty].

(Note that this is where we use the convention that
\tau_{-1/2}
is promising.)
The first inequality is obtained by repeated applications of Lemma
4.3
;
the second is due to (
4.4
).
Thus, if we fix
\ell\geq-\log_{2}c_{0}+2
, then indeed,

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}[\text{at least one of\penalty\ $\tau_{k-1/2},\dots,\tau_{k-\ell+1/2}$ is promising}\,|\,T=\infty]\geq\tfrac{1}{2}.

(4.5)

We are now ready to define the coupling
\mathbb{P}
of the theorem.
Follow
\mathbf{P}
up to the first promising stopping time
\tau_{j+1/2}
with
j\geq k-\ell
.
Then follow the coupling
\rm P
of Lemma
4.4
; write
\tau_{\text{final}}
for the stopping time described in said lemma.
If
\mathbf{P}
does not encounter a promising stopping time
\tau_{j+1/2}
with
j\geq k-\ell
, set
\tau_{\text{final}}=\infty
.
Then, due to Lemma
4.4
and to (
4.5
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\tau_{\rm final}<\infty\,|\,T=\infty]\geq\tfrac{1}{2}c_{1}\,c_{2}^{\ell}.

Since
\varepsilon
and
\ell
are independent of
r
and
R
, the right hand side is bounded away from
0

uniformly in
r\leq R/4
. Moreover,
\tau_{\rm final}
satisfies the conditions stated in the theorem.
Multiply the above by
\mathbb{P}[T=\infty]=\mathbb{P}[\zeta\neq\zeta^{\prime}]
to obtain the desired inequality.

\square

Proof of Lemma
4.3

Fix
\varepsilon>0
and fix a realisation of
\tau_{j}
,
\omega_{[\tau_{j}]}
and
\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau_{j}]}
.
Lemma
3.2
ensures that, by choosing
\delta>0
small enough, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}[\text{$\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{j+1/2}}$ is a flower domain which is not\penalty\ $\delta$-well-separated}\,|\,\omega_{[\tau_{j}]},\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau_{j}]}]<\varepsilon/2.

Suppose now that either
\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{j+1/2}}
is a
\delta
-well-separated flower domain,
or that
\omega_{\tau_{j+1/2}}
induced free boundary conditions on it.
In either case, if
\tau_{j+1/2}
is not promising,
it is because
\mathscr{A}
has a conditionally small probability to be connected to
\Lambda_{\rho}
in
\omega^{\prime}
.

Continue the coupling
\mathbf{P}
by revealing first the connected component
{\sf C}
of
\mathscr{A}
in
\omega^{\prime}\cap\Lambda_{\rho}^{c}
,
then the rest of the connected component of
P_{1}
and
P_{3}
.
If
{\sf C}
does not reach
\Lambda_{\rho}
, then it is entirely surrounded by closed edges of
\omega^{\prime}
.
Thus, the boundary conditions in
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}
on the complement of
{\sf C}
are identical,
which is to say that
T
occurs before
\tau_{j+1}
.
Thus

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}[T>\tau_{j+1}\,|\,\omega_{[\tau_{j+1/2}]},\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau_{j+1/2}]}]\leq\phi_{\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{j+1/2}}}^{\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau_{j+1/2}]}}[\mathscr{A}\leftrightarrow\Lambda_{\rho}]<\delta.

By choosing
\delta>0
small enough, both displays may be rendered smaller than
\varepsilon/2
.
Apply the union bound to obtain the desired inequality.

\square

Proof of Lemma
4.4

Fix
\delta>0
and a realisation
\mathscr{F}
of
\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{j+1/2}}
, with boundary conditions
\zeta
and
\zeta^{\prime}

induced by
\omega_{[\tau_{j+1/2}]}
and
\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau_{j+1/2}]}
, respectively, on
\mathscr{F}
.
Assume that
\mathscr{F}
,
\zeta
and
\zeta^{\prime}
satisfy the assumptions of the lemma.
There are three situations that need to be analysed; we will proceed in increasing order of difficulty. Set
\rho:=4^{k-j}r
.

Case 1:
\mathscr{F}
is a flower domain with two petals wired in
\zeta^{\prime}
but not in
\zeta
.

First assume that
\mathscr{F}
is a
\delta
-well-separated flower domain
containing two primal petals
P_{i}
and
P_{j}
that are not wired together in
\zeta
.
Recall that
P_{i}
and
P_{j}
are necessarily wired together in
\zeta^{\prime}
.

Then
\rm P
is constructed as follows.
Attempt to explore the double four-petal flower domain
(\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out})
between
\Lambda_{r}
and
\Lambda_{2r}

for the configuration
\omega
.
If no such double four-petal flower domain exists set
\sigma=\infty
and reveal the remaining edges in lexicographical order.
If it exists, reveal the configurations in
\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}\cap\mathscr{F}
and let
\sigma
be the stopping time marking the end of this stage.

Let
H
be the event that the double four-petal flower domain
(\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out})
exists,
that the two primal petals
P_{1}^{\rm out}
and
P_{3}^{\rm out}

of
\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}
are connected in
\omega^{\prime}_{[\sigma]}
to
P_{i}
and
P_{j}
, respectively,
while
P_{i}
and
P_{j}
are not connected to each other
nor to any primal petal of
\mathscr{F}
in
\omega_{[\sigma]}
.
It is a standard consequence of the separation of arms that there exists a constant
c(\delta)>0
such that

\displaystyle{\rm P}[H\,|\,(\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out})\text{ double four-petal flower domain}]\geq c(\delta)\,\pi_{4}(p;r,4\rho).

Lemma
3.4
ensures that the event in the conditioning has uniformly positive probability,
and hence so does
H
.
Finally, notice that when
H
occurs, the boundary conditions induced by
\omega^{\prime}_{[\sigma]}
on
\partial\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}
are a boost of those induced by
\omega_{[\sigma]}
. This concludes the proof in this case.

Case 2:
\mathscr{F}
is not a flower domain.

Next, assume that
\mathscr{F}
is not a flower domain, and therefore that
\zeta=0
.
Start the coupling by attempting to reveal
the double four-petal flower domains
(\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out})
between
\Lambda_{r}
and
\Lambda_{2r}

and
(\overline{\mathscr{F}}_{\rm in},\overline{\mathscr{F}}_{\rm out})
between
\Lambda_{\rho}
and
\Lambda_{3\rho/2}
, respectively, for the configuration
\omega
(if
\rho=r
, only perform the latter exploration).
If these two double four-petal flower domains exist, proceed by revealing the configurations in
\overline{\mathscr{F}}_{\rm in}\cap{\mathscr{F}}_{\rm out}
; call
\sigma_{0}
the stopping time marking the end of this stage.

Let
H
be the event that the two double four-petal flower domains above exist,
that
\overline{P}_{1}^{\rm in}
is connected to
{P}_{1}^{\rm out}

and
\overline{P}_{3}^{\rm in}
to
{P}_{3}^{\rm out}
in
\omega\cap\overline{\mathscr{F}}_{\rm in}\cap{\mathscr{F}}_{\rm out}

and that
\overline{P}_{2}^{\rm in}
is connected to
{P}_{2}^{\rm out}

and
\overline{P}_{4}^{\rm in}
to
{P}_{4}^{\rm out}
in
\omega^{*}\cap\overline{\mathscr{F}}_{\rm in}\cap{\mathscr{F}}_{\rm out}
.
As in the first case, it is a consequence of the separation of arms and Lemma
3.4

that there exists
c_{0}>0
such that

\displaystyle{\rm P}[H]\geq c_{0}\,\pi_{4}(p;r,4\rho).

(4.6)

Next we reveal the configurations in
\mathscr{F}\cap\overline{\mathscr{F}}_{\rm out}
in a fashion described below.
Write
H^{\prime}
for the event that
\overline{P}_{1}^{\rm out}
and
\overline{P}_{3}^{\rm out}
are connected
to each other in
\omega^{\prime}\cap\overline{\mathscr{F}}_{\rm out}
, but not in
\omega\cap\overline{\mathscr{F}}_{\rm out}
.
Observe that if
H
and
H^{\prime}
occur, then the boundary conditions induced on
\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}

by
\omega\cap\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}
and
\omega^{\prime}\cap\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}
, respectively, form a boosting pair.
At this stage, it will be useful to introduce the following claim.

Claim 4.6

There exists
c_{1}=c_{1}(\delta)>0
and
four disjoint domains
D_{1},\dots,D_{4}
contained in
\mathscr{F}\cap\overline{\mathscr{F}}_{\rm out}
(they depend on
\mathscr{A}
) such that

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathscr{D}_{1}}^{\omega^{\prime}_{[\sigma_{0}]},0}[\mathscr{A}\longleftrightarrow\overline{P}_{1}^{\rm out}]>c_{1},\qquad

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathscr{D}_{3}}^{\omega^{\prime}_{[\sigma_{0}]},0}[\mathscr{A}\longleftrightarrow\overline{P}_{3}^{\rm out}]>c_{1},

(4.7)

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathscr{D}_{2}}^{\omega_{[\sigma_{0}]},1}[\partial\mathscr{F}\xleftrightarrow{\omega^{*}}\overline{P}_{2}^{\rm out}]>c_{1},\qquad

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathscr{D}_{4}}^{\omega_{[\sigma_{0}]},1}[\partial\mathscr{F}\xleftrightarrow{\omega^{*}}\overline{P}_{4}^{\rm out}]>c_{1},

(4.8)

where
\omega^{\prime}_{[\sigma_{0}]},0
(and
\omega_{[\sigma_{0}]},1
) are the boundary conditions on
\partial D_{i}
induced
by the configuration equal to
\omega^{\prime}
(and
\omega
, respectively), completed in the unexplored region of
D_{i}^{c}
by closed (respectively, open) edges.

Essentially
D_{1},\dots,D_{4}
should be viewed as disjoint tubes connecting the petals
\overline{P}_{i}^{\rm out}

to four disjoint regions of
\partial\mathscr{F}
. Each tube should be thought of as thick enough to contain either primal or dual paths with positive probability (for instance we may think of each tube as being constructed using a finite number of rectangles of aspect ratio
2
).
Moreover, we should further require that the regions of
\partial\mathscr{F}
contained in
D_{1}
and
D_{3}
contain sufficiently many points of
\mathscr{A}
that a connection reaches these points with positive probability.
Let us delay the proof of this technical result and finish the proof of the lemma in this case.

Fix the four domains
D_{1},\dots,D_{4}
given by the claim.
Reveal the configuration in
\mathscr{F}\cap\overline{\mathscr{F}}_{\rm out}
by first revealing the configurations outside
D_{1},\dots,D_{4}
,
then in each of the quads
D_{1},\dots D_{4}
.
Due to (
CBC
),

\displaystyle{\rm P}\big[\mathscr{A}\xleftrightarrow{\omega^{\prime}\cap D_{1}}\overline{P}_{1}^{\rm out},\,\mathscr{A}\xleftrightarrow{\omega^{\prime}\cap D_{3}}\overline{P}_{3}^{\rm out},\,\partial\mathscr{F}\xleftrightarrow{\omega^{*}\cap D_{2}}\overline{P}_{2}^{\rm out},\,\partial\mathscr{F}\xleftrightarrow{\omega^{*}\cap D_{4}}\overline{P}_{4}^{\rm out}\,\big|\,(\omega_{[\sigma_{0}]},\omega_{[\sigma_{0}]}^{\prime})\big]\geq c_{1}^{4},

whenever
(\omega_{[\sigma_{0}]},\omega_{[\sigma_{0}]}^{\prime})
are such that
H
occur.
Notice now that, since all points of
\mathscr{A}
are wired together in
\omega^{\prime}
outside of
\mathscr{F}
,
but that all the boundary of
\mathscr{F}
is free in
\omega
,
if the event above occurs, then so does
H^{\prime}
.
Thus,

\displaystyle{\rm P}[H^{\prime}\cap H]\geq c_{1}^{4}{\rm P}[H]\geq c_{0}\,\pi_{4}(p;r,4\rho).

If we now set
\sigma
to be the stopping time at which all of
\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}^{c}
has been explored and
H^{\prime}
has been found to occur,
and
\sigma=\infty
otherwise, then
\sigma
satisfies the properties claimed in the lemma.

Case 3:
\mathscr{F}
is a flower domain but the petals wired in
\zeta^{\prime}
are wired in
\zeta
.

The construction in this is very similar to that of Case 2.
Start off by revealing
the double four-petal flower domains
(\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out})
between
\Lambda_{r}
and
\Lambda_{2r}

and
(\overline{\mathscr{F}}_{\rm in},\overline{\mathscr{F}}_{\rm out})
between
\Lambda_{\rho}
and
\Lambda_{3\rho/2}
, respectively, for the configuration
\omega
.
Define
H
and
H^{\prime}
in the same way as in Case 2.
The only difference is in the way in which the configurations in
\mathscr{F}\cap\overline{\mathscr{F}}_{\rm out}
are revealed so that

{\rm P}[H^{\prime}\,|\,H]
is bounded below by a constant depending only on
\delta
.

Write
P_{1},\dots,P_{2k}
for the petals of
\mathscr{F}
.
By the union bound and the assumption on
\mathscr{A}
, there exists a free petal
P_{j}
such that

\displaystyle\phi^{\zeta^{\prime}}_{\mathscr{F}}[P_{j}\cap\mathscr{A}\xleftrightarrow{\omega^{\prime}}\Lambda_{\rho}]\geq\delta/k.

Recall that
\mathscr{F}
is
\delta
-well-separated, and therefore
k
is bounded in terms of
\delta
only.
A construction similar to that of the claim may be performed,
with
\mathscr{A}
replaced by
\mathscr{A}\cap P_{j}
and
\partial\mathscr{F}
replaced by
P_{j}
.
We conclude in the same way as in Case 2.

\square

We now turn to the proof of Claim
4.6
.
This type of construction is usually tedious because of the general form of
\mathscr{F}
, but may be performed fairly explicitly.
We warn the reader of the difficulties arising from the potential bottlenecks of
\mathscr{F}
and the fact that the RSW result (
RSW
) is not valid with arbitrary boundary conditions in arbitrary quads when
q=4
.
Nevertheless, with sufficient care, the domains
D_{1},\dots,D_{4}
may be constructed as unions of small squares
\Lambda_{c\rho n}(x)
with
x\in c\rho\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and
c>0
a small constant independent of
\rho
.
We propose below an alternative, more innovative construction of
D_{1},\dots,D_{4}
.
We will use the following result from
[
DMT20
]
(see Remark 4.2 to be precise).

The extremal distance between the arcs
(ab)
and
(cd)
of a quad
(\mathscr{D},a,b,c,d)
is the unique value

\ell=\ell_{\mathscr{D}}[(ab),(cd)]
such that there exists a conformal transformation
\psi
from
\mathscr{D}
(seen as a domain in the continuum)
to
(0,1)\times(0,\ell)
, with
a,b,c,d
being mapped (by the continuous extension of
\psi
) to the corners of
[0,1]\times[0,\ell]
, in counterclockwise order, starting with the lower-left corner.

Proposition 4.7
(RSW in terms of extremal distance)

For all
L>0
, there exists
\eta(L)>0
such that, for all
1\leq q\leq 4
,
p\in(0,1)
,
R<L(p)
,
and
(\mathscr{D},a,b,c,d)
a discrete quad contained in
\Lambda_{R}
,
if
\ell_{\mathscr{D}}[(ab),(cd)]\leq L
then

\phi_{\mathscr{D},p}^{1/0}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]\geq\eta(L),

where
1/0
denotes the boundary condition on
\mathscr{D}
where the arcs
(ab)
and
(cd)
are wired and the rest of the boundary is free.

While the result is stated for
p=p_{c}
only in
[
DMT20
]
, the reader will easily check that its proof extends readily to the near-critical regime using (
RSW’
).

Proof of Claim
4.6

Write
B_{s}
for the euclidian ball of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
of radius
s
centred at
0
, and
B_{s}(z)
for its translate by
z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Let
\psi
be a conformal map from
\mathscr{F}\cap\overline{\mathscr{F}}_{\rm out}
to some
B_{1}\setminus B_{s}
.
The existence of such a map is given by the uniformization theorem for the topological annulus
\mathscr{F}\cap\overline{\mathscr{F}}_{\rm out}
.
Note that
s
is determined by
\mathscr{F}\cap\overline{\mathscr{F}}_{\rm out}
.
Moreover, since
\partial\mathscr{F}
intersects
\Lambda_{2\rho}
and

\partial\overline{\mathscr{F}}
is contained in
{\rm Ann}(\rho,\frac{3}{2}\rho)
,
s
is bounded uniformly away from
0
and
1
,
and the endpoints of
\psi(P_{1}),\dots,\psi(P_{4})
are uniformly far from each other.

Fix some small positive constant
c_{0}=c_{0}(\delta)<(1-s)/16
which will be chosen below and will depend only on
\delta
.
Let
a_{0},\dots,a_{K}=a_{0}
(with
K=2\pi/c_{0}
) be points on the circle
\partial B_{1}
indexed in counterclockwise order and at a distance
c_{0}
of each other.
Write
(a_{i}a_{i+1})
for the arc of
\partial B_{1}
contained between
a_{i}
and
a_{i+1}
;
identify
\psi^{-1}(a_{i}a_{i+1})
to the corresponding vertices of
\partial\mathscr{F}
. Let
\mathscr{A}_{i}:=\mathscr{A}\cap\psi^{-1}(a_{i}a_{i+1})
.

Next, we argue that there exist two indices
i,j
with
|i-j|\geq 8
(modulo
K
) such that

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathscr{F}}^{\zeta^{\prime}}[\mathscr{A}_{i}\longleftrightarrow\psi^{-1}(B_{2c_{0}}(a_{i}))^{c}]

\displaystyle\geq c_{0}\delta/2,

(4.9)

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathscr{F}}^{\zeta^{\prime}}[\mathscr{A}_{j}\longleftrightarrow\psi^{-1}(B_{2c_{0}}(a_{j}))^{c}]

\displaystyle\geq c_{0}\delta/2.

Indeed, if the above fails, then there necessarily exists
i
such that for all
j\notin\{i,\dots,i+7\}
,

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathscr{F}}^{\zeta^{\prime}}[\mathscr{A}_{j}\longleftrightarrow\psi^{-1}(B_{2c_{0}}(a_{j}))^{c}]<c_{0}\delta/2.

(4.10)

Assuming that this is the case, explore the open cluster
\mathbf{C}
of
\mathscr{A}\setminus\psi^{-1}(a_{i}a_{i+8})
.
Due to the small value of
c_{0}
and to the assumption above,
we conclude by the union bound that

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathscr{F}}^{\zeta^{\prime}}[\mathbf{C}\text{ intersects }\Lambda_{\rho}]<\delta/2.

(4.11)

If
\mathbf{C}
does not intersects
\Lambda_{\rho}
, the measure on
\mathscr{F}\setminus\mathbf{C}
obtained by conditioning on
\mathbf{C}
has free boundary conditions for all vertices adjacent to
\mathbf{C}
.
Then, due to Proposition
4.7
applied repeatedly to the dual model, we conclude that for
c_{0}
small enough,

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathscr{F}}^{\zeta^{\prime}}[\mathscr{A}\longleftrightarrow\Lambda_{\rho}\,|\,\mathbf{C}\text{ does no intersect }\Lambda_{\rho}]<\delta/2.

(4.12)

Combining (
4.11
) and (
4.12
) we conclude that
\phi_{\mathscr{F}}^{\zeta^{\prime}}[\mathscr{A}\leftrightarrow\Lambda_{\rho}]<\delta
,
which contradicts the assumption that the stopping time at which
\mathscr{F}
was discovered was promising.
Thus (
4.9
) is proved.

Fix
i,j
with
|i-j|\geq 8
(modulo
K
) and satisfying (
4.9
). For what comes next, we refer to Figure
7
.
For
k=1,2,3,4
, set

(D_{k},a_{k},b_{k},c_{k},d_{k}):=\psi^{-1}[(D^{\prime}_{k},a^{\prime}_{k},b^{\prime}_{k},c^{\prime}_{k},d^{\prime}_{k})],

where the quads
(D_{k}^{\prime},a_{k}^{\prime},b_{k}^{\prime},c_{k}^{\prime},d_{k}^{\prime})
satisfy:

•

D^{\prime}_{k}\subset B_{1}\setminus B_{s}
for every
k
;

•

the extremal distances
\ell_{\mathscr{D}_{k}}[(a_{k}b_{k}),(c_{k}d_{k})]
belong to
(\kappa,1/\kappa)
with
\kappa=\kappa(c_{0})\in(0,\infty)
for every
k
;

•

(a^{\prime}_{k}b^{\prime}_{k})
is equal to
(a_{i-3}a_{i+3})
,
(a_{i+3}a_{j-3})
,
(a_{j-3}a_{j+3})
and
(a_{j+3}a_{i-3})
for
k=1,2,3
and
4
, respectively;

•

(c^{\prime}_{k}d^{\prime}_{k})=\psi(\overline{P}_{k}^{\rm out}
) for every
k
;

•

D^{\prime}_{1}
and
D^{\prime}_{3}
contain
B_{3c_{0}}(a_{i})
and
B_{3c_{0}}(a_{j})
respectively.

The construction of the domains
(D^{\prime}_{k},a^{\prime}_{k},b^{\prime}_{k},c^{\prime}_{k},d^{\prime}_{k})
is straightforward.

We now check (
4.7
) and (
4.8
). We start by the latter and focus on the first inequality. The boundary conditions induced by
\{\omega_{[\sigma_{0}]},1\}
on
D_{2}
are free on
\overline{P}_{2}^{\rm out}
and on
\psi^{-1}((a_{i+3}a_{j-3}))
. Therefore, the result follows directly from Proposition
4.7
and duality.
We now turn to (
4.7
) and focus on the first inequality. Let

\displaystyle\ell

\displaystyle:=\partial\psi^{-1}(B_{2c_{0}}(a_{i}))^{c}\setminus\partial(\mathscr{F}\cap\overline{\mathscr{F}}^{\rm out}).

First, a mixing-type argument using Proposition
4.7
and (
4.9
) combine to give

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathscr{D}_{1}}^{\omega^{\prime}_{[\sigma_{0}]},0}[\mathscr{A}_{i}\longleftrightarrow\ell]\geq c_{2}\phi_{\mathscr{F}}^{\zeta^{\prime}}[\mathscr{A}_{i}\longleftrightarrow\ell]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:two_arcs}}}{{\geq}}c_{3}(\delta).

Second, if
\ell^{\pm}:=\ell\cap\psi^{-1}(H^{\pm})
, where
H^{+}
is the half-plane on the left of the line going from 0 to
\tfrac{1}{2}(a_{i}+a_{i+1})
(where left is understood when looking in the direction
\tfrac{1}{2}(a_{i}+a_{i+1})
) and
H^{-}=\mathbb{C}\setminus H^{+}
.
The union bound implies that
\#
may be chosen equal to
+
or
-
so that

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathscr{D}_{1}}^{\omega^{\prime}_{[\sigma_{0}]},0}[\mathscr{A}_{i}\longleftrightarrow\ell^{\#}]\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\phi_{\mathscr{D}_{1}}^{\omega^{\prime}_{[\sigma_{0}]},0}[\mathscr{A}_{i}\longleftrightarrow\ell].

We assume below that
\#=+
(the same can be done for
\#=-
). Condition on the left-most path
\Gamma
going from
\mathscr{A}_{i}
to
\ell^{+}
. Then, conditioned on
\Gamma
, the domain carved from
D_{1}
by removing
\Gamma
and all the edges explored to determine
\Gamma
has wired boundary conditions on
\overline{P}_{1}^{\rm out}
, wired on
\Gamma
, and boundary conditions dominating the free boundary conditions elsewhere. Since the extremal distance between
\overline{P}_{1}^{\rm out}
and
\Gamma
in this new domain is larger than
\kappa^{\prime}=\kappa^{\prime}(\kappa,\delta)>0
, we deduce from Proposition
4.7
that

\phi_{\mathscr{D}_{1}}^{\omega^{\prime}_{[\sigma_{0}]},0}[\mathscr{A}_{i}\longleftrightarrow\overline{P}_{1}^{\rm out}|\mathscr{A}_{i}\longleftrightarrow\ell^{+}]\geq c_{4}.

Combining the last three displayed equations gives the first inequality of (
4.7
), and therefore concludes the proof.

\square

Figure 7:
The uniformization map
\Psi
from
\mathscr{F}\cap\overline{\mathscr{F}}^{\rm out}
into
B_{1}\setminus B_{s}
. On the right, the black dots denote the
a_{i}
. We depicted the balls
B_{2c_{0}}(a_{i})
and
B_{3c_{0}}(a_{i})
. The four domains
D_{1}^{\prime},\dots,D_{4}^{\prime}
can be chosen in many ways. In bigger red, the path
\ell^{+}
and its preimage
\psi^{-1}(\ell^{+})
. The path
\Gamma
from
\mathscr{A}
into
\psi^{-1}(\ell^{+})
is drawn in dark blue.

4.2
Mixing rate versus coupling

In this section we estimate the probability under the coupling of Theorem
4.1
that
\tau
occurs.
The relevant result of this section is the following theorem.

Theorem 4.8

For any
p\in(0,1)
and any
4r<R\leq L(p)
, the following holds.
Fix
\mathscr{G}
an
\eta
-well-separated inner flower domain on
\Lambda_{R}
or outer flower domain on
\Lambda_{r}

and
\xi
and
\xi^{\prime}
a boosting pair of boundary conditions on
\mathscr{G}
.
Let
\mathbb{P}
be the coupling of Theorem
4.1

(points (i) or (ii), depending on whether
\mathscr{G}
is an inner or outer flower domain),
and recall the stopping time
\tau
associated to
\mathbb{P}
.
Then

\mathbb{P}[\tau<\infty]\asymp\Delta_{p}(r,R).

(4.13)

As a consequence,
if
\mathscr{G}
is an inner flower domain and
H
is either the crossing event of an
\eta
-regular quad at scale
r
or
H=A_{r/2}
,
or if
\mathscr{G}
is an outer flower domain and
H
is either the crossing event of an
\eta
-regular quad at scale
R

translated so that it is contained in
{\rm Ann}(R,2R)
or
H=A_{R}
, then

\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi^{\prime}}[H]-\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi}[H]\asymp\Delta_{p}(r,R).

(4.14)

When
\mathscr{G}
is an inner flower domain, (
4.14
) should be understood as follows.
Any boosting pair of boundary conditions at scale
R
boosts the probability of any crossing event at scale
r

by a quantity comparable to
\Delta_{p}(r,R)
.
The same holds when the boundary conditions are at scale
r
and the crossing event is at scale
R
.
Recall that
\Delta_{p}(r,R)
was defined in terms of the boost that a specific pair of boundary conditions at scale
R

has on a specific crossing event at scale
r
.
Thus, in addition to stating that the boost of any pair of boundary conditions on any crossing event is comparable,
the proposition also links the boost from outside in to that from inside out.

The rest of this section is dedicated to showing Theorem
4.8
.
The proof is split into several steps, each corresponding to a lemma.
First, as a consequence of Theorem
4.1
(i),
we prove that the influence of boundary conditions on the crossing of any two regular quads is comparable.

Lemma 4.9

Fix
p\in(0,1)
and
2r<R\leq L(p)
.
Let
\mathscr{G}
be an
\eta
-well-separated inner flower domain on
\Lambda_{R}

and
\xi
and
\xi^{\prime}
be a boosting pair of boundary conditions on
\mathscr{G}
.
Moreover, let
H
be either the crossing event of an
\eta
-regular quad at scale
r
or
H=A_{r/2}
.
Then

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi^{\prime}}[H]-\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi}[H]\asymp\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi^{\prime}}[\mathscr{C}(\Lambda_{r})]-\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi}[\mathscr{C}(\Lambda_{r})].

(4.15)

In particular,

\displaystyle\phi_{\Lambda_{R}}^{1}[H]-\phi_{\Lambda_{R}}^{0}[H]\asymp\Delta_{p}(r,R).

(4.16)

A particular case of (
4.16
) shows that
\Delta_{p}(1,R)\asymp\Delta_{p}(R)
.
In addition, by taking
H=\mathscr{C}(\Lambda_{r/2})
, (
4.16
) also proves that

\displaystyle\Delta_{p}(r,R)\asymp\Delta_{p}(r/2,R),

(4.17)

and more generally that replacing
r
by a multiple of
r
only affects
\Delta_{p}
by a multiplicative constant.

As will be apparent from the proof,
an equivalent of (
4.15
) may also be proved for outer flower domains on
\Lambda_{r}

and crossing events at scale
R
. However, an equivalent of (
4.16
) cannot be shown with the same proof, as least for now (but will be later).

Proof

Fix
p
,
r
,
R
,
\mathscr{G}
,
\xi
,
\xi^{\prime}
and
H
as in the statement.
Let
\mathbb{P}
be the coupling of Theorem
4.1
(i) between
\phi_{\Lambda_{R}}^{\xi^{\prime}}
and
\phi_{\Lambda_{R}}^{\xi}
.
Then, using the notation of the theorem,

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi^{\prime}}[H]-\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi}[H]=\mathbb{P}[\omega^{\prime}\in H,\omega\notin H]\leq\mathbb{P}[\zeta\neq\zeta^{\prime}]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\mathbb{P}[\tau<\infty].

Moreover, by Theorem
3.6
,
