\displaystyle\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi^{\prime}}[H]-\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi}[H]\geq\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau<\infty\}}\big(\phi_{\mathscr{F}_{\tau}}^{\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau]}}[H]-\phi_{\mathscr{F}_{\tau}}^{\omega_{[\tau]}}[H]\big)\big]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\mathbb{P}[\tau<\infty],

where
\mathbb{E}
stands for the expectation associated to
\mathbb{P}
.
The two displays above imply that

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi^{\prime}}[H]-\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi}[H]\asymp\mathbb{P}[\tau<\infty].

(4.18)

Apply this to a generic
H
and to
H=\mathscr{C}(\Lambda_{r})
to obtain (
4.15
).
Finally, (
4.15
) applied with
\mathscr{G}=\Lambda_{R}
,
\xi=0
,
\xi^{\prime}=1
gives (
4.16
).

\square

Next, we deduce an interpretation of
\Delta_{p}(r,R)
as a covariance between events at scales
r
and
R
.
Considering the symmetry between
r
and
R
below, this result is used to link the influence from outside in to that from inside out.

Lemma 4.10

For every
p
and every
4r\leq R\leq L(p)
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}[A_{r/2},A_{R}]\asymp\phi_{\Lambda_{r}^{c}}^{1}[A_{R}]-\phi_{\Lambda_{r}^{c}}^{0}[A_{R}]\asymp\Delta_{p}(r,R).

(4.19)

Proof

We start by proving the equivalence between
\mathrm{Cov}(A_{r/2},A_{R})
and
\Delta(r,R)
.
By the monotonicity of boundary conditions (
CBC
),

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}(A_{r/2},A_{R})=\phi[A_{R}](\phi[A_{r/2}\,|\,A_{R}]-\phi[A_{r/2}])\leq\phi_{\Lambda_{R}}^{1}[A_{r/2}]-\phi_{\Lambda_{R}}^{0}[A_{r/2}]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\Delta(r,R),

where the last inequality is due to Lemma
4.9
.

For the converse bound, some additional work is needed.
Let
\mathbb{P}
denote the coupling between
\phi
and
\phi[\cdot\,|A_{r/2}]
inside
\Lambda_{R/2}
produced by applying Remark
3.10
then Theorem
4.1
(ii).
Recall that
\zeta
and
\zeta^{\prime}
denote the boosting pair of boundary conditions on
\Lambda_{R/2}^{c}

induced by
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}
, respectively.
Complete the coupling outside of
\Lambda_{R/2}

by an arbitrary increasing coupling.
Then, Theorems
3.6
and
4.1
(ii) show that

\displaystyle{\rm Cov}(A_{r/2},A_{R})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}[\omega^{\prime}\in A_{R},\,\omega\notin A_{R}]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau<\infty\}}\big(\phi_{\mathscr{F}_{\tau}}^{\omega_{[\tau]}^{\prime}}[A_{R}]-\phi_{\mathscr{F}_{\tau}}^{\omega_{[\tau]}}[A_{R}]\big)\big]

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\mathbb{P}[\tau<\infty]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\mathbb{P}[\zeta^{\prime}\neq\zeta].

Using that
\phi[A_{r/2}]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!1
, we deduce from the display above that

\mathrm{Cov}(A_{r/2},A_{R})\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\mathbb{P}[\zeta^{\prime}\neq\zeta]
. Moreover

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\zeta^{\prime}\neq\zeta]

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\mathbb{P}[\omega^{\prime}\in A_{R/2},\,\omega\notin A_{R/2}]\geq\mathrm{Cov}(A_{r/2},A_{R/2})\quad\text{ and}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\zeta^{\prime}\neq\zeta]

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\mathbb{P}[\omega^{\prime}\notin A_{R/2},\,\omega\in A_{R/2}^{*}]\geq\mathrm{Cov}(A_{r/2},A_{R/2}^{*}),

where
A_{R/2}^{*}=\{\Lambda_{R/2}\not\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{R}\}
is the event
A_{R/2}
applied to the dual model.
Divide the equations above by
\phi[A_{R/2}]
and
\phi[A_{R/2}^{*}]
, respectively, both of which are uniformly positive quantities.
Using the monotonicity of boundary conditions (
CBC
), we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\zeta^{\prime}\neq\zeta]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\phi[A_{r/2}\,|\,A_{R/2}]-\phi[A_{r/2}\,|\,A_{R/2}^{*}]\geq\phi_{\Lambda_{R}}^{1}[A_{r/2}]-\phi_{\Lambda_{R}}^{0}[A_{r/2}]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\Delta(r,R).

Together with (
4.2
) and (
4.2
) this shows that

\displaystyle\Delta(r,R)\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\mathbb{P}[\zeta^{\prime}\neq\zeta]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\mathrm{Cov}(A_{r/2},A_{R})\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\Delta(r,R).

Finally, we turn to the equivalence between
\mathrm{Cov}(A_{r/2},A_{R})
and
\phi_{\Lambda_{r}^{c}}^{1}[A_{R}]-\phi_{\Lambda_{r}^{c}}^{0}[A_{R}]
.
The monotonicity of boundary conditions (
CBC
) shows that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}(A_{r/2},A_{R})\leq\phi[A_{R}\,|\,A_{r/2}]-\phi[A_{R}]\leq\phi_{\Lambda_{r}^{c}}^{1}[A_{R}]-\phi_{\Lambda_{r}^{c}}^{0}[A_{R}].

Conversely,

\displaystyle\phi_{\Lambda_{r}^{c}}^{1}[A_{R}]-\phi_{\Lambda_{r}^{c}}^{0}[A_{R}]

\displaystyle\leq\phi[A_{R}\,|\,A_{r}]-\phi[A_{R}\,|\,A_{r}^{*}]

by (
CBC
)

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\mathrm{Cov}(A_{R},A_{r})-\mathrm{Cov}(A_{R},A_{r}^{*})

as
\phi[A_{r}]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!1
&
\phi[A_{r}^{*}]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!1

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!(\phi[A_{r}\,|\,A_{R}]-\phi[A_{r}])+(\phi[A_{r}^{*}]-\phi[A_{r}^{*}\,|\,A_{R}])

since
\phi[A_{R}]\leq 1

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\Delta_{p}(2r,R)

by (
CBC
) and (
4.14
)

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\Delta_{p}(r,R)

by (
4.17
)

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\mathrm{Cov}[A_{r/2},A_{R}]

by (
4.2
).

The last two displays prove that
\mathrm{Cov}[A_{r/2},A_{R}]\asymp\phi_{\Lambda_{r}^{c}}^{1}[A_{R}]-\phi_{\Lambda_{r}^{c}}^{0}[A_{R}]
.

\square

Notice that (
4.2
)–(
4.2
) also show that

\displaystyle\Delta_{p}(r,R)\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\mathrm{Cov}[A_{r/2},A_{R}]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\mathrm{Cov}[A_{r/2},A_{R/2}]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\Delta_{p}(r,R/2)\geq\Delta_{p}(r,R),

and more generally that replacing
R
by a constant multiple of
R
only affects
\Delta_{p}
by a multiplicative constant.

Finally, we claim that all boosting pairs of boundary conditions at scale
R

influence
A_{r/2}
by a similar amount; the same holds from the inside out.

Lemma 4.11

Fix
p
and
4r\leq R\leq L(p)
. Then

(i)
for any
\eta
-well-separated inner flower domain
\mathscr{G}
on
\Lambda_{R}
and
any boosting pair of boundary conditions
\xi,\xi^{\prime}
on
\mathscr{G}
,

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi^{\prime}}[A_{r/2}]-\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi}[A_{r/2}]\asymp\phi_{\Lambda_{R}}^{1}[A_{r/2}]-\phi_{\Lambda_{R}}^{0}[A_{r/2}];

(4.42)

(ii)
for any
\eta
-well-separated outer flower domain
\mathscr{G}
on
\Lambda_{r}
and
any boosting pair of boundary conditions
\xi,\xi^{\prime}
on
\mathscr{G}
,

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi^{\prime}}[A_{R}]-\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi}[A_{R}]\asymp\phi_{\Lambda_{r}^{c}}^{1}[A_{R}]-\phi_{\Lambda_{r}^{c}}^{0}[A_{R}].

(4.43)

Figure 8:
The graph
G
(the black edges between the true vertices of the petals and the vertices in red mean that they are all merged into the red vertex, or equivalently that we added
open
edges between them and the red vertices. We also depicted the flower domains
\mathscr{G}
and
\mathscr{F}_{\tau}
, as well as the event
H
, that corresponds to the occurrence of the red paths.

Proof

We will only treat point
(i)
as point
(ii)
is identical.

By the monotonicity of boundary conditions,

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi^{\prime}}[A_{r/2}]-\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi}[A_{r/2}]\leq\phi_{\Lambda_{R}}^{1}[A_{r/2}]-\phi_{\Lambda_{R}}^{0}[A_{r/2}].

We turn to the converse bound. We recommend to take a look at Figure
8
.
By monotonicity, we may assume that
\xi,\xi^{\prime}
are both coherent with
\mathscr{G}
,
and that there exists exactly one pair of sets in the partition
\xi
that are wired together in
\xi^{\prime}
(due to the coherence condition, each such set contains at least one primal petal of
\mathscr{G}
).
Consider the graph
G
obtained from
\mathscr{G}
as follows.
All vertices contained in a non-singleton set of the partition
\xi

are collapsed to a single point (in particular, all points on each primal petal of
\mathscr{G}
get collapsed together).
Write
a_{1},\dots,a_{k}
for the points thus obtained.
Then there exist two distinct points
a_{i}
and
a_{j}

such that the corresponding groups of petals are wired in
\xi^{\prime}
.
Finally,
G
is the graph obtained after the collapsing procedure described above,
with an additional edge
f
between
a_{i}
and
a_{j}
.
There is an obvious correspondence between the edges of
\mathscr{G}
and those of
G\setminus\{f\}
,
and we will identify them from now on. Let
\phi_{G}
be the random-cluster measure on the finite graph
G
(note that
G
is not a subgraph of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
).

With the construction above,
\phi^{\xi}_{\mathscr{G}}
and
\phi^{\xi^{\prime}}_{\mathscr{G}}
are simply the restrictions of

\phi_{G}[\cdot\,|\omega_{f}=0]
and
\phi_{G}[\cdot\,|\omega_{f}=1]
, respectively, to
\mathscr{G}
.
As such,
\phi_{G}[A_{r/2}]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!1
and Bayes’s formula imply that

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi^{\prime}}[A_{r/2}]-\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi}[A_{r/2}]

\displaystyle=\phi_{G}[A_{r/2}|\omega_{f}=1]-\phi_{G}[A_{r/2}|\omega_{f}=0]

\displaystyle\geq\phi_{G}[A_{r/2}|\omega_{f}=1]-\phi_{G}[A_{r/2}]

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\phi_{G}[\omega_{f}=1|A_{r/2}]-\phi_{G}[\omega_{f}=1].

Let now
\mathbb{P}
be the coupling between
\phi_{G}
and
\phi_{G}[\cdot\,|A_{r/2}]
obtained as follows:

•

reveal the edges of
\Lambda_{R/2}
in the order dictated by Remark
3.10
.
If this stage produces an outer flower domain with a boosting pair of boundary conditions,
apply Theorem
4.1
(ii) up to the associated stopping time
\tau
;

•

reveal all remaining edges of
\mathscr{G}
;

•

reveal the state of
f
.

If
\tau<\infty
,
there exist two primal petals
P_{i}
and
P_{j}
of
\mathscr{F}_{\tau}
that are wired in
\omega_{[\tau]}^{\prime}
but not in
\omega_{[\tau]}
.
Choose arbitrarily one such pair of petals.
Let
H=H(\mathscr{F}_{\tau},\omega_{[\tau]},\omega_{[\tau]}^{\prime})
be the event that
a_{i}
is connected to
P_{i}
in
\omega\cap\mathscr{F}_{\tau}
,
that
a_{j}
is connected to
P_{j}
in
\omega\cap\mathscr{F}_{\tau}
,
but that
a_{i}
and
a_{j}
are not connected to each other or to any other primal petal of
\mathscr{F}_{\tau}
in
\omega\cap\mathscr{F}_{\tau}
.

Since
\mathscr{F}_{\tau}
is
1/2
-well-separated and
\mathscr{G}
is
\eta
-well-separated, and since
\mathscr{F}_{\tau}\cap\mathscr{G}
contains the annulus
{\rm Ann}(R/2,R)
,
by standard applications of Theorem
2.1
,
we find that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(H\,|\,\tau<\infty,\mathscr{F}_{\tau},\omega_{[\tau]},\omega_{[\tau]}^{\prime})\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!1.

The occurrence of
H
may be determined before revealing the state of the edge
f
.
Moreover, if
H
occurs, then the endpoints
a_{i}
and
a_{j}
of
f

are connected in
\omega^{\prime}\setminus\{f\}
, but not in
\omega\setminus\{f\}
.
Indeed,
\omega^{\prime}
dominates
\omega
, which implies that

a_{i}
is connected to
P_{i}
(in
\omega^{\prime}\cap\mathscr{F}_{\tau}
), which is connected to
P_{j}
(in
\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau]}
),
which in turn is connected to
a_{j}
(in
\omega^{\prime}\cap\mathscr{F}_{\tau}
).

It follows that, at the last step of the coupling, if
\tau<\infty
and
H
occurs,
there is a probability
\frac{(1-p)q}{p+(1-p)q}
that
f
is closed in
\omega
but open in
\omega^{\prime}
.
To summarise, we find

\displaystyle\phi_{G}[\omega_{f}=1|A_{r/2}]-\phi_{G}[\omega_{f}=1]=\mathbb{P}[\omega_{f}=0,\omega^{\prime}_{f}=1]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\mathbb{P}[H\text{ and }\tau<\infty]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\mathbb{P}[\tau<\infty].

Finally, by Theorem
4.1
(ii), the fact that
\phi_{G}[A_{R/2}]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!1

and the comparison between boundary conditions (
CBC
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\tau<\infty]

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\mathbb{P}[\text{$\omega$ and\penalty\ $\omega^{\prime}$ induce different b.c. on\penalty\ $\Lambda_{R/2}^{c}$}]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}[\omega^{\prime}\in A_{R/2}\text{ and }\omega\notin A_{R/2}]

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\mathrm{Cov}_{G}(A_{r/2},A_{R/2})\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\phi_{\Lambda_{R}}^{1}[A_{r/2}]-\phi_{\Lambda_{R}}^{0}[A_{r/2}],

where
\mathrm{Cov}_{G}
is the covariance under
\phi_{G}
and the last inequality is given by (
4.16
).
Equations (
4.2
)–(
4.2
) prove

\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi^{\prime}}[A_{r/2}]-\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi}[A_{r/2}]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\phi_{\Lambda_{R}}^{1}[A_{r/2}]-\phi_{\Lambda_{R}}^{0}[A_{r/2}],

as desired.

\square

We are finally ready to prove Theorem
4.8
.

Proof of Theorem
4.8

We focus on the case where
\mathscr{G}
is an inner flower domain on
\Lambda_{R}
;
the case where
\mathscr{G}
is an outer flower domain is identical.
Recall that
\xi
and
\xi^{\prime}
form a boosting pair of boundary conditions on
\mathscr{G}
and that
\tau
is the stopping time
associated to the coupling of Theorem
4.1
between
\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi^{\prime}}
and
\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi}
.
Due to Lemmata
4.9
, we find that

\mathbb{P}[\tau<\infty]\asymp\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi^{\prime}}[A_{r/2}]-\phi_{\mathscr{G}}^{\xi}[A_{r/2}].

Now, Lemmata
4.11
and
4.11
indicate that the right-hand side above is of order
\Delta_{p}(r,R)
, and the proof is complete.

\square

4.3
\Delta_{p}
controls the mixing rate: proof of Theorem
1.6
(v)

For the lower bound, (
4.19
) gives that

\max\Big\{\Big|\frac{\phi_{p}[A\cap B]}{\phi_{p}[A]\phi_{p}[B]}-1\Big|\,:\,A\in\mathscr{F}(\Lambda_{r}),B\in\mathscr{F}(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\Lambda_{R})\Big\}\geq\frac{\phi_{p}[A_{r/2}\cap A_{R}]}{\phi_{p}[A_{r/2}]\phi_{p}[A_{R}]}-1\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\Delta_{p}(r,R).

For the upper bound, use the spatial Markov property (
SMP
) to get that for every
A\in\mathscr{F}(\Lambda_{r})
and
B\in\mathscr{F}(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\Lambda_{R})
,

\Big|\frac{\phi_{p}[A\cap B]}{\phi_{p}[A]\phi_{p}[B]}-1\Big|\leq\max\Big\{\Big|\frac{\phi_{\Lambda_{R},p}^{\xi^{\prime}}[A]}{\phi_{\Lambda_{R},p}^{\xi}[A]}-1\Big|\ :\xi,\xi^{\prime}\text{ b.c. on }\partial\Lambda_{R}\Big\}.

(4.56)

Now, consider the coupling
\mathbb{P}
between
\omega^{0}
and
\omega^{1}
constructed in Theorem
4.8

on
\Lambda_{R}\setminus\Lambda_{2r}
and boundary conditions
0
and
1
respectively on
\Lambda_{R}
(which form a boosting pair).
By applying the same decision tree for the boundary conditions
\xi
and
\xi^{\prime}
, we obtain two additional configurations

\omega^{\xi},\omega^{\xi^{\prime}}
with laws
\phi_{\Lambda_{R}}^{\xi}
and
\phi_{\Lambda_{R}}^{\xi^{\prime}}
, respectively,
and such that
\omega^{0}\leq\omega^{\xi}\leq\omega^{1}
and
\omega^{0}\leq\omega^{\xi^{\prime}}\leq\omega^{1}
.
If
\zeta
and
\zeta^{\prime}
are the boundary conditions induced on
\partial\Lambda_{2r}
by
\omega^{0}
and
\omega^{1}
, we see that

\phi_{\Lambda_{R},p}^{\xi^{\prime}}[A]-\phi_{\Lambda_{R},p}^{\xi}[A]\leq\mathbb{P}[\zeta\neq\zeta^{\prime}]\max_{\psi}\phi_{\Lambda_{2r},p}^{\psi}[A]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\Delta_{p}(r,R)\phi_{\Lambda_{R},p}^{\xi}[A],

where in the second inequality we used Theorem
4.1
as well as the mixing property to replace
\phi_{\Lambda_{2r},p}^{\psi}[A]
by
\phi_{\Lambda_{R},p}^{\xi}[A]
. This concludes the proof.

4.4
Quasi-multiplicativity of
\Delta_{p}
: proof of Theorem
1.6
(ii)

The following corollary is a slight generalisation (in the introduction it is the case
r=0
only) of the quasi-multiplicativity property of Theorem
1.6
.

Corollary 4.12
(Quasi-multiplicativity of
\Delta_{p}
)

For any
p
and
r<n<R\leq L(p)
,

\displaystyle\Delta_{p}(r,R)\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\Delta_{p}(r,n)\Delta_{p}(n,R)\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\Delta_{p}(r,R).

Proof

Let
\mathbb{P}
be the coupling between
\phi_{\Lambda_{R}}^{1}
and
\phi_{\Lambda_{R}}^{0}
in
{\rm Ann}(n,R)
given by Theorem
4.1
(i)
and let
\zeta
and
\zeta^{\prime}
be the boundary conditions induced by
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}
on
\partial\Lambda_{n}
.
Complete the coupling inside
\Lambda_{n}
by an arbitrary increasing coupling of
\phi_{\Lambda_{n}}^{\zeta}
and
\phi_{\Lambda_{n}}^{\zeta^{\prime}}
.
Write
\mathbb{E}
for the expectation associated to
\mathbb{P}
.

On the one hand, Theorem
4.8
and the comparison between boundary conditions (
CBC
) yields

\displaystyle\Delta_{p}(r,R)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\zeta\neq\zeta^{\prime}\}}(\phi_{\Lambda_{n}}^{\zeta^{\prime}}[\mathscr{C}(\Lambda_{r})]-\phi_{\Lambda_{n}}^{\zeta}[\mathscr{C}(\Lambda_{r})])]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}[\zeta\neq\zeta^{\prime}](\phi_{\Lambda_{n}}^{1}[\mathscr{C}(\Lambda_{r})]-\phi_{\Lambda_{n}}^{0}[\mathscr{C}(\Lambda_{r})])

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\Delta_{p}(n,R)\Delta_{p}(r,n).

On the other hand, recall that when
\tau<\infty
,

\mathscr{F}_{\tau}
is a
1/2
-well-separated inner flower domain on
\Lambda_{n}

and that
\omega_{[\tau]}
and
\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau]}
induce a boosting pair of boundary conditions on
\mathscr{F}_{\tau}
.
Thus, Theorem
4.8
implies

\displaystyle\Delta_{p}(r,R)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau<\infty\}}(\phi_{\mathscr{F}_{\tau}}^{\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau]}}[\mathscr{C}(\Lambda_{r})]-\phi_{\mathscr{F}_{\tau}}^{\omega_{[\tau]}}[\mathscr{C}(\Lambda_{r})])]

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\mathbb{P}[\tau<\infty]\Delta_{p}(n,R)

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\Delta_{p}(r,n)\Delta_{p}(n,R).

\square

4.5
Mixing rate versus pivotality: proof of Theorem
1.6
(v)

This section concerns the proof of (
1.18
). This property is not used in the rest of the paper, but it proves that Kesten’s scaling relation does not extend with
\pi_{4}(p,r,R)
instead of
\Delta_{p}(r,R)
.

The upper bound follows trivially from Proposition
2.9
, hence we are left with proving

\displaystyle\Delta_{p}(r,R)\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!(R/r)^{c}\,\pi_{4}(p;r,R),

for some constant
c>0
and all
p
and
r\leq R\leq L(p)
.

Remark 4.13

Note that the bound
\Delta_{p}(r,R)\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\pi_{4}(p,r,R)
follows readily from Theorems
4.1
and
4.8
.
Thus, it is the polynomial improvement of
(R/r)^{c}
that is the core of the above inequality.
The rest of the section is devoted to the proof of (
4.5
).

We will use the following notation.
Let
r<R
,
\mathscr{F}
be an outer flower domain on
\Lambda_{r}
and
\mathscr{G}
be an inner flower domain on
\Lambda_{R}
,
each containing exactly four petals denoted by
P_{1},\dots,P_{4}
and
P_{1}^{\prime},\dots,P_{4}^{\prime}
, respectively.
Then
\xi^{1}
(resp.
\xi^{0}
) are the boundary conditions on
\mathscr{F}
which are coherent with its flower domain structure
and in which the primal petals
P_{1}
and
P_{3}
are wired (resp. not wired) together.
The similarly defined boundary conditions on
\mathscr{G}
are written
\zeta^{1}
and
\zeta^{0}
, respectively.

For
i,j\in\{0,1\}
, denote by
\phi_{\mathscr{F}\cap\mathscr{G}}^{\xi^{i}\cup\zeta^{j}}
the measure on the subgraph
\mathscr{F}\cap\mathscr{G}
with
the boundary condition
\xi^{i}\cup\zeta^{j}
which is the partition of
\partial(\mathscr{F}\cap\mathscr{G})=\partial\mathscr{F}\cup\partial\mathscr{G}
given by the union of the partitions
\xi^{i}
of the inner boundary and
\zeta^{j}
of the outer one.

For configurations on
\mathscr{F}\cap\mathscr{G}
, define the events

\displaystyle A_{4}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G}):=\{P_{1}\xleftrightarrow{\omega}P_{1}^{\prime},\,P_{3}\xleftrightarrow{\omega}P_{3}^{\prime},\,P_{2}\xleftrightarrow{\omega^{*}}P_{2}^{\prime},P_{4}\xleftrightarrow{\omega^{*}}P_{4}^{\prime}\},

\displaystyle\tilde{A}_{4}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G}):=\{P_{1}\xleftrightarrow{\omega^{\prime}}P_{1}^{\prime},\,P_{3}\xleftrightarrow{\omega^{\prime}}P_{3}^{\prime},\,P_{2}\xleftrightarrow{\omega^{*}}P_{2}^{\prime},P_{4}\xleftrightarrow{\omega^{*}}P_{4}^{\prime}\}.

Also write
\phi_{\mathscr{F}\cap\Lambda_{R}}^{\xi^{i}\cup 1}
and
\phi_{\mathscr{F}\cap\Lambda_{R}}^{\xi^{i}\cup 0}
for the measure on
\mathscr{F}\cap\Lambda_{R}

with boundary conditions
\xi^{i}
on
\partial\mathscr{F}
and respectively wired and free boundary conditions on
\partial\Lambda_{R}
.
Define
A_{4}(\mathscr{F},R)
and
\tilde{A}_{4}(\mathscr{F},R)
as in the last display, with
P_{1}^{\prime},\dots,P_{4}^{\prime}
all replaced by
\partial\Lambda_{R}
.

The following lemma states that the probability of the four-arm event
A_{4}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})

increases substantially if we allow the primal arms to be in
\omega^{\prime}
rather than in
\omega
. It is particularly important in the lemma below that
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{G}
are not assumed to be well-separated.

Lemma 4.14

There exists
\delta>0
such that the following holds.
For any
p\in(0,1)
and
1\leq r\leq L(p)
,
any outer flower domain
\mathscr{F}
on
\Lambda_{r}
and any inner flower domain
\mathscr{G}
on
\Lambda_{2r}
,
both containing exactly four petals, and any
i\in\{0,1\}
,
there exists a coupling
\mathbb{P}
of
\phi_{\mathscr{F}\cap\mathscr{G}}^{\xi^{i}\cup\zeta^{0}}
and
\phi_{\mathscr{F}\cap\mathscr{G}}^{\xi^{i}\cup\zeta^{1}}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\tilde{A}_{4}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})]\geq(1+\delta)\mathbb{P}[A_{4}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})].

(4.63)

Proof

Fix
p
,
r
,
i
,
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{G}
as above.
We will first treat the case where both flower domains are well-separated, then use it to solve the general case.

Proof when
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{G}
are
1/2
-well-separated.

Let
x=(3n/2,0)
and start the coupling by exploring the double four-petal flower domain
(\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out})

between
\Lambda_{n/8}(x)
and
\Lambda_{n/4}(x)
in
\omega
.
If this stage fails, reveal the rest of the configuration in an arbitrary increasing fashion.
If
(\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out})
exists, continue by revealing the configuration inside
\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}
.
Write
\xi_{{\rm in}}^{0}<\xi_{{\rm in}}^{1}
for the two boundary conditions on
\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}
which are coherent with the flower domain structure.

We now use the same argument as in the proof of (
3.7
) to study the connection probability between

P_{1}^{\rm in}
and
P_{3}^{\rm in}
inside
\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}
in
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}
.
The conditional law of
\omega
in
\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}
is

\displaystyle(1-\lambda)\phi_{\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}}^{\xi_{\rm in}^{0}}+\lambda\phi_{\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}}^{\xi_{\rm in}^{1}}\qquad\text{ with }\qquad\lambda:=\phi_{\mathscr{F}\cap\mathscr{G}}^{\xi^{i}\cup\zeta^{0}}[P_{1}^{\rm out}\xleftrightarrow{\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}}P_{3}^{\rm out}\,|\,\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}],

while that of
\omega^{\prime}
dominates

\displaystyle(1-\lambda^{\prime})\phi_{\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}}^{\xi_{\rm in}^{0}}+\lambda^{\prime}\phi_{\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}}^{\xi_{\rm in}^{i}}\qquad\text{ with }\qquad\lambda^{\prime}:=\phi_{\mathscr{F}\cap\mathscr{G}}^{\xi^{i}\cup\zeta^{1}}[P_{1}^{\rm out}\xleftrightarrow{\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}}P_{3}^{\rm out}\,|\,\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}].

Thus,
\lambda^{\prime}-\lambda
may be lower bounded by the probability that
P_{1}^{\rm out}
and
P_{3}^{\rm out}

are connected in
\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}
to the two primal petals of
\mathscr{G}
, but not to each other.
By Theorem
2.1
, we conclude that
\lambda^{\prime}-\lambda\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!1
, and by Lemma
3.7
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\omega^{\prime}\in\{P_{1}^{\rm in}\xleftrightarrow{\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}}P_{3}^{\rm in}\}\text{ but }\omega\notin\{P_{1}^{\rm in}\xleftrightarrow{\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}}P_{3}^{\rm in}\}\,|\,\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!1.

Finally, reveal the configurations on
\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}
.

Let
H
be the event that in
\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}
:

•

P_{1}^{\rm out}
is connected to
P_{1}
in
\omega
,

•

P_{3}^{\rm out}
is connected to
P_{1}^{\prime}
in
\omega
,

•

P_{3}
is connected to
P_{3}^{\prime}
in
\omega
,

•

P_{2}
,
P_{2}^{\prime}
and
P_{4}^{\rm out}
are connected in
\omega^{*}
, and

•

P_{4}
,
P_{4}^{\prime}
and
P_{2}^{\rm out}
are connected in
\omega^{*}
.

In other words,
H
is the event that the connection between
P_{1}^{\rm in}
and
P_{3}^{\rm in}
inside
\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}

is pivotal for
A_{4}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})
. See Figure
9
for an illustration.
Theorem
2.1
and the well-separation of
\mathscr{F}
,
\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}
and
\mathscr{G}
imply that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[H\,|\,\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}\text{ and\penalty\ $(\omega,\omega^{\prime})$ on\penalty\ $\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}$}]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!1.

Figure 9:
In the graph
\mathscr{F}\cap\mathscr{G}
, we first explore the double four-petal flower domain
(\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out})
,
then reveal the configurations in
\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}
and
\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}
. If
H
occurs (see the blue paths),
then
A_{4}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})
depends on the connection inside
\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}
between its primal petals.
If this connection occurs in
\omega^{\prime}
but not in
\omega
, then the configurations are in
\tilde{A}_{4}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})\setminus A_{4}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})
.

Now, since
A_{4}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})
implies the occurrence of
\tilde{A}_{4}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\tilde{A}_{4}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})]-\mathbb{P}[A_{4}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})]\geq\mathbb{P}[(\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out})\text{ exist},\,P_{1}^{\rm out}\xleftrightarrow{\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}}P_{3}^{\rm out}\text{ in\penalty\ $\omega^{\prime}$ but not in\penalty\ $\omega$},\,H].

Finally, (
4.5
), (
4.5
) and Lemma
3.4
together imply that the right-hand side is larger than
c_{0}>0
.
This concludes the proof of (
4.63
) when
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{G}
are
1/2
-well-separated.

Proof when
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{G}
are not
1/2
-well-separated.

We will construct the coupling
\mathbb{P}
between
\phi_{\mathscr{F}\cap\mathscr{G}}^{\xi^{i},\zeta^{0}}
and
\phi_{\mathscr{F}\cap\mathscr{G}}^{\xi^{i},\zeta^{1}}
in two steps.
We start by exploring the outer flower domain
\overline{\mathscr{F}}
between
\Lambda_{r}
and
\Lambda_{5r/8}
in
\omega

and the inner flower domain
\overline{\mathscr{G}}
from
\Lambda_{2r}
to
\Lambda_{7r/8}
also in
\omega
.
Say that
(\overline{\mathscr{F}},\overline{\mathscr{G}})
is
good
if

•

\overline{\mathscr{F}}
and
\overline{\mathscr{G}}
each contain exactly four petals
and are
1/2
-well-separated;

•

the four petals of
\overline{\mathscr{F}}
are connected in
\mathscr{F}\setminus\overline{\mathscr{F}}
to the corresponding petals of
\mathscr{F}

by paths of alternating types in
\omega
;

•

the four petals of
\overline{\mathscr{G}}
are connected in
\mathscr{G}\setminus\overline{\mathscr{G}}
to the corresponding petals of
\mathscr{G}

by paths of alternating types in
\omega
.

If
(\overline{\mathscr{F}},\overline{\mathscr{G}})
is not good, complete
(\omega,\omega^{\prime})
inside
\overline{\mathscr{F}}\cap\overline{\mathscr{G}}

using an arbitrary increasing coupling.
When
(\overline{\mathscr{F}},\overline{\mathscr{G}})
is good,
the first case may be applied to
\overline{\mathscr{F}}
and
\overline{\mathscr{G}}
,
and provides a way to complete
(\omega,\omega^{\prime})
inside
\overline{\mathscr{F}}\cap\overline{\mathscr{G}}

so that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\tilde{A}_{4}(\overline{\mathscr{F}},\overline{\mathscr{G}})\setminus A_{4}(\overline{\mathscr{F}},\overline{\mathscr{G}})\,|\,\text{$(\overline{\mathscr{F}},\overline{\mathscr{G}})$ good}]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!1,

Moreover, notice that in this case
A_{4}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})
and
\tilde{A}_{4}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})
occur if and only if

A_{4}(\overline{\mathscr{F}},\overline{\mathscr{G}})
and
\tilde{A}_{4}(\overline{\mathscr{F}},\overline{\mathscr{G}})
, respectively, do.
By summing the display above, we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\tilde{A}_{4}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})]-\mathbb{P}[A_{4}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\mathbb{P}[\text{$(\overline{\mathscr{F}},\overline{\mathscr{G}})$ good}].

Finally, it is a standard consequence of the RSW theory (specifically of the separation of arms) that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\text{$(\overline{\mathscr{F}},\overline{\mathscr{G}})$ good}]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\mathbb{P}[A_{4}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})],

where the constant in
\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!
does not depend on
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{G}
.
The last two displays provide the desired conclusion.

\square

Corollary 4.15

For
\rho
large enough,
p\in(0,1)
and
r<R\leq L(p)
with
R=(\rho^{2}+2)^{k}r
,
any outer flower domain
\mathscr{F}
on
\Lambda_{r}
with exactly four petals
and any
i\in\{0,1\}
,
there exists an increasing coupling
\mathbb{P}
of
\phi_{\mathscr{F}\cap\Lambda_{R}}^{\xi^{i}\cup 0}
and
\phi_{\mathscr{F}\cap\Lambda_{R}}^{\xi^{i}\cup 1}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\tilde{A}_{4}(\mathscr{F},R)]\geq(1+\tfrac{\delta}{2})^{k}\,\mathbb{P}[A_{4}(\mathscr{F},R)],

(4.68)

where
\delta>0
is the constant given by Lemma
4.14
.

Proof

The proof proceeds by induction on
k
.
Consider the value of
\rho>1
fixed; it will be chosen at the end of the proof
and it will be apparent that it is independent of
p
,
r
,
R
or
\mathscr{F}
.
The case
k=0
is trivially true.

Fix
k\geq 0
and assume that (
4.68
) holds for this value of
k
; we will now prove (
4.68
) for
k+1
.
Let
\mathscr{F}
be an inner flower domain on
\Lambda_{r}
and fix boundary conditions
\xi\in\{\xi^{0},\xi^{1}\}
on
\partial\mathscr{F}
.
The coupling
\mathbb{P}
is built in three steps:

Step 1:

Explore the inner flower domain from
\partial\Lambda_{2r\rho}
to
\partial\Lambda_{2r}

and the outer one from
\partial\Lambda_{2r\rho}
to
\partial\Lambda_{2r\rho^{2}}
in
\omega
;
call these
\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}}
and
\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}}
, respectively.
We will abuse notation by identifying
(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}},\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}})
with the entire configuration
(\omega,\omega^{\prime})
on
\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}}^{c}\cap\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}}^{c}
.
Say that
(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}},\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}})
is
good
if both
\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}}
and
\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}}
have exactly four petals

P^{\rm in}_{1},\dots,P^{\rm in}_{4}
and
P^{\rm out}_{1},\dots,P^{\rm out}_{4}
, respectively, and if
in
\omega\cap(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}}^{c}\cap\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}}^{c})
there exist open paths connecting

P^{\rm in}_{1}
to
P^{\rm out}_{1}
and
P^{\rm in}_{3}
to
P^{\rm out}_{3}

and dual-open paths connecting
P^{\rm in}_{2}
to
P^{\rm out}_{2}
and
P^{\rm in}_{4}
to
P^{\rm out}_{4}
.
As before, we use the notation
\zeta_{{\rm in}}^{0}
,
\zeta_{{\rm in}}^{1}
for the two boundary conditions on
\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}}
coherent with it being a flower domain, and
\zeta_{{\rm out}}^{0}
and
\zeta_{{\rm out}}^{1}
for those on
\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}}
.

Stage 2:

If
(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}},\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}})
is not good, we sample the rest of the configurations according to an arbitrary increasing coupling.
If
(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}},\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}})
is good,
observe that the law of
\omega
in
\mathscr{G}_{\rm out}
, conditionally on the revealed set, is a linear combination

(1-\lambda)\phi_{\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}}\cap\Lambda_{R}}^{\zeta_{{\rm out}}^{0}\cup 0}+\lambda\phi_{\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}}\cap\Lambda_{R}}^{\zeta_{{\rm out}}^{1}\cup 0}\qquad\text{where}\qquad\lambda:=\phi_{\mathscr{F}\cap\Lambda_{R}}^{\xi\cup 0}[P^{\rm in}_{1}\xleftrightarrow{\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}}}P^{\rm in}_{3}\,|\,(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}},\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}})].

Moreover, the conditional law of
\omega^{\prime}
dominates

\displaystyle(1-\lambda)\phi_{\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}}\cap\Lambda_{R}}^{\zeta_{{\rm out}}^{0}\cup 1}+\lambda\phi_{\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}}\cap\Lambda_{R}}^{\zeta_{{\rm out}}^{1}\cup 1},

(4.69)

for the same value
\lambda
.

Since
\tilde{A}_{4}(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}},R)
is increasing in
\omega^{\prime}
and decreasing in
\omega
,
we may use (
4.68
) for
k
(which is our induction hypothesis) applied between
\mathscr{G}_{\rm out}
and
\Lambda_{R}
,
once with the boundary conditions
\zeta_{{\rm out}}^{0}
on
\mathscr{G}_{\rm out}
and once with the boundary conditions
\zeta_{{\rm out}}^{1}
,
to produce an increasing coupling of
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}
on
\mathscr{G}_{\rm out}
so that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\tilde{A}_{4}(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}},R)|\,(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}},\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}})\text{ good}]

\displaystyle\geq(1+\tfrac{\delta}{2})^{k}\,\mathbb{P}[A_{4}(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}},R)|\,(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}},\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}})\text{ good}].

(4.70)

Step 3:

Finally, we sample
(\omega,\omega^{\prime})
in
\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}}\cap\mathscr{F}
according to a specific coupling between the measures in this region,
with boundary conditions induced by the previously revealed parts of
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}
, respectively.
Recall that this stage is reached only if
(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}},\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}})
is good
and that the revealed configurations suffice to decide whether
\tilde{A}_{4}(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}},R)
occurred or not.

If
\tilde{A}_{4}(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}},R)
did not occur, complete the coupling in an arbitrary increasing way.
If
\tilde{A}_{4}(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}},R)
did occur,
then the boundary conditions induced by the already revealed parts of
\omega
on
\mathscr{G}_{\rm in}
are equal to
\zeta_{{\rm in}}^{0}
.
Indeed, in
\omega\cap\mathscr{G}_{\rm out}
,
P^{\rm out}_{1}
and
P^{\rm out}_{3}
are disconnected from each other due to the dual arms.
On the contrary, the boundary conditions induced by the revealed region of
\omega^{\prime}
on
\mathscr{G}_{\rm in}
dominate
\zeta_{{\rm in}}^{1}
,
since in
\omega^{\prime}\cap\mathscr{G}_{\rm out}
,
P^{\rm out}_{1}
and
P^{\rm out}_{3}
are connected to
\partial\Lambda_{R}
, which is wired.
Thus, we may apply Lemma
4.14
to continue the coupling so that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\tilde{A}_{4}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}})|\,(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}},\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}})\text{ good and }\tilde{A}_{4}(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}},R)]

\displaystyle\quad\geq(1+\delta)\mathbb{P}[A_{4}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}})|\,(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}},\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}})\text{ good and }\tilde{A}_{4}(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}},R)].

(4.71)

This concludes the construction of the coupling
\mathbb{P}
; next we show that
\mathbb{P}
satisfies (
4.68
).

If
(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}},\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}})
is good and
\tilde{A}_{4}(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}},R)
and
\tilde{A}_{4}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}})
both occur,
then so does
\tilde{A}_{4}(\mathscr{F},R)
.
By (
4.70
), (
4.71
),
and the fact that
(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}},\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}})
are determined by
\omega
alone, we find

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\tilde{A}_{4}(\mathscr{F},R)]

\displaystyle\geq(1+\delta)(1+\tfrac{\delta}{2})^{k}\sum_{(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}},\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}})\text{ good}}\phi_{\mathscr{F}}^{\xi\cup 0}[(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}},\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}})]\phi_{\mathscr{F}}^{\xi\cup 0}[A_{4}(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}},R)\cap A_{4}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}})|\,(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}},\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}})]

\displaystyle=(1+\delta)(1+\tfrac{\delta}{2})^{k}\sum_{(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}},\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}})\text{ good}}\phi_{\mathscr{F}}^{\xi\cup 0}[(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}},\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}})]\phi_{\mathscr{F}}^{\xi\cup 0}[A_{4}(\mathscr{F},R)|\,(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}},\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}})]

\displaystyle=(1+\delta)(1+\tfrac{\delta}{2})^{k}\phi_{\mathscr{F}}^{\xi\cup 0}[A_{4}(\mathscr{F},R)\text{ and }(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}},\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}})\text{ good}].

(4.72)

The first equality is due to the fact that, conditionally on a good
(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}},\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}})
,

A_{4}(\mathscr{F},R)
occurs if and only if both
A_{4}(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}},R)
and
A_{4}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}})
do.
The last equality is obtained directly by summation.

Observe now that for
A_{4}(\mathscr{F},R)
to occur,
\mathscr{G}_{\rm in}
and
\mathscr{G}_{\rm out}
need to have at least four petals each.
Moreover, if they each have exactly four petals, then these need to be connected in such a way that

(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}},\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}})
is good.
Thus

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathscr{F}}^{\xi\cup 0}[A_{4}(\mathscr{F},R),(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}},\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}})\text{ not good}]\leq

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathscr{F}}^{\xi\cup 0}[A_{4}(\mathscr{F},R),\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}}\text{ has at least 6 petals}]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathscr{F}}^{\xi\cup 0}[A_{4}(\mathscr{F},R),\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}}\text{ has at least 6 petals}].

(4.73)

We will argue that both of the terms in the right-hand side are small compared to the quantity
\phi_{\mathscr{F}}^{\xi,0}[A_{4}(\mathscr{F},R)]
,
provided
\rho
is large enough.

Indeed, due to the quasi-multiplicativity of the four-arm event – here applied to the slightly unusual event
A_{4}(\mathscr{F},R)
–
and the mixing property (
Mix
),
there exists a universal constant
C
(that does not depend on
\mathscr{F}
,
p
,
r
or
R
) such that

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathscr{F}}^{\xi\cup 0}[A_{4}(\mathscr{F},R),\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}}\text{ has at least 6 petals}]

\displaystyle\leq C\phi_{\mathscr{F}}^{\xi\cup 0}[A_{4}(\mathscr{F},2r)]\phi_{\mathscr{F}}^{\xi\cup 0}[A_{4}(2\rho r,R)]\pi_{6}(2r,2\rho r)

\displaystyle\leq C^{2}\phi_{\mathscr{F}}^{\xi\cup 0}[A_{4}(\mathscr{F},R)]\frac{\pi_{6}(2r,2\rho r)}{\pi_{4}(2r,2\rho r)}.

Due to Theorem
2.1
,
\rho>0
may be chosen independently of
\mathscr{F}
,
p
,
r
or
R
so that

\displaystyle C^{2}\frac{\pi_{6}(2r,2\rho r)}{\pi_{4}(2r,2\rho r)}\leq\delta/4.

Assuming this is the case, and by the same reasoning for the second term of (
4.5
), we find

\displaystyle(1+\delta)\phi_{\mathscr{F}}^{\xi,0}\big[A_{4}(\mathscr{F},R),(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}},\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}})\text{ good}\big]\geq(1+\delta/2)\phi_{\mathscr{F}}^{\xi,0}\big[A_{4}(\mathscr{F},R)\big],

which, when inserted in (
4.5
), proves (
4.68
) for
R=(\rho^{2}+2)^{k+1}r
.

\square

Proof of (
4.5
)

Fix
\delta>0
and
\rho
given by Corollary
4.15
.
Due to Theorem
1.6
(ii),
it suffices to prove the statement for
R=2(\rho^{2}+2)^{k}r
. From now on, fix such values
r
and
R
.

We use the same notation as in the proof of Corollary
4.15

and construct a coupling
\mathbb{P}
between
\phi_{\Lambda_{R}}^{0}
and
\phi_{\Lambda_{R}}^{1}
similar to that of the previous proof.

First, explore the double four-petal flower domain between
\Lambda_{r}
and
\Lambda_{2r}
;
call it
(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}},\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}})
.
If no such double four-petal flower domain exists, proceed with an arbitrary coupling.
If
(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}},\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}})
exists, use the coupling provided by Corollary
4.15
to complete
(\omega,\omega^{\prime})
in
\mathscr{G}_{\rm out}
so that,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[A_{4}(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}},R)\,|\,(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}},\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}})]

\displaystyle\geq(1+\tfrac{\delta}{2})^{k}\,\mathbb{P}[A_{4}(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}},R)\,|\,(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}},\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}})].

(4.74)

Finally, use an arbitrary increasing coupling inside
\mathscr{G}_{\rm in}
.

When
(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}},\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}})
exists and
\tilde{A}_{4}(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}},R)
occurs,
the boundary conditions imposed by the revealed portions of
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}

are equal to
\zeta^{0}_{\rm in}
and dominate
\zeta^{1}_{\rm in}
, respectively.
Using Theorem
3.6
and Lemma
3.4
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\Delta_{p}(r,R)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\big[\omega^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}(\Lambda_{r}),\omega\notin\mathscr{C}(\Lambda_{r})\big]

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\mathbb{P}\big[\tilde{A}_{4}(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}},R),\,(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}},\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}})\text{ exists}\big]

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!(1+\tfrac{\delta}{2})^{k}\mathbb{P}\big[A_{4}(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}},R),\,(\mathscr{G}_{{\rm in}},\mathscr{G}_{{\rm out}})\text{ exists}\big]

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!(R/2r)^{\varepsilon}\pi_{4}(r,R),

where
\varepsilon=\log(1+\frac{\delta}{2})/\log{(\rho^{2}+2)}>0
.

\square

5
Derivatives in terms of
\Delta_{p}

5.1
Derivatives for crossing and arm events

In this section we obtain expressions for the derivatives of probabilities of crossing events and arm events in terms of
\Delta_{p}
.
In addition, we upper bound the derivative of the mixing rate
\Delta_{p}
by similar expressions.
The relevant results are Proposition
5.1
and
5.6
, respectively.
These will hold within the critical window, and are instrumental in proving the main stability results Theorem
1.4
and (
1.17
).

We start by a proposition which states a slightly weaker form of Corollary
1.7
,
but extends the expression to logarithmic derivatives of probabilities of arm events.

Proposition 5.1

Fix
\eta>0
. For
p\in(0,1)
and every
\eta
-regular quad
(\mathscr{D},a,b,c,d)
at scale
R\leq L(p)
,

R^{2}\Delta_{p}(R)+\sum_{\ell=R}^{L(p)}\ell\Delta_{p}(\ell)\Delta_{p}(R,\ell)\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\tfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}p}\phi_{p}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\sum_{\ell=1}^{R}\ell\Delta_{p}(\ell)+\sum_{\ell=R}^{L(p)}\ell\Delta_{p}(\ell)\Delta_{p}(R,\ell),

(5.1)

where the constants in
\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!
depend on
\eta
.
Moreover, for any
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{k}
and any
r\leq R\leq L(p)
,

R^{2}\Delta_{p}(R)+\sum_{\ell=R}^{L(p)}\ell\Delta_{p}(\ell)\Delta_{p}(R,\ell)\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\tfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}p}\log\phi_{p}[A_{\sigma}(r,R)]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\sum_{\ell=1}^{R}\ell\Delta_{p}(\ell)+\sum_{\ell=R}^{L(p)}\ell\Delta_{p}(\ell)\Delta_{p}(R,\ell),

where the constants in
\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!
depend on
\sigma
.

After proving Proposition
1.8
in the next section, we may use the quasi-multiplicativity of
\Delta_{p}
to replace the first term
\sum_{\ell\leq R}\ell\Delta_{p}(\ell)
in the upper bounds above by
R^{2}\Delta_{p}(R)
, hence deducing an up to constant estimate on the derivative. This is stated in Corollary
7.1
.

Remark 5.2

The formula (
5.1
) for the derivative of the crossing probability of
\mathscr{D}

is significantly different than the one for percolation,
since edges far from
\mathscr{D}
– which correspond to the second term in the formula – may contribute substantially.
Indeed, if we accept the asymptotic
\Delta(r,R)\asymp(r/R)^{\iota}
, and if
\iota<1
,
then the edges at distance
L(p)
contribute most to (
5.1
); when
\iota>1
however,
the derivative is governed by the contribution of edges close to
\mathscr{D}
.
See Section
8.2.4
for consequences of these two types of behaviour.

The proof of Proposition
5.1
is based on the following lemma,
which controls the influence of each edge on a crossing event.
Below, we call
e
a (open)
r
-pivotal
in
\omega
for a crossing event in
(\mathscr{D},a,b,c,d)

if
\omega
contains a crossing of
\mathscr{D}
from
(ab)
to
(cd)
, but
\omega\cap(\mathscr{D}\setminus\Lambda_{r}(e))
does not.
Call this event
{\rm Piv}_{r,e}(\mathscr{D})
.

Lemma 5.3

There exists
c>0
such that, for any
\eta>0
, the following holds.
For
p\in(0,1)
,
R\leq L(p)
, every
\eta
-regular quad
(\mathscr{D},a,b,c,d)
of size
R

and every edge
e
at a distance
n
from
\partial\mathscr{D}
,

\displaystyle\Delta_{p}(R)\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\mathrm{Cov}_{p}[\omega_{e};\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\sum_{r=n/2}^{3R}\frac{\Delta_{p}(r)}{r}\phi_{p}[{\rm Piv}_{r,e}(\mathscr{D})]

\displaystyle\text{ if\penalty\ $n\leq 2R$},

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}_{p}[\omega_{e};\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]

\displaystyle\asymp\Delta_{p}(n)\Delta_{p}(R,n)

\displaystyle\text{ if\penalty\ $2R\leq n\leq 2L(p)$},

(5.6)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}_{p}[\omega_{e};\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\Delta_{p}(L(p))\Delta_{p}(R,L(p))e^{-cn/L(p)}

\displaystyle\text{ if\penalty\ $n\geq 2L(p)$},

where the constants in
\asymp
and
\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!
depend on
\eta
.

The same bounds hold with

\mathrm{Cov}_{p}[\omega_{e};A_{\sigma}(r,R)]/\phi_{p}[A_{\sigma}(r,R)]
instead of
\mathrm{Cov}_{p}[\omega_{e};\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]
,
with constants which depend on
\sigma
;
n
should then be replaced with the distance from
e
to
\partial{\rm Ann}(r,R)
.

Remark 5.4

Due to the choice of
\mathscr{D}
, all edges
e
inside
\mathscr{D}
are in case (
5.3
). By (
RSW
),
\phi_{p}[{\rm Piv}_{r,e}(\mathscr{D})]
is uniformly bounded away from
0
for all
R\leq r\leq 2R
.
These terms of the sum account for a contribution of order at least
\Delta_{p}(R)
to the right-hand side of (
5.3
).
When
e
is in the “bulk” of
\mathscr{D}
(that is at a distance of order
R
from
\mathscr{D}^{c}
),
the lower and upper bounds in (
5.3
) are comparable.

In addition we point out that the same results hold for covariances under a measure
\phi_{G}^{\xi}
for any sub-graph
G
of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
that contains
\Lambda_{2R}
, any boundary conditions
\xi
, and any edge
e
closer to
\mathscr{D}
than to
G^{c}
. The proof below adapts readily.

Theorem
1.6
(i) is a particular case of Lemma
5.3
.

Proof of Theorem
1.6
(i)

Apply (
5.3
) and observe that the right-hand side is smaller than
\sum_{r=\eta R}^{R}\frac{1}{r}\Delta_{p}(r)\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\Delta_{p}(R)
.

\square

Proof of Lemma
5.3

We will prove here formulas for the crossing of a quad
(\mathscr{D},a,b,c,d)
;
the proof for arm events is similar and entails standard adaptations.
Write
\mathscr{C}
instead of
\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})
.
Recall that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}[\omega_{e};\mathscr{C}]=\phi[\omega_{e}](\phi[\mathscr{C}\,|\,\omega_{e}=1]-\phi[\mathscr{C}])\asymp\phi[\mathscr{C}\,|\,\omega_{e}=1]-\phi[\mathscr{C}\,|\,\omega_{e}=0].

We will prove each of the equations separately, starting with the second.

Proof of (
5.6
):

Construct an increasing coupling
\mathbb{P}
between
\phi[\cdot\,|\,\omega_{e}=0]
and
\phi[\cdot\,|\,\omega_{e}=1]

producing configurations
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}
as follows: the coupling contains three stages based on
Theorem
4.1
(ii), Lemma
3.14
and Theorem
4.8
, respectively. We refer to Figure
10
for a picture.

1.

Apply the coupling inside
\Lambda_{n/4}(e)
between

\phi[\cdot\,|\,\omega_{e}=0]
and
\phi[\cdot\,|\,\omega_{e}=1]

using the procedure of Theorem
4.1
(ii),
up to the associated stopping time, which we denote by
\tau_{1}
.
If
\tau_{1}=\infty
, continue the coupling using an arbitrary increasing coupling.
Recall that when
\tau_{1}<\infty
,

\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{1}}
is a
1/2
-well-separated outer flower domain on
\Lambda_{n/4}

and that
\omega_{[\tau_{1}]}
and
\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau_{1}]}
induce a boosting pair of boundary conditions on
\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{1}}
.

2.

Continue the coupling on
\Lambda_{n/2}^{c}
between

\phi^{\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau_{1}]}}_{\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{1}}}
and
\phi^{\omega_{[\tau_{1}]}}_{\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{1}}}

using the procedure of Lemma
3.14
,
up to the associated stopping time, which we denote by
\tau_{2}
.
If
\tau_{2}=\infty
, continue the coupling using an arbitrary increasing coupling.
Recall that when
\tau_{2}<\infty
,

\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{2}}
is a
1/2
-well-separated inner flower domain on
\Lambda_{n/2}

and that
\omega_{[\tau_{2}]}
and
\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau_{2}]}
induce a boosting pair of boundary conditions on
\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{2}}
.

3.

Complete the coupling inside
\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{2}}
using an arbitrary increasing coupling of

\phi^{\omega^{\prime}_{[\tau_{2}]}}_{\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{2}}}
and
\phi^{\omega_{[\tau_{2}]}}_{\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{2}}}
.

Figure 10:
In blue, the edges discovered until
\tau_{1}
. In red, the edges discovered between
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
. Finally in green, the edges that are discovered afterwards. Note that the domain
\mathscr{D}
is a priori much smaller than the box
\Lambda_{n/2}
.

For the upper bound observe that, since
\mathscr{D}\subset\Lambda_{n/2}
and
\Lambda_{n/2}(e)
are disjoint, if
\zeta
and
\zeta^{\prime}
are the boundary conditions induced on
\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{1}}
, Theorem
4.8
gives

\displaystyle\phi[\mathscr{C}\,|\,\omega_{e}=1]-\phi[\mathscr{C}\,|\,\omega_{e}=0]

\displaystyle\leq(\phi_{\Lambda_{n/2}}^{1}[\mathscr{C}]-\phi_{\Lambda_{n/2}}^{0}[\mathscr{C}])\,\mathbb{P}[\zeta\neq\zeta^{\prime}]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\Delta_{p}(R,n/2)\Delta_{p}(n).

Finally,
\Delta_{p}(R,n/2)
may be replaced by
\Delta_{p}(R,n)
due to (
4.2
).
For the lower bound, Theorem
4.8
gives

\displaystyle\phi[\mathscr{C}\,|\,\omega_{e}=1]-\phi[\mathscr{C}\,|\,\omega_{e}=0]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}[\omega^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C},\omega\notin\mathscr{C}]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{2}<\infty\}}\big(\phi_{\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{2}}}^{\omega^{\prime}_{[{\tau_{2}}]}}[\mathscr{C}]-\phi_{\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{2}}}^{\omega_{[{\tau_{2}}]}}[\mathscr{C}]\big)\big]

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\mathbb{P}[\tau_{1}<\infty]\,\mathbb{P}[\tau_{2}<\infty\,|\,\tau_{1}<\infty]\,\Delta_{p}(R,n/2).

The second term above is of constant order by Lemma
3.14
.
The first term is of order
\Delta_{p}(1,n/4)
by Theorem
4.8
. Since
\Delta_{p}(1,n/4)\geq\Delta_{p}(n)
and
\Delta_{p}(R,n/2)\geq\Delta_{p}(R,n)
, we obtain the result.

\diamond

Proof of (
5.3
):
We focus here on the case
p<p_{c}
; when
p>p_{c}
the proof is obtained by duality.
Construct the following coupling
\mathbb{P}
between
\phi_{p}[\cdot\,|\,\omega_{e}=0]
and
\phi_{p}[\cdot\,|\,\omega_{e}=1]
:

1.

Use the coupling given by Theorem
4.1
(ii) between
\phi_{p}[\cdot\,|\,\omega_{e}=0]
and
\phi_{p}[\cdot\,|\,\omega_{e}=1]
inside
\Lambda_{L(p)}(e)
;
let
\xi
and
\xi^{\prime}
be the boundary conditions induced by the revealed configurations
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}

on the boundary of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\Lambda_{L(p)}(e)
;

2.

Continue the coupling in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\Lambda_{L(p)}
using the decision tree that explores the connected components of
\Lambda_{L(p)}(e)

in
\omega^{\prime}
(see Example 2 of Section
2.3
);
let
\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}
be the boundary conditions induced by the revealed regions of
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}
on
\partial\Lambda_{L(p)}
;

3.

Use an arbitrary increasing coupling of
\phi_{\Lambda_{L(p)},p}^{\zeta}
and
\phi_{\Lambda_{L(p)},p}^{\zeta^{\prime}}
.

If
\xi=\xi^{\prime}
, then
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}
are identical in the complement of
\Lambda_{L(p)}(e)
.
Moreover, as explained in Section
2.3
, for
\zeta
to differ from
\zeta^{\prime}
,

\omega^{\prime}
must contain a connection between
\Lambda_{L(p)}(e)
and
\Lambda_{L(p)}
. Thus, we find

\displaystyle\phi_{p}[\mathscr{C}\,|\,\omega_{e}=1]-\phi_{p}[\mathscr{C}\,|\,\omega_{e}=0]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\xi\neq\xi^{\prime}\}}\mathbbm{1}_{\omega^{\prime}\in\{\Lambda_{L}(e)\xleftrightarrow{}\Lambda_{L(p)}\}}(\phi^{\zeta^{\prime}}_{\Lambda_{L},p}[\mathscr{C}]-\phi^{\zeta}_{\Lambda_{L},p}[\mathscr{C}])\big]

(5.11)

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\Delta_{p}({L(p)})\phi_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\Lambda_{L(p)},p}^{1}[\Lambda_{L(p)}\longleftrightarrow\Lambda_{L(p)}(e)]\Delta_{p}(R,{L(p)}).

In the second inequality we used the monotonicity of boundary conditions (
CBC
) and Theorem
4.8
.
Finally, due to the mixing property of Proposition
2.9
and Proposition
2.12
,
the second term of the last product is bounded above by
\exp[-cn/L(p)]
for some positive constant
c
.

\diamond

Proof of (
5.3
):

For the lower bound, translate
\mathscr{D}
and
e
such that
e\notin\Lambda_{\eta R/2}
and
\Lambda_{\eta R/2}\subset\mathscr{D}
.
Since
\mathscr{D}
is assumed
\eta
-regular, this is possible.
We produce a coupling
\mathbb{P}
between
\phi_{p}[\cdot\,|\,\omega_{e}=0]
and
\phi_{p}[\cdot\,|\,\omega_{e}=1]
as follows.

1.

Explore the double four-petal flower domain
(\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out})
in
\omega
between
\Lambda_{\eta R/4}
and
\Lambda_{\eta R/8}
;
if no such double four-petal flower domain exists, reveal the rest of the configurations in arbitrary order.

2.

Continue
\mathbb{P}
by sampling
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}
inside
\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}
.

3.

Reveal the configurations in
\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}
.

Now, if
(\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out})
exists, the argument of (
5.6
) shows that

\displaystyle\phi[P_{1}^{\rm out}\xleftrightarrow{\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}}P_{3}^{\rm out}|\omega_{e}=1,\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}]-\phi[P_{1}^{\rm out}\xleftrightarrow{\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}}P_{3}^{\rm out}|\omega_{e}=0,\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\Delta_{p}(R).

Indeed,
e
is at a distance comparable to
R
from
(\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out})
;
for details see the proof of point (
3.7
) of Theorem
3.6
.
Note that this is simply a statement on the conditional probability of the connections between the petals
P_{1}
and
P_{3}
,
we do not reveal the configurations
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}
on
\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}
.

Then, as in the proof of (
3.7
), the previous estimate gives that

\displaystyle\phi[P_{1}^{\rm in}\xleftrightarrow{\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}}P_{3}^{\rm in}|\omega_{e}=1,\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}]-\phi[P_{1}^{\rm in}\xleftrightarrow{\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}}P_{3}^{\rm in}|\omega_{e}=0,\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\Delta_{p}(R).

Let
H
be the event that
P_{1}^{\rm out}
and
P_{3}^{\rm out}
are connected in
\omega\cap\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}\cap\mathscr{D}
to the arcs
(ab)
and
(cd)
, respectively, while
P_{2}^{\rm out}
and
P_{4}^{\rm out}
are connected in
\omega^{*}\cap\mathscr{F}_{\rm out}\cap\mathscr{D}
to the arcs
(bc)
and
(da)
respectively.
By Theorem
2.1
,

\displaystyle\phi[H\,|\,(\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out})\text{ and }(\omega,\omega)^{\prime}\text{ on\penalty\ $\mathscr{F}^{c}_{\rm out}$}]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!1.

Finally, when
H
occurs,
\mathscr{D}
is crossed if and only if
P_{1}^{\rm in}
and
P_{3}^{\rm in}
are connected inside
\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}
.
As a consequence, the two displays above and Lemma
3.4
conclude that

\displaystyle\phi_{p}[\mathscr{C}\,|\,\omega_{e}=1]-\phi_{p}[\mathscr{C}\,|\,\omega_{e}=0]

\displaystyle\qquad\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\mathbb{P}[(\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out})\text{ exists}]\mathbb{P}[P_{1}^{\rm in}\xleftrightarrow{\omega^{\prime}\cap\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}}P_{3}^{\rm in}\text{ but }P_{1}^{\rm in}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu/\hfil$\crcr$\displaystyle\xleftrightarrow{\omega\cap\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}}$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\hfil\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu/\hfil$\crcr$\textstyle\xleftrightarrow{\omega\cap\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}}$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu/\hfil$\crcr$\scriptstyle\xleftrightarrow{\omega\cap\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}}$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu/\hfil$\crcr$\scriptscriptstyle\xleftrightarrow{\omega\cap\mathscr{F}_{\rm in}}$\crcr}}}}P_{3}^{\rm in}\,|\,(\mathscr{F}_{\rm in},\mathscr{F}_{\rm out})\text{ exists}]

\displaystyle\qquad\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\Delta_{p}(R).

We turn to the upper bound.
For
s>\lceil\log n\rceil
, let
\mathscr{P}_{s}={\rm Piv}_{2^{s},e}(\mathscr{D})\setminus{\rm Piv}_{2^{s-1},e}(\mathscr{D})
,
and set
\mathscr{P}_{s}={\rm Piv}_{2^{s},e}(\mathscr{D})
when
s=\lceil\log n\rceil
.
These events partition
\mathscr{C}
, and we find that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}_{p}(\omega_{e},\mathscr{C})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s>\log n}(\phi[\omega_{e}|\mathscr{P}_{s}]-\phi[\omega_{e}])\phi_{p}[\mathscr{P}_{s}]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s>\log n}(\phi_{\Lambda_{2^{s-1}}(e)}^{1}[\omega_{e}]-\phi_{\Lambda_{2^{s-1}}(e)}^{0}[\omega_{e}])\phi_{p}[{\rm Piv}_{2^{s},e}(\mathscr{D})]

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\sum_{r\geq n/2}\tfrac{1}{r}\Delta_{p}(r)\phi_{p}[{\rm Piv}_{r,e}(\mathscr{D})].

where the first inequality is based on the spatial Markov property, the fact that
\mathscr{P}_{s}
is measurable in the edges outside
\Lambda_{2^{s-1}}

and the inclusion
\mathscr{P}_{s}\subset{\rm Piv}_{2^{s},e}(\mathscr{D})
.
The second is a simple reindexing of the sum that uses (
4.2
)
and the monotonicity in
r
of the events
{\rm Piv}_{r,e}(\mathscr{D})
.
To obtain the upper bound in (
5.3
), it suffices to notice that
{\rm Piv}_{r,e}(\mathscr{D})
may not occur for
r\geq 3R
.

\square

Remark 5.5

The upper bounds in (
5.6
) and (
5.3
)
may be shown to apply to any event
H
which depends only on the edges in
\Lambda_{R}
.
Indeed, for
H
increasing the proof above applies mutatis mutandis.
When
H
is not increasing, some additional care needs to be taken when bounding

|\phi[H\,|\,\omega_{e}=1]-\phi[H\,|\,\omega_{e}=0]|
in (
5.1
) and (
5.11
).
Notice, however, that in both equations one may produce a coupling of
\phi[\cdot\,|\,\omega_{e}=0]
and
\phi[\cdot\,|\,\omega_{e}=1]

that produces equal configurations inside
\Lambda_{R}
with probability
1-O(\Delta_{p}(R))
and
1-O(\Delta_{p}(R,n))
, respectively.
As a consequence, the terms on the right-hand side in (
5.6
) and (
5.3
)
bound from above the distance in total variation between the restrictions of

\phi[\cdot\,|\,\omega_{e}=1]
and
\phi[\cdot\,|\,\omega_{e}=0]
to
\Lambda_{R}
.

Proof of Proposition
5.1

We focus here on the expression for the crossing probability of a quad;
the proof for the derivatives of probabilities of arm events is identical.

Fix
p
and some
\eta
-regular quad
(\mathscr{D},a,b,c,d)
at scale
R\leq L(p)
.
By grouping the contributions of different edges depending on their distance to the origin and the boundary of
\Lambda_{R}
,
and using Lemma
5.3
we have

\displaystyle\tfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}p}\phi_{p}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p(1-p)}\sum_{e}\mathrm{Cov}_{p}(\omega_{e},\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D}))\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!R^{2}\Delta_{p}(R)+\sum_{n=R}^{L(p)}n\Delta_{p}(n)\Delta_{p}(R,n),

since there are order
R^{2}
edges in the case (
5.3
) and order
n
edges at distance
n
from
\partial\mathscr{D}
.

For the upper bound we use the three upper-bounds of Lemma
5.3
.
We split the edges into three categories:
those at a distance less than
2R
from
\partial\mathscr{D}
,
those at a distance between
2R
and
L(p)
from
\partial\mathscr{D}
,
and those at a distance larger than
L(p)
from
\partial\mathscr{D}
.

For the two last categories, keeping in mind that there are
O(n)
edges at a distance exactly
n\geq 2R
from
\partial\mathscr{D}
,
and applying (
5.6
) and (
5.3
), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{e:\,{\rm dist}(e,\partial\mathscr{D})\geq 2R}\!\!\!\!\!\!\mathrm{Cov}_{p}(\omega_{e},\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D}))

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\sum_{n=2R}^{2L(p)}n\Delta_{p}(n)\Delta_{p}(R,n)+\sum_{n\geq 2L(p)}n\Delta_{p}(L(p))\Delta_{p}(R,L(p))e^{-cn/L(p)}

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!R^{2}\Delta_{p}(R)+\sum_{n=R}^{L(p)}n\Delta_{p}(n)\Delta_{p}(R,n).

Indeed, the second sum of the middle term may be ignored due to the exponential factor.
The first term in the second line is only important in the particular situation when
R
is very close to
L(p)
.

We turn to the edges in the first category: those close to
\partial\mathscr{D}
. We refer to Figure
11
for an illustration.
Fix
n\leq 2R
. We start by studying the contribution to the derivative of the edges at a distance
n
of the arc
(ab)
(the cases of other arcs
(bc)
,
(cd)
and
(da)
),
and we precisely focus on the term in the right-hand side of (
5.3
) for these edges and corresponding
to some fixed
n/2\leq r\leq 3R
.
Consider a family of vertices
a=x_{0},\dots,x_{k}
found counterclockwise along
(ab)
at a
\ell^{\infty}
distance
r
from each other, with the last vertex
x_{k}
at distance at most
r
of
b
(when
r
is large
k
is equal to 0). Consider the event
E_{i}
that
(x_{i-4}x_{i-3})
is dual connected to
(da)
and
(x_{i+3}x_{i+4})
is connected to
(cd)
, with the convention that if
i\leq 4/\eta
or
i\geq k-4/\eta
then
E_{i}
is the full event. We claim that, for every
e\subset\Lambda_{r}(x_{i})
,

\phi_{p}[{\rm Piv}_{r,e}(\mathscr{D})]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\phi_{p}[E_{i}].

Indeed, the inequality is trivial when
i\leq 4/\eta
or
i\geq k-4/\eta
since
E
is the full event.
For the remaining values of
i
(which exist only when
r\ll\eta R
),
observe that for
{\rm Piv}_{r,e}(\mathscr{D})
to occur,
\Lambda_{2r}(x_{i})
needs to be connected by a primal path to
(cd)

and by dual paths to
(bc)
and
(da)
.
In particular, the interface starting from
d
and delimiting the primal cluster of
(cd)
in
\mathscr{D}

necessarily hits
\Lambda_{2r}(x_{i})
before hitting the arc
(ab)
.
Let
\Gamma
be the section of this interface from
d
up to the first time it hits
\Lambda_{2r}(x_{i})
.
Conditionally on
\Gamma
, one may use Theorem
2.1
to construct a dual path connecting
\Gamma
to
(x_{i-4}x_{i-3})

and a primal path connecting
\Gamma
to
(x_{i+3}x_{i+4})
with probability uniformly bounded away from zero.
These paths, together with
\Gamma
, induce the connections required by
E_{i}
.

Notice now that any edge at distance
n
from
(ab)
is contained in at least one
\Lambda_{r}(x_{i})
.
Conversely there are
O(r)
vertices in each
\Lambda_{r}(x_{i})
at a distance exactly
n
from
(ab)
.
Thus, summing over the edges
e
at a distance
n
from
(ab)
gives

\sum_{e:{\rm dist}(e,(ab))=n}\phi_{p}[{\rm Piv}_{r,e}(\mathscr{D})]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!r\sum_{i=0}^{k}\phi_{p}[E_{i}]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!r,

where the second inequality is due to the fact that at most
O(1)
events
E_{i}
can occur simultaneously.

One may do the same with edges at a distance
n
of
(bc)
,
(cd)
and
(da)
.
Finally, summing the previous displayed equation over
r
and using (
5.3
), we find

\displaystyle\sum_{e:{\rm dist}(e,\partial\mathscr{D})\leq 2R}\mathrm{Cov}_{p}(\omega_{e},\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D}))

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\sum_{n=1}^{2R}\sum_{r=n/2}^{3R}\tfrac{1}{r}\Delta_{p}(r)\sum_{e:{\rm dist}(e,\partial\mathscr{D})=n}\phi_{p}[{\rm Piv}_{r,e}(\mathscr{D})]

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\sum_{n=1}^{2R}\sum_{r=n/2}^{3R}\Delta_{p}(r)\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\sum_{r=1}^{R}r\Delta_{p}(r).

The above combined with (
5.1
) implies the upper bound in (
5.1
).

\square

Figure 11:
A depiction of the points
x_{0},\dots,x_{k}
(note that
x_{k}
is not necessarily equal to
b
).
The occurrence of
{\rm Piv}_{r,e}(\mathscr{D})
for some
e\in\Lambda_{r}(x_{i})
induces the existence of the exploration path
\Gamma
(in red).
When orienting
\Gamma
in the direction of its exploration, that is from
d
to
\Lambda_{2r}(x_{i})
,

\Gamma
has dual open edges on its right and primal ones on its left.
Thus, conditionally on
\Gamma
, the blue paths occur with uniformly positive probability, and induce the occurrence of
E_{i}
.

5.2
Derivative for the mixing rate

Proposition 5.6

For every
p
and two edges
e
and
f
at a distance
R\leq L(p)
of each other,

\Big|\tfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}p}\log\mathrm{Cov}_{p}(\omega_{e},\omega_{f})\big|\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\sum_{\ell=1}^{R}\ell\Delta_{p}(\ell)+\sum_{\ell=R}^{L(p)}\ell\Delta_{p}(\ell)\Delta_{p}(R,\ell).

(5.18)

Exactly as for crossing events, one may use the next section to replace
\sum_{\ell\leq R}\ell\Delta_{p}(\ell)
by
R^{2}\Delta_{p}(R)
; see Corollary
7.1
of Section
7
.

Remark 5.7

Since
\mathrm{Cov}_{p}(\omega_{e},\omega_{f})
was shown in (
5.6
)
to be comparable to
\Delta_{p}(R)^{2}
, the above should be understood as a bound on the logarithmic derivative of
\Delta_{p}(R)
.
Indeed, (
5.18
) combined with (
5.6
) yields

\displaystyle\Big|\log\frac{\Delta_{p}(R)}{\Delta_{p_{c}}(R)}\Big|\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\int_{p_{c}}^{p}\Big(\sum_{\ell=1}^{R}\ell\Delta_{u}(\ell)+\sum_{\ell=R}^{L(u)}\ell\Delta_{u}(\ell)\Delta_{u}(R,\ell)\Big)\,du,

for all
p
and
R\leq L(p)
.
This inequality will be useful when
R
is of the same order as
L(p)
,
in which case the terms with
R\leq\ell\leq L(p)

may be absorbed by the first sum on the right-hand side.

Proof

Fix
p
,
e
and
f
as in the statement. Then

\displaystyle\tfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}p}\mathrm{Cov}_{p}(\omega_{e},\omega_{f})

\displaystyle=\sum_{g\in\mathbb{E}}\phi_{p}[\omega_{e}\omega_{f}\omega_{g}]-\phi_{p}[\omega_{e}\omega_{f}]\phi_{p}[\omega_{g}]-\phi_{p}[\omega_{f}\omega_{g}]\phi_{p}[\omega_{e}]

(5.21)

\displaystyle\qquad-\phi_{p}[\omega_{e}\omega_{g}]\phi_{p}[\omega_{f}]+2\phi_{p}[\omega_{e}]\phi_{p}[\omega_{f}]\phi_{p}[\omega_{g}].

We will write these terms differently, depending on the position of
g
.
First, consider an edge
g\in\Lambda_{R/2}(e)
; the corresponding term may be written as

\displaystyle\phi_{p}[\omega_{f}]\Big(\phi_{p}[\omega_{e}\omega_{g}|\omega_{f}]-\phi_{p}[\omega_{g}|\omega_{f}]\phi_{p}[\omega_{e}]-\phi_{p}[\omega_{e}|\omega_{f}]\phi_{p}[\omega_{g}]-\phi_{p}[\omega_{e}\omega_{g}]+2\phi_{p}[\omega_{e}]\phi_{p}[\omega_{g}]\Big).

(5.22)

Write
\ell
for the distance between
e
and
g
and set
G:=\Lambda_{3\ell/2}(e)
. For any event
A
depending only on the edges in
G
, we have

\displaystyle\phi_{p}[A]=\sum_{\xi}\phi_{p}[B_{\xi}]\phi_{G,p}^{\xi}[A]\quad\text{ and }\quad\phi_{p}[A\,|\,\omega_{f}]=\sum_{\xi}\phi_{p}[B_{\xi}\,|\,\omega_{f}]\phi_{G,p}^{\xi}[A],

where the sum is over all boundary conditions
\xi
imposed on
G
by the configuration outside of
G
, and
B_{\xi}
is the event that the boundary conditions induced on
G
are
\xi
. Thus, (
5.22
) may be written as

\displaystyle\phi_{p}[\omega_{f}]\sum_{\xi}(\phi_{p}[B_{\xi}\,|\,\omega_{f}]-\phi_{p}[B_{\xi}])(\underbrace{\phi_{G,p}^{\xi}[\omega_{e}\omega_{g}]-\phi_{G,p}^{\xi}[\omega_{g}]\phi_{p}[\omega_{e}]-\phi_{G,p}^{\xi}[\omega_{e}]\phi_{p}[\omega_{g}]+\phi_{p}[\omega_{e}]\phi_{p}[\omega_{g}]}_{F(\xi)}).

By adding and subtracting
\phi_{G,p}^{\xi}[\omega_{e}]\phi_{G,p}^{\xi}[\omega_{g}]
, we find

\displaystyle F(\xi)=\mathrm{Cov}_{G,p}^{\xi}(\omega_{e},\omega_{g})+(\phi_{G,p}^{\xi}[\omega_{g}]-\phi_{p}[\omega_{g}])(\phi_{G,p}^{\xi}[\omega_{g}]-\phi_{p}[\omega_{g}]).

where
\mathrm{Cov}_{G}^{\xi}
stands for the covariance under the measure
\phi_{G,p}^{\xi}
.
Next, we apply Lemma
5.3
to
\phi_{G,p}^{\xi}
instead of
\phi_{p}

– notice that the choice of
G
ensures that both
e
and
g
are at a distance of order
\ell
from
\partial G
,
and the lemma does indeed apply as explained in Remark
5.4
– to obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}_{G}^{\xi}(\omega_{e},\omega_{g})

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\Delta_{p}(\ell)^{2}.

In addition, for any
\xi
, Theorem
4.8
yields

\displaystyle\phi_{G,p}^{\xi}[\omega_{g}]-\phi_{p}[\omega_{g}]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\Delta_{p}(\ell)\quad\text{ and }\quad\phi_{G,p}^{\xi}[\omega_{e}]-\phi_{p}[\omega_{e}]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\Delta_{p}(\ell).

Then, (
5.22
) may be bounded above by

\displaystyle\sum_{\xi}\|F\|_{\infty}\cdot|\phi_{p}[B_{\xi}|\omega_{f}]-\phi_{p}[B_{\xi}]|\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\Delta_{p}(R)\Delta_{p}(\ell,R)\Delta_{p}(\ell)^{2}\asymp\Delta_{p}(\ell)\Delta_{p}(R)^{2},

since the total variation distance between the restrictions of
\phi_{p}[\cdot\,|\omega_{f}]
and
\phi_{p}
to
G

is bounded by a universal multiple of
\Delta_{p}(R)\Delta_{p}(\ell,R)
, as shown in Lemma
5.3
(see also Remark
5.5
).
The second equivalence is given by the quasi-multiplicativity of
\Delta_{p}
(
4.12
).

Edges
g\in\Lambda_{R/2}(f)
have the same contribution to (
5.22
) due to symmetry.
Finally, we consider edges
g
that are outside
\Lambda_{R/2}(e)\cup\Lambda_{R/2}(f)
; write
r
for the distance between
g
and
e
.
Let
G
be the domain formed of the edges at a distance at least
R/4
from the segment uniting
e
and
f
;
notice that
g
is at a distance at least
R/4
from
G
.
Applying the same argument as in the first case, with the roles of
\omega_{f}
and
\omega_{g}
inverted, we find
that the term corresponding to
g
in (
5.21
) is

\displaystyle\phi_{p}[\omega_{g}]\sum_{\xi}\big(\phi_{p}[\xi\,|\,\omega_{g}]-\phi_{p}[\xi]\big)\big(\mathrm{Cov}_{G,p}^{\xi}(\omega_{e},\omega_{f})+(\phi_{G,p}^{\xi}[\omega_{e}]-\phi_{p}[\omega_{e}])(\phi_{G,p}^{\xi}[\omega_{f}]-\phi_{p}[\omega_{f}])\big)

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\Delta_{p}(\ell\wedge L(p))\Delta_{p}(R,\ell\wedge L(p))e^{-cr/L(p)}\Delta_{p}(R)^{2}

\displaystyle\asymp\Delta_{p}(R)\Delta_{p}(\ell\wedge L(p))^{2}e^{-cr/L(p)},

(5.27)

where the sum in the left-hand side is over all the boundary conditions
\xi
on
G
imposed by the configuration outside of
G
.
Indeed, by Lemma
5.3
, the total variation distance between the restrictions of
\phi[\cdot\,|\,\omega_{g}]
and
\phi
to
G

is bounded by a universal multiple of
\Delta_{p}(\ell\wedge L(p))\Delta_{p}(R,\ell\wedge L(p))e^{-cr/L(p)}
,
while each term in the second parentheses of the left-hand side is bounded by multiples of
\Delta_{p}(R)^{2}
.

Summing now over
g
and using (
5.2
) and (
5.27
), we find

\displaystyle\tfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}p}\mathrm{Cov}_{p}(\omega_{e},\omega_{f})

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\sum_{\ell=1}^{R/2}\ell\Delta_{p}(\ell)\Delta_{p}(R)^{2}+\sum_{\ell>R/2}\ell\Delta_{p}(R)\Delta_{p}(\ell\wedge L(p))^{2}e^{-cr/L(p)}

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\Delta_{p}(R)^{2}\Big(\sum_{\ell=1}^{R}\ell\Delta_{p}(\ell)+\sum_{\ell=R}^{L(p)}\ell\Delta_{p}(R,\ell)\Delta_{p}(\ell)\Big).

In the second inequality, we use the exponential term to eliminate all terms with
\ell>L(p)
and (
4.17
) and (
4.2
)
to adjust the range of
\ell
in the sum.
Finally, divide by
\mathrm{Cov}_{p}(\omega_{e},\omega_{f})\asymp\Delta_{p}(R)^{2}
to obtain the desired result.

\square

6
Lower bound on
\Delta_{p}(r,R)
: proof of Proposition
1.8

In this section, we prove Proposition
1.8
. The section will be divided in two as the case
1\leq q<4
is quite different from the case
q=4
.

6.1
Lower bound on
\Delta_{p}(r,R)
for
1\leq q<4

In this section, we assume that
1\leq q<4
. We will prove the following stronger statement.

Proposition 6.1

Fix
1\leq q<4
. There exists
\delta=\delta(q)>0
such that for
p\in(0,1)
and
r\leq R\leq L(p)
,

\pi_{4}(p,r,R)\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!(r/R)^{2-\delta}.

(6.1)

This implies (
1.21
) since
\Delta_{p}(r,R)\geq\pi_{4}(p,r,R)
by the observation at the beginning of Section
4.5

(one does not even require the polynomial improvement (
1.18
)).
Let us mention that (
6.1
) is expected to fail for
q=4
, which explains why the case
q=4
needs to be treated separately.

Proof

By symmetry, we only need to treat the case
p\geq p_{c}
. The case
p=p_{c}
was obtained in a recent paper
[
DMT20
, Prop. 6.8]
. The argument extends readily to our setting.

Indeed, if
E
denotes the event that
\Lambda_{3R}
contains both an open circuit and
a dual open circuit surrounding
\Lambda_{2R}
, with the open one being connected to
\partial\Lambda_{4R}
,
then it is shown in
[
DMT20
]
that

\pi_{4}(p,r,R)\geq\phi_{p}[E]\inf_{\mathscr{D}}\phi_{\mathscr{D},p}[\mathbf{M}_{r}(\mathscr{D},R)],

where the infimum is taken over all simply connected domains containing
\Lambda_{2R}
and contained in
\Lambda_{3n}

and
\mathbf{M}_{r}(\mathscr{D},R)
is an increasing random variable defined in
[
DMT20
, Sec. 1.5]
.
When
R\leq L(p)
, Theorem
2.1
implies that
\phi_{p}[E]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!1
.
Moreover, since
\mathbf{M}_{r}(\mathscr{D},R)
is increasing,

\displaystyle\inf_{\mathscr{D}}\phi_{\mathscr{D},p}[\mathbf{M}_{r}(\mathscr{D},R)]\geq\inf_{\mathscr{D}}\phi_{\mathscr{D},p_{c}}[\mathbf{M}_{r}(\mathscr{D},R)]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!(R/r)^{\delta_{0}},

for some universal
\delta_{0}>0
,
where the last inequality is given by
[
DMT20
, Prop. 1.4]
. Combining the above displays produces the desired bound.

\square

Remark 6.2

The proof looks simple but we wish to insist that the whole difficulty of the argument is in
[
DMT20
]
.

6.2
Lower bound on
\Delta_{p}(r,R)
for
q=4

The reasoning of the previous section does not apply for
q=4
, as it is expected that

\pi_{4}(p,r,R)\asymp(\tfrac{r}{R})^{2}

(see Remark
6.11
). Nevertheless, this is not contradictory with the fact that
\Delta_{p}(r,R)\geq\delta(\tfrac{r}{R})^{2-\delta}
as we know from Section
4.5
that
\Delta_{p}(r,R)
is polynomially larger than
\pi_{4}(p,r,R)
.
Since we do not currently know how to prove that
\pi_{4}(p,r,R)\asymp(\tfrac{r}{R})^{2}
,
we adopt a direct approach to prove Proposition
1.8
, that does not involve the comparison to
\pi_{4}(p,r,R)
.

Proof of (
1.21
)

The lower bound on
\Delta_{p}(r,R)
follows directly from the combination of the next two propositions, which respectively correspond to the required bound at
p_{c}
and the stability of
\Delta_{p}
below the characteristic length.

\square

Proposition 6.3
(Lower bound at
p_{c}
)

For
q=4
, there exists
\delta_{0}>0
such that for every
R\geq 2r>0
,

\Delta_{p_{c}}(r,R)\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\pi_{2}(r,R)(r/R)\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!(r/R)^{2-\delta_{0}}.

(6.2)

Proposition 6.4
(Stability of
\Delta
)

For
q=4
, every
p
and
2\leq r\leq R\leq L(p)
,

\Delta_{p}(r,R)\asymp\Delta_{p_{c}}(r,R).

(6.3)

The section is divided as follows.
The proof makes use of the parafermionic observable introduced by Smirnov
[
Smi10
]
, we therefore start by recalling this notion.
Then, we prove each proposition in a separate section, in the order in which they are stated.

6.2.1
Parafermionic observable: a crash-course

Since the parafermionic observable is used only in this section, we give minimal details and refer to the literature, for instance the review article
[
Dum17
]
and the research paper
[
DMT20
]
(which is implementing a reasoning very similar to the present one, with similar notations). We also recommend that the reader take a look at Figures
12
.

Parafermionic observables are usually defined in Dobrushin domains,
but in the present study we limit ourselves to situations where the wired arc is reduced to a single point,
and therefore the domain has free boundary conditions. We do, however, authorise the domains to be non-simply-connected.

Fix a finite connected subgraph
\Omega
of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and a vertex
x\in\Omega
with a neighbour
x^{\prime}
in the infinite connected component of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\Omega
.
Recall that
\Omega^{\diamond}
is spanned by the edges of
(\mathbb{Z}^{2})^{\diamond}
bordering the faces of
(\mathbb{Z}^{2})^{\diamond}
that contain vertices of
\Omega
. As such the vertices of
\Omega^{\diamond}
have either degree
2
or
4
in
\Omega^{\diamond}
(here one should be careful when looking at a vertex of
\Omega^{\diamond}
that is corresponding to an edge outside of
\Omega
with both endpoints in
\Omega
: in this case we think of this vertex as being split into two “prime ends” of degree 2 in
\Omega^{\diamond}
, but this case does not occur for domains considered in this paper).
Let
e_{x}
be the first edge of
\Omega^{\diamond}
bordering the face of
(\mathbb{Z}^{2})^{\diamond}
containing
x
,
when going around said face in clockwise order, starting from
xx^{\prime}
.

For a configuration
\omega
on
\Omega
, let
\overline{\omega}
be the loop configuration on
\Omega^{\diamond}
associated to
\omega
.
In the loop configuration, the loop passing through
e_{x}
is called
exploration path
;
it is denoted by
\gamma=\gamma(\omega)
and is oriented counterclockwise, so as to have primal open edges on its left and dual-open ones on its right.
For an edge
e\in\gamma
, let
\text{W}_{\gamma}(e,e_{x})
be the winding of
\gamma
between
e
and
e_{x}
,
that is the number of left turns minus the number of right turns taken by
\gamma
when going from
e
to
e_{x}
multiplied by
\pi/2
.

Definition 6.5

For
(\Omega,x)
as above, the parafermionic observable
F=F_{\Omega,x}
is defined for any (medial) edge
e
of
\Omega^{\diamond}
by

F(e)\penalty\ :=\penalty\ \phi^{0}_{\Omega,p_{c},4}[\mathrm{W}_{\gamma}(e,e_{x}){\rm e}^{{\rm i}\mathrm{W}_{\gamma}(e,e_{x})}\mathbbm{1}_{e\in\gamma}].

The parafermionic observable satisfies a very special property first observed in
[
Smi10
]
(see also
[
Dum17
, Thm. 5.16]
for a statement with a similar notation).
For every vertex of
\Omega^{\diamond}
with four incident edges in
\Omega^{\diamond}
,

\sum_{i=1}^{4}\eta(e_{i})F(e_{i})=F(e_{1})-{\rm i}F(e_{2})-F(e_{3})+{\rm i}F(e_{4})=0,

(6.4)

where
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
,
e_{3}
and
e_{4}
are the four edges incident to
v
, indexed in clockwise order,
and
\eta(e_{i})
is the complex number of norm one with same direction as
e_{i}
and orientation from
v
towards the other endpoint of
e_{i}
.
Summing this relation over all vertices of
\Omega^{\diamond}
of degree four, we obtain that

\sum_{e\in\mathscr{C}}\eta(e)F(e)=0,

(6.5)

where
\mathscr{C}
is the set of medial-edges of
\Omega^{\diamond}
having exactly one endpoint of degree two in
\Omega^{\diamond}
,
and
\eta(e)
is the complex number of modulus one, collinear with the edge
e
and oriented towards the outside of
\Omega
.

Figure 12:
The primal and dual graphs in plain and dashed black lines. In bold (plain and dashed respectively), the configurations
\omega
and
\omega^{*}
. The graph
\Omega^{\diamond}
is in red and the configuration
\overline{\omega}
in blue. The path
\gamma(\omega)
is in bold blue. We also presented two examples of edges
e
and
e^{\prime}
