, one for which the corresponding vertex of the primal graph has degree three, and one for which it has degree two.

Remark 6.6

This relation should be understood as stating that
the contour integral of the parafermionic observable along the boundary of
\Omega^{\diamond}
is
0
.
While it is common to use the above in simply connected domains, we insist that (
6.5
) is valid for any
\Omega
as above (it is important that
x
is on the exterior face).

For random-cluster models with general values of
q
between 1 and 4, and the loop
O(n)
models, a similar property was used to obtain estimates for the two-point functions, see e.g.
[
DS12
,
DST17
,
DGPS17
,
DMT20
]
. Here, we propose a new use of this property.

For
q=4
, the complex phase of the observable (that is
{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\mathrm{W}_{\gamma}(e,e_{x})}
) is invariant under addition of factors
2\pi
to the winding. Therefore, it is (almost) determined by the orientation of
e
.
Indeed, for any oriented edge
e
and any
\omega
such that
\gamma
passes through
e

\displaystyle\eta(e){\rm e}^{{\rm i}\mathrm{W}_{\gamma}(e,e_{x})}=\begin{cases}+1&\text{ if\penalty\ $\gamma$ is oriented like\penalty\ $e$},\\
-1&\text{ if\penalty\ $\gamma$ is oriented opposite to\penalty\ $e$}.\end{cases}

(6.6)

Moreover, for
y\in\partial\Omega

(exclude from this explanation the situation where
y
has exactly two opposite neighbours in
\Omega
),
there exist two edges
e,e^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}
bordering the face of
(\mathbb{Z}^{2})^{\diamond}
containing
y

such that
\gamma
passes through
e
if and only if it passes through
e^{\prime}
, see Figure
12
.
When this happens and
y
is not equal to
x
,
e
and
e^{\prime}
may be chosen such that
\gamma
always passes first through
e
towards the exterior of
\Omega
, then through
e^{\prime}
, towards the interior.
Finally,
\gamma
performs
4-d_{y}
positive
\pi/2
turns
between the passage through
e
and
e^{\prime}
, where
d_{y}
is the degree of
y
inside
\Omega
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}_{\gamma}(e,e_{x})=\mathrm{W}_{\gamma}(e^{\prime},e_{x})+(4-d_{y})\pi/2.

(6.7)

When
y=x
, the two contributions to (
6.5
) of
e
and
e^{\prime}
are
0
and
3\pi/2
, respectively.
Inserting (
6.6
) and (
6.7
) into (
6.5
) yields

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in\partial\Omega\\
y\neq x\end{subarray}}(4-d_{y})\phi_{\Omega}^{0}[A(x,y)]=\tfrac{3\pi}{2},

(6.8)

where
A(x,y)
is the event that
\gamma
passes between
y
and its neighbour outside
\Omega
.
When
y
has a neighbour in the infinite component of
\Omega^{c}
, then
A(x,y)=\{x\longleftrightarrow y\}
,
but since we do not ask
\Omega
to be simply connected, this is not always the case for other
y
.

A more sophisticated analysis yields (
6.8
)
also when some
y\in\mathscr{C}
have exactly two opposite neighbours in
\Omega
.

6.2.2
Lower bound on
\Delta_{p_{c}}(r,R)
at
p_{c}
: proof of Proposition
6.3

The second inequality follows from (
2.2
), we therefore focus on the first one.
By subtracting (
6.8
) for
\Omega=\Lambda_{R}
and
\Omega={\rm Ann}(r,R)
with
x=(0,R)
,
we find

\sum_{y\in\partial\Lambda_{R}}(4-d_{y})\big[\phi_{\Lambda_{R}}^{0}[A(x,y)]-\phi_{{\rm Ann}(r,R)}^{0}[A(x,y)]\big]=\sum_{y\in\partial\Lambda_{r}}(4-d_{y})\phi_{{\rm Ann}(r,R)}^{0}[A(x,y)].

(6.9)

We will now estimate the terms on the left and right-hand sides of the above.

Remark 6.7

It is reasonable to expect that the left-hand side is of order
R\pi_{1}^{+}(R)^{2}\Delta_{p_{c}}(r,R)
,
while the right one of order
r\pi_{1}^{+}(R)\pi_{1}^{+}(r)\pi_{2}(r,R)
, which would produce the desired result. Nevertheless, we are not able to prove this due to boundary terms near corners of the inner box, which we cannot control.
Instead, we will use a more sophisticated strategy.
Let us mention that the conjectural scaling limit of the model suggests that
\pi_{1}^{+}(r,R)\asymp(r/R)
and
\pi_{2}(r,R)\asymp\sqrt{r/R}
, which would imply that
\Delta_{p_{c}}(r,R)\asymp\sqrt{r/R}
.

First, on the right-hand side, the event
A(x,y)
occurs if and only if
x
and
y
are connected in
\omega
and
\Lambda_{r}
and
\Lambda_{R}
are connected by a path in
\omega^{*}
. Quasi-multiplicativity implies that

\displaystyle\sum_{y\in\partial\Lambda_{r}}(4-d_{y})\phi_{{\rm Ann}(r,R)}^{0}[A(x,y)]\asymp\pi_{1}^{+}(R)\pi_{2}(r,R)\sum_{k\leq r/2}\phi_{\mathbb{U}}^{0}[(k,0)\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{r}],

(6.10)

where
\mathbb{U}:=\{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:x_{1}\leq 0\text{ or }x_{2}\leq 0\}
is the lower-left three-quarter plane.

On the left-hand side, for
y\in\partial\Lambda_{R}
,
A(x,y)
corresponds to the event that
x
and
y
are connected.
Also, the measure
\phi_{{\rm Ann}(r,R)}^{0}
may be viewed as the measure in
\Lambda_{R}
conditioned on the event
\{\Lambda_{r}\equiv 0\}
that every edge with one endpoint in
\Lambda_{r-1}
is closed.
The previous study of
\Delta_{p_{c}}(r,R)
thus implies that for any
r\leq R/2
,

\displaystyle\phi_{\Lambda_{R}}^{0}[A(x,y)]-\phi_{\Lambda_{R}}^{0}[A(x,y)\,|\,\Lambda_{r}\equiv 0]\asymp\Delta_{p_{c}}(r,R)\big[\phi_{\Lambda_{R}}^{0}[A(x,y)]-\phi_{\Lambda_{R}}^{0}[A(x,y)\,|\,\Lambda_{R/2}\equiv 0]\big].

We deduce that

\sum_{y\in\partial\Lambda_{R}}(4-d_{y})\big[\phi_{\Lambda_{R}}^{0}[A(x,y)]-\phi_{{\rm Ann}(r,R)}^{0}[A(x,y)]\big]\asymp\Delta_{p_{c}}(r,R)\Sigma_{R},

(6.11)

where

\Sigma_{R}:=\sum_{y\in\partial\Lambda_{R}}(4-d_{y})\big[\phi_{\Lambda_{R}}^{0}[A(x,y)]-\phi_{\Lambda_{R}}^{0}[A(x,y)\,|\,\Lambda_{R/2}\equiv 0]\big]

(6.12)

is a constant that depends on
R
only.
Inserting (
6.10
) and (
6.11
) into (
6.9
) gives

\displaystyle\Delta_{p_{c}}(r,R)\Sigma_{R}\asymp\pi_{1}^{+}(R)\pi_{2}(r,R)\sum_{k\leq r/2}\phi_{\mathbb{U}}^{0}[(k,0)\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{r}],

for any
r\leq R/2
. Divide the relation above for an arbitrary
r\leq R/2
with the same relation for
r=R/2
(we use that
\Delta_{p_{c}}(r/2,R)\asymp\Delta_{p_{c}}(r,R)
) to find

\Delta_{p_{c}}(r,R)\asymp\pi_{2}(r,R)\,\frac{\sum_{k\leq r/2}\phi_{\mathbb{U}}^{0}[(k,0)\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{r}]}{\sum_{k\leq R/4}\phi_{\mathbb{U}}^{0}[(k,0)\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{R}]}\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\pi_{2}(r,R)(r/R).

(6.13)

The inequality above is obtained by summing over
j=0,\dots,R/r
and
k=1,\dots,r/2
the inequality below :

\phi_{\mathbb{U}}^{0}[(k+rj/2,0)\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{R}]\leq\phi_{\mathbb{U}}^{0}[(k,0)\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{r}],

which is a direct consequence of the comparison between boundary conditions (
CBC
).

Remark 6.8

Applying (
6.13
) with
r=1
, the comparison between boundary conditions (
CBC
) and (
2.2
) imply that

\pi_{1}(R)\Delta_{p_{c}}(R)\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\frac{\pi_{1}(R)\pi_{2}(R)}{\sum_{k\leq R/4}\phi_{\mathbb{U}}^{0}[(k,0)\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{R}]}\geq\frac{\pi_{1}(R)\pi_{2}(R)}{\tfrac{1}{4}R\pi_{1}(3R/4)}\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\pi_{2}(R)/R\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!R^{c-2}.

6.2.3
Stability of
\Delta_{p}(r,R)
: proof of Proposition
6.4

The proof is based on the following quantity. Call a subgraph
\Omega
of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
an
topological
R
-annulus
if it is of the form
\Lambda_{R}\setminus H
with
H

a simply connected domain such that
\Lambda_{R/8}\subset H\subset\Lambda_{R/4}
; see Figure
13
.
The boundary of
\Omega
is split into
\partial\Lambda_{R}
and
\partial_{\rm in}\Omega:=\partial\Omega\setminus\partial\Lambda_{R}
.
Set

\mathbf{N}_{\Omega}(p):=\sum_{y\in\partial_{\rm in}\Omega}\phi_{\Omega,p}^{0}[y\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{R/2}].

The first observation is the following lemma stating that for
q=4
the quantity
\mathbf{N}_{\Omega}(p)
does not depend on the choice of the
R
-annulus
\Omega
, or on the choice of
p
such that
R\leq L(p)
.

Lemma 6.9

For every
p\geq p_{c}
and
R\leq L(p)
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\Omega}\mathbf{N}_{\Omega}(p^{*})\asymp\sup_{\Omega}\mathbf{N}_{\Omega}(p_{c})\asymp\inf_{\Omega}\mathbf{N}_{\Omega}(p_{c})\asymp\inf_{\Omega}\mathbf{N}_{\Omega}(p),

(6.14)

where the infimum and supremum are taken over topological
R
-annuli.

Figure 13:
In grey the domain
\mathscr{H}
(which is an topological
R
-annulus) carved by the event
F
(in blue). In red, the exploration path
\gamma=\gamma(\omega)
going around the boundary vertex
x
.

Proof

We start by proving that

\displaystyle\sup_{\Omega}\mathbf{N}_{\Omega}(p_{c})\asymp\Sigma_{R}/\pi_{1}^{+}(R)\asymp\inf_{\Omega}\mathbf{N}_{\Omega}(p_{c}),

(6.15)

where
\Sigma_{R}
was introduced in (
6.12
) in the previous section.

Fix
\Omega
an topological
R
-annulus and set
x:=(0,R)
.
Then, due to (
2.1
), for every
y\in\partial_{\rm in}\Omega
,

\displaystyle\phi_{\Omega,p_{c}}^{0}[y\longleftrightarrow x\text{ and }\partial_{\rm in}\Omega\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\longleftrightarrow}}\partial\Lambda_{R}]\asymp\pi_{1}^{+}(R)\phi_{\Omega,p_{c}}^{0}[y\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{R/2}].

In particular, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{N}_{\Omega}(p_{c})\asymp\frac{1}{\pi_{1}^{+}(R)}\sum_{y\in\partial_{\rm in}\Omega}\phi_{\Omega,p_{c}}^{0}[A(x,y)].

(6.16)

Subtracting (
6.8
) for the domains
\Lambda_{R}
and
\Omega
,
and following the proof of Proposition
6.3
, we find that

\sum_{y\in\partial_{\rm in}\Omega}\phi_{\Omega,p_{c}}^{0}[A(x,y)]\asymp\Sigma_{R},

which concludes the proof of (
6.15
).

We now prove that for every
p
and
R\leq L(p)
,

\inf_{\Omega}\mathbf{N}_{\Omega}(p)\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!R^{2}\pi_{4}(p,R)\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\sup_{\Omega}\mathbf{N}_{\Omega}(p),

(6.17)

where the infimum and supremum are taken over topological
R
-annuli. Note that this inequality implies the result. Indeed,
since
\mathbf{N}_{\Omega}(p)
is increasing in
p
and
\pi_{4}(p^{*},R)=\pi_{4}(p,R)
(by duality), the previous inequality implies that for
p\geq p_{c}
and
R\leq L(p)=L(p^{*})
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\Omega}\mathbf{N}_{\Omega}(p_{c})\geq\sup_{\Omega}\mathbf{N}_{\Omega}(p^{*})\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!R^{2}\pi_{4}(p^{*},R)=R^{2}\pi_{4}(p,R)\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\inf_{\Omega}\mathbf{N}_{\Omega}(p)\geq\inf_{\Omega}\mathbf{N}_{\Omega}(p_{c}),

which combines with (
6.15
) to give the result.
We now focus on the proof of (
6.17
).
We proceed similarly to
[
DMT20
, Prop 6.8]
, but from inside out.

Let
F
be the event that in
{\rm Ann}(R/8,R/4)
there exists an open circuit surrounding
\Lambda_{R/8}
which is connected to
\Lambda_{R/8}
,
as well as a dual-open circuit, which is necessarily outside of the open one; see Figure
13
.
If
F
occurs, let
\mathscr{H}=\mathscr{H}(\omega)
be the graph formed of the union of all open clusters that intersect
\Lambda_{R/8}
,
along with all finite components of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
minus the said union. Then, due to the definition of
F
,

\Lambda_{R/8}\subset\mathscr{H}\subset\Lambda_{R/4}
.
Write
\Omega(\omega)=\Lambda_{R}\setminus\mathscr{H}
for the random topological
R
-annulus formed by removing
\mathscr{H}
.

When the measure
\phi^{0}_{\Lambda_{R},p}
is conditioned on
F\cap\{\Omega(\omega)=\Omega\}
,
its restriction to
\Omega
is
\phi^{0}_{\Omega,p}
.
Notice that if
y\in\partial_{\rm in}\Omega
is connected to
\partial\Lambda_{R/2}
by an open path, then a four arm event to distance
R/8
occurs around
y
. Thus, using the quasi-multiplicativity (
Mix
) and Theorem
2.1
(to bound the probability of
F
from below), we find that

\displaystyle R^{2}\pi_{4}(p,R)\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\phi_{\Lambda_{R},p}[\mathbf{N}_{\Omega(\omega)}(p)\,\mathbbm{1}_{F}]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\inf_{\Omega}\mathbf{N}_{\Omega}(p).

Conversely, the separation of arms for the four-arm event and Theorem
2.1
show that for each
y\in{\rm Ann}(5R/32,7R/32)
,

\displaystyle\phi_{\Lambda_{R},p}[F\cap\{y\in\partial_{\rm in}\Omega(\omega)\}\cap\{y\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{R/2}\}]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\pi_{4}(p,R).

Summing over all
y
we find

\displaystyle R^{2}\pi_{4}(p,R)\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\phi_{\Lambda_{R},p}[\mathbf{N}_{\Omega(\omega)}(p)\mathbbm{1}_{F}]\leq\sup_{\Omega}\mathbf{N}_{\Omega}(p).

\square

Remark 6.10

The previous proof shows as a byproduct that
\pi_{4}(p,R)\asymp\pi_{4}(R)
for every
R\leq L(p)
.
It is somehow surprising that the stability of
\pi_{4}
below the correlation length can be directly extracted from the parafermionic observable.
Recall, however, that this is only valid for
q=4
.

Remark 6.11

The previous proof also implies that
R^{2}\pi_{4}(R)
is of the order of
\mathbf{N}_{\Omega}(p_{c})
for every topological
R
-annulus
\Omega
. In particular, taking
\Omega=\Lambda_{R/8}
gives that

R^{2}\pi_{4}(R)\asymp R\pi_{1}^{+}(R).

(6.18)

The conjectural scaling limit of the model suggests that
\pi_{1}^{+}(R)\asymp R^{-1}
, which would imply that
\pi_{4}(R)\asymp R^{-2}
. We see that in this case the fact that
\Delta_{p_{c}}(R)\gg\pi_{4}(R)
is crucial for the bound (
6.2
).

Figure 14:
By sliding the boxes along a given path one by one from the exterior towards
y
, one finds the first time that the twice larger box
\Lambda_{2\cdot 8^{k}}(z_{k})
disconnects
y
from the boundary. Note that by construction the first time it stops cannot be the quite the same for different
k
. In particular, the box
\Lambda_{8^{k}/2}(z_{k})
being at a distance
8^{k}/2
of the boundary of
\Lambda_{8^{k}}(z_{k})
, it is also at such a distance of
\partial_{\rm in}\Omega
. Since on contrary
\Lambda_{8^{\ell}/2}(z_{\ell})
is within a distance
8^{\ell}\times 3/2
of it, we immediately deduce that the two boxes do not intersect as soon as
\ell<k
.

Proof of Proposition
6.4

We treat the case
p>p_{c}
; the case
p<p_{c}
can be done similarly. Fix
R\leq L(p)
.
The inequality (
5.7
) implies that

\displaystyle\Big|\log\frac{\Delta_{p}(r,R)}{\Delta_{p_{c}}(r,R)}\Big|

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\underbrace{\int_{p_{c}}^{p}\Big(\sum_{\ell=1}^{R}\ell\Delta_{u}(\ell)\Big)du}_{(A)}+\underbrace{\int_{p_{c}}^{p}\Big(\sum_{\ell=R}^{L(u)}\ell\Delta_{u}(\ell)\Delta_{u}(R,\ell)\Big)du}_{(B)}

and we need to prove that
(A)
and
(B)
are bounded by constants that are independent of
R
and
p>p_{c}
.
The bound on
(B)
is easy to obtain since (
5.1
) shows that
(B)\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\phi_{p}[\mathscr{C}(\Lambda_{R})]-\phi_{p_{c}}[\mathscr{C}(\Lambda_{R})]\leq 1
. We therefore focus on
(A)
.

Choose the topological
R
-annulus
\Omega
minimizing
\mathbf{N}_{\Omega}(p)
and observe that by Lemma
6.9
,
\mathbf{N}_{\Omega}(p)\asymp\mathbf{N}_{\Omega}(p_{c})
.
We claim that

\displaystyle\tfrac{d}{du}\log\mathbf{N}_{\Omega}(u)\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\sum_{\ell=1}^{R}\ell\Delta_{u}(\ell).

Observe that (
6.2.3
) implies that

(A)\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\log\mathbf{N}_{\Omega}(p)-\log\mathbf{N}_{\Omega}(p_{c})\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!1

which concludes the proof of the proposition.
Thus, we only need to prove (
6.2.3
), which we do next.

Start by observing that

\displaystyle\tfrac{d}{du}\log\mathbf{N}_{\Omega}(u)=\sum_{y\in\partial_{\rm in}\Omega}\tfrac{d}{du}\phi_{\Omega,u}^{0}[y\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{R/2}]=\tfrac{1}{u(1-u)}\sum_{y\in\partial_{\rm in}\Omega}\,\sum_{e\in\Omega}\mathrm{Cov}[y\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{R/2},\omega_{e}].

(6.21)

Fix some
y\in\partial_{\rm in}\Omega
and set
N:=\lfloor\log_{8}R\rfloor
.
Then, there exist points
z_{1},\dots,z_{N-2}
in
\Omega

such that for each
k
,
\Lambda_{8^{k}}(z_{k})\subset\Omega
but
y
is not connected to
\partial\Lambda_{R}
in
the subgraph
\Omega\setminus\Lambda_{2\cdot 8^{k}}(z_{k})
; the latter condition includes the situation where
y\in\Lambda_{2\cdot 8^{k}}(z_{k})
.
See Figure
14

and its caption for an explanation of this elementary fact.
For
k<\ell
, since
\Lambda_{2\cdot 8^{k}}(z_{k})
intersects the boundary of
\Omega
, but
\Lambda_{8^{\ell}}(z_{\ell})\subset\Omega
,
we conclude that
\Lambda_{8^{k}/2}(z_{k})
and
\Lambda_{8^{\ell}/2}(z_{\ell})
do not intersect.
Thus, the boxes
(\Lambda_{8^{k}/2}(z_{k}))_{k\leq N-2}
are pairwise disjoint.

Now, for any
e\in\Lambda_{8^{k}/2}(z_{k})
for one such
k
, a direct application of Theorem
4.8
implies that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}[y\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{R/2},\omega_{e}]\asymp\Delta_{u}(8^{k})\phi_{\Omega,u}^{0}[y\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{R/2}].

Summing over
e
and keeping in mind that all covariances in (
6.21
) are non-negative gives

\displaystyle\tfrac{d}{du}\phi_{\Omega,u}^{0}[y\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{R/2}]

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\sum_{k=1}^{N-2}\sum_{e\in\Lambda_{8^{k}/2}(z_{k})}\mathrm{Cov}[y\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{R/2},\omega_{e}]

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\sum_{k=1}^{N-2}8^{2k}\Delta_{u}(8^{k})\phi_{\Omega,u}^{0}[y\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{R/2}]

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\Big(\sum_{\ell=1}^{R}\ell\Delta_{u}(\ell)\Big)\phi_{\Omega,u}^{0}[y\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{R/2}].

For the last inequality, we used the fact that
\Delta_{u}(\ell)\asymp\Delta_{u}(8^{k})
for any
8^{k}\leq\ell\leq 8^{k+1}
.
Finally, summing over
y
, we find

\displaystyle\tfrac{d}{du}\mathbf{N}_{\Omega}(u)\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\mathbf{N}_{\Omega}(u)\sum_{\ell=1}^{R}\ell\Delta_{u}(\ell),

which concludes the proof of (
6.2.3
) and of the whole proposition.

\square

7
Proofs of the stability theorems

In this section, we prove stability results for crossing probabilities, arm events and
\Delta_{p}
.
First, observe that due to the previous section, Propositions
5.1
and
5.6
immediately lead to the following corollary.

Corollary 7.1

(i) Fix
\eta>0
. For every
p\in(0,1)
and every
\eta
-regular quad
(\mathscr{D},a,b,c,d)
at scale
R\leq L(p)
,

\tfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}p}\phi_{p}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]\asymp R^{2}\Delta_{p}(R)+\sum_{\ell=R}^{L(p)}\ell\Delta_{p}(\ell)\Delta_{p}(R,\ell),

(7.1)

where the constants in
\asymp
depend on
\eta
.

(ii) For every
p\in(0,1)
,
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{k}
and
r\leq R\leq L(p)
,

\tfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}p}\log\phi_{p}[A_{\sigma}(r,R)]\asymp R^{2}\Delta_{p}(R)+\sum_{\ell=R}^{L(p)}\ell\Delta_{p}(\ell)\Delta_{p}(R,\ell),

(7.2)

where the constants in
\asymp
depend on
\sigma
.

(iii) For every
p\in(0,1)
and two edges
e
and
f
at a distance
R\leq L(p)
of each other,

\big|\tfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}p}\log\mathrm{Cov}_{p}(\omega_{e},\omega_{f})\big|\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!R^{2}\Delta_{p}(R)+\sum_{\ell=R}^{L(p)}\ell\Delta_{p}(\ell)\Delta_{p}(R,\ell).

(7.3)

Proof

By (
1.21
) and (
1.16
), for any
p\in(0,1)
and
R\leq L(p)
,

\sum_{\ell=1}^{R}\ell\Delta_{p}(\ell)\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!R^{2}\Delta_{p}(R)
. Insert this into
Propositions
5.1
and
5.6
to obtain the desired results.

\square

We next prove the stability of crossing probabilities and arm events probabilities stated in Theorem
1.4
.

Proof of Theorem
1.4

Fix
p\neq p_{c}
.
We prove (
1.4
); the stability for arm events can be deduced similarly.
Fix some
\eta
-regular discrete quad
(\mathscr{D},a,b,c,d)
at some scale
R\geq 1
.
The inequality (
1.4
) is trivial when
R>L(p)
, and we focus on the case where
R\leq L(p)
.
Applying (
5.1
) to
u
between
p_{c}
and
p
, we find,

\displaystyle\tfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}u}\phi_{u}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]

\displaystyle\asymp R^{2}\Delta_{u}(R)+\sum_{\ell=R}^{L(p)}\ell\Delta_{u}(\ell)\Delta_{u}(R,\ell)+\sum_{\ell=L(p)}^{L(u)}\ell\Delta_{u}(\ell)\Delta_{u}(R,\ell)

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!(\tfrac{R}{L(p)})^{\delta}\,\Big[L(p)^{2}\Delta_{u}(L(p))+\sum_{\ell=L(p)}^{L(u)}\ell\Delta_{u}(\ell)\Delta_{u}(L(p),\ell)\Big],

where the inequality is due to a simple computation based on the quasi-multiplicativity of
\Delta_{p}
and the two inequalities from (
1.21
) (the constant
\delta
is actually given by this displayed equation).
The terms for
\ell\geq L(p)
are dominated by the corresponding sum in the second line.

Corollary
7.1
applied to
\mathscr{C}(\Lambda_{L(p)})
together with (
7
) imply that

\displaystyle\tfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}u}\phi_{u}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!(\tfrac{R}{L(p)})^{\delta}\,\tfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}u}\phi_{u}[\mathscr{C}(\Lambda_{L(p)})].

Integrate the above between
p_{c}
and
p
to find

\displaystyle|\phi_{p}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]-\phi_{p_{c}}[\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{D})]|\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!(\tfrac{R}{L(p)})^{\delta}|\phi_{p}[\mathscr{C}(\Lambda_{L(p)})]-\phi_{p_{c}}[\mathscr{C}(\Lambda_{L(p)})]|\leq(\tfrac{R}{L(p)})^{\delta}.

\square

Remark 7.2

One may also obtain the following improvement of (
1.11
) (similar to (
1.4
)):

\displaystyle\exp[-C(R/L(p))^{\delta}]\leq\frac{\pi_{1}(p,R)}{\pi_{1}(p_{c},R)}\leq\exp[C(R/L(p))^{\delta}]\qquad\text{ for all\penalty\ $R\leq L(p)$},

(7.6)

where
\delta,C>0
are universal constants depending only on
q
.

Moreover, since Corollary
7.1
applies to logarithmic derivatives of any arm event probabilities,
(
7.6
) also applies to
\pi_{\sigma}(p,R)
and
\pi_{\sigma}^{+}(p,R)
, with
C
depending on the color sequence
\sigma
.
Notice, however, that we do not claim to prove stability for probabilities of arm events in the half-plane with boundary conditions strictly on the half-plane.

We now turn to the proof of the stability of
\Delta_{p}
.
Let us note that for
q=4
, the stability of
\Delta_{p}
was proved also in Section
6.2
, as a step towards (
1.21
).

Proof of Theorem
1.6
(iii).

Fix
p\neq p_{c}
,
R\leq L(p)
and two edges
e
and
f
at a distance
R
of each other.
For
u
between
p
and
p_{c}
, Corollary
7.1
gives

\displaystyle\Big|\tfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}u}\log\mathrm{Cov}_{u}(\omega_{e},\omega_{f})\big|

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!R^{2}\Delta_{u}(R)+\sum_{\ell=R}^{L(u)}\ell\Delta_{u}(\ell)\Delta_{u}(R,\ell)\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\tfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}u}\phi_{u}[\mathscr{C}(\Lambda_{R})].

By integrating the above between
p_{c}
and
p
, we find

\frac{\mathrm{Cov}_{p}(\omega_{e},\omega_{f})}{\mathrm{Cov}_{p_{c}}(\omega_{e},\omega_{f})}\asymp 1.

(7.7)

Apply now (
5.6
) to deduce that

\Delta_{p}(R)\asymp\sqrt{\mathrm{Cov}_{p}(\omega_{e},\omega_{f})}\asymp\sqrt{\mathrm{Cov}_{p_{c}}(\omega_{e},\omega_{f})}\asymp\Delta_{p_{c}}(R).

\square

Remark 7.3

It will come to no surprise to the reader that the same type of improved stability as in (
7.6
) may be shown for the covariance. Getting the same result for
\Delta_{p}
itself seems more difficult as we crucially rely on an up-to-constant relation between
\Delta_{p}
and the covariance, and that the derivative of
\Delta_{p}
itself is less obvious.

8
Derivation of the scaling relations

This section is dedicated to proving the scaling relations (Theorems
1.9
,
1.10
and
1.11
).
The proof of the near-critical scaling relations (Theorem
1.11
) is based on the stability below the characteristic length (Theorem
1.4
and Theorem
1.6
(iii)). With the latter results at our disposal, the proofs of the critical and near-critical scaling relations (
1.23
)–(
1.28
) are very close to those for Bernoulli percolation and contain no significant innovation. For this reason, we are voluntarily quick on these proofs, trying to merely recall the crucial ingredients.

The main novelties in this section are the independent proof of the scaling relation involving the magnetic field (Section
8.3
), and the derivation of the scaling relation involving
\alpha
.

8.1
Scaling relations at criticality: proof of Theorem
1.9

In this section we work with
p=p_{c}
and we drop it from the notation.
We will prove a stronger result, which implies (
1.23
) when taking
x\in\partial\Lambda_{R}
.

Lemma 8.1

Fix
1\leq q\leq 4
. For every
R\geq 1
and every
x\in\Lambda_{R}
,

\pi_{1}(R)^{2}\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\phi_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}[0\xleftrightarrow{\Lambda_{2R}}x,0\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{R}]\leq\phi_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}[0\longleftrightarrow x]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\pi_{1}(|x|)^{2}.

(8.1)

Proof

The middle inequality is obvious. For the right one, observe that if
0
and
x
are connected, then

0\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{r}
and
x\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{r}(x)
, where
r:=\lfloor|x|/3\rfloor
.
The invariance under translations of
\phi_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
, the mixing property (
Mix
) and (
2.9
) give that

\phi_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}[0\longleftrightarrow x]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\pi_{1}(r)^{2}\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\pi_{1}(|x|)^{2}.

We now prove the left inequality. The FKG inequality (
FKG
) implies that

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}[0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Lambda_{2R}}}{{\longleftrightarrow}}x,0\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{R}]

\displaystyle\geq\phi_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}[A_{R},0\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{2R},x\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{3R}(x)]

\displaystyle\geq\phi_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}[A_{R}]\pi_{1}(2R)\pi_{1}(3R)

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\pi_{1}(R)^{2},

where we used (
RSW’
) and (
2.9
).

\square

Recall that
{\sf C}
is the cluster of
0
. Let
{\rm rad}({\sf C}):=\max\{r:{\sf C}\text{ intersects }\partial\Lambda_{r}\}
be the radius of
{\sf C}
.

Lemma 8.2

For every
R\geq 1
,

\displaystyle R^{2}\pi_{1}(R)

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\phi_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}[|{\sf C}|\,|R\leq{\rm rad}({\sf C})<4R]\leq\sqrt{\phi_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}[|{\sf C}|^{2}\,|R\leq{\rm rad}({\sf C})<4R]}\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!R^{2}\pi_{1}(R).

Proof

Fix
n\geq 1
. The inequality in the middle is the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.
For the first one,
observe that (
8.1
) and (
RSW’
) imply that

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}[|{\sf C}|\mathbbm{1}_{R\leq{\rm rad}({\sf C})<4R}]\geq\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{R}}\phi_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}[0\xleftrightarrow{\Lambda_{2R}}x,\Lambda_{2R}\not\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{4R}]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!|\Lambda_{R}|\pi_{1}(R)^{2}.

Divide the above by
\phi_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}[R\leq{\rm rad}({\sf C})<4R]\leq\pi_{1}(R)
to obtain the first inequality in (
8.2
).

We turn to the last inequality of (
8.2
). We have

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}[|{\sf C}|^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{R\leq{\rm rad}({\sf C})<4R}]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{x,y\in\Lambda_{4R}}\phi_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}[0\longleftrightarrow x,0\longleftrightarrow y,0\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{R}].

Fix
x,y\in\Lambda_{4R}

and assume first that
|x|\leq|y|\leq|x-y|
.
Set
\ell:=|x|
and
k:=|y|
.
Observe that the event on the right induces the following events which are listed along with the order – up to constants – of their probabilities (which are obtained thanks to (
2.9
)):

•

0\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{\ell/4}
– of probability of order
\pi_{1}(\ell)
;

•

x\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{\ell/4}(x)
– of probability of order
\pi_{1}(\ell)
;

•

y\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{k/4}(y)
– of probability of order
\pi_{1}(k)
;

•

\partial\Lambda_{\min\{2\ell,k/4\}}\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{k}
– of probability of order
\pi_{1}(k)/\pi_{1}(\ell)
;

•

\partial\Lambda_{\min\{2k,R\}}\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{R}
– of probability of order
\pi_{1}(R)/\pi_{1}(k)
.

Several iterations of the mixing property (
Mix
) imply that

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}[0\longleftrightarrow x,0\longleftrightarrow y,0\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{R}]

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\pi_{1}(\ell)\pi_{1}(k)\pi_{1}(R).

Observe now that for
1\leq\ell\leq k\leq 4R
, there are
8\ell
vertices
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with
|x|=\ell

and
8k
vertices
y
with
|y|=k
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in\Lambda_{4R}\\
|x|\leq|y|\leq|x-y|\end{subarray}}\phi_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}[0\longleftrightarrow x,0\longleftrightarrow y,0\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{R}]

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq k\leq 4R}\ell\pi_{1}(\ell)k\pi_{1}(k)\pi_{1}(R)\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!R^{4}\pi_{1}(R)^{3},

where in the last line we used that

\sum_{k=1}^{R}k\pi_{1}(k)\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!R^{2}\pi_{1}(R)

(8.8)

which is a consequence of (
2.2
) and (
2.9
). The same upper bound may be obtained for any of the other five possible orderings of
|x|,|y|,|x-y|
.
Overall, we conclude that

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}[|{\sf C}|^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{R\leq{\rm rad}({\sf C})<4R}]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!R^{4}\pi_{1}(R)^{3},

which gives the result by dividing by

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}[R\leq{\rm rad}({\sf C})<4R]

\displaystyle\geq\phi_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}[0\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{R},\Lambda_{R}\not\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{2R}]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\pi_{1}(R),

where in the last inequality we used the mixing property (
Mix
) and (
RSW’
).

\square

Proof of Theorem
1.9

Lemma
8.1
applied with
R=2|x|
and (
2.9
) directly imply (
1.23
).
We turn to the proof of (
1.24
).
Fix
R\geq 1
and
r:=\varphi(R)
.
Let us start with the lower bound on
\phi_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}[|{\sf C}|\geq R]
.
Let
c\in(0,1)
be the constant appearing in the first bound
\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!
of (
8.2
).
Then, using the definition of
\varphi
, (
8.2
), the Paley–Zygmund inequality and (
8.1
), we find that

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}[|{\sf C}|\geq\tfrac{c}{2}R]

\displaystyle\geq\phi_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}[|{\sf C}|\geq\tfrac{c}{2}r^{2}\pi_{1}(r)]

\displaystyle\geq\phi_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}[|{\sf C}|\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\phi_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}(|{\sf C}|\,|\,r\leq{\rm rad}({\sf C})<4r)]

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\frac{\phi_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}[|{\sf C}|\,|\,r\leq{\rm rad}({\sf C})<4r]^{2}}{\phi_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}[|{\sf C}|^{2}\,|\,r\leq{\rm rad}({\sf C})<4r]}\,\phi_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}[r\leq{\rm rad}({\sf C})<4r]

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\phi_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}[r\leq{\rm rad}({\sf C})<4r]

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\pi_{1}(r).

This concludes the proof of the lower bound since (
2.9
) implies that
\phi(\tfrac{c}{2}R)\asymp\phi(R)=r
.

We turn to the complementary upper bound. Using the Markov inequality in the third line and the definition of
\varphi
and (
8.1
) in the fourth, we obtain that

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}[|{\sf C}|\geq R]

\displaystyle=\phi_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}[|{\sf C}|\geq R,{\rm rad}({\sf C})>r]+\phi_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}[|{\sf C}|\geq R,{\rm rad}({\sf C})\leq r]

\displaystyle\leq\pi_{1}(r)+\phi_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}[|{\sf C}\cap\Lambda_{r}|\geq R]

\displaystyle\leq\pi_{1}(r)+\frac{1}{R}\sum_{u\in\Lambda_{r}}\phi_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}[0\longleftrightarrow u]

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\pi_{1}(r)+\frac{1}{r^{2}\pi_{1}(r)}\sum_{u\in\Lambda_{r}}\pi_{1}(|u|)^{2}\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\pi_{1}(r),

where the last inequality follows from (
2.2
) via the following computation

\frac{1}{k}\sum_{k=1}^{r}\frac{k\pi_{1}(k)^{2}}{r\pi_{1}(r)^{2}}\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\frac{1}{r}\sum_{k=1}^{r}(k/r)^{1-2c}\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!1.

\square

8.2
Scaling relations in the near-critical regime: proof of Theorem
1.11

By Theorem
1.3
and (
2.9
), we have that
L(p)\asymp\xi(p)
and
\pi_{1}(L(p))\asymp\pi_{1}(\xi(p))
, so we only need to show (
1.26
)–(
1.29
) with
L(p)
instead of
\xi(p)
.

8.2.1
Proof of (
1.26
) (scaling relation between
\beta
,
\nu
, and
\xi_{1}
)

On the one hand, the stability below the characteristic length (Theorem
1.4
) gives

\displaystyle\theta(p)\leq\pi_{1}(p,L(p))\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\pi_{1}(L(p)).

On the other hand, the FKG inequality (
FKG
), (
RSW’
), and Corollary
2.14
imply that

\displaystyle\theta(p)\geq\phi_{p}[0\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{2L(p)},A_{L(p)},\Lambda_{L(p)}\longleftrightarrow\infty]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\pi_{1}(p,2L(p))\geq\pi_{1}(2L(p))\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\pi_{1}(L(p)).

8.2.2
Proof of (
1.27
) (scaling relation between
\gamma
,
\nu
and
\xi_{1}
)

We start with the case
p<p_{c}
. First, Proposition
2.12
can be improved to

c\pi_{1}(R)^{2}\exp[-C|x|/L(p)]\leq\phi_{p}[0\longleftrightarrow x]\leq C\pi_{1}(R)^{2}\exp[-c|x|/L(p)],

(8.11)

where
R:=\min\{|x|,L(p)\}
and
c,C>0
are uniform constants.
Indeed, one simply needs to combine the proofs of Proposition
2.12
and Lemma
8.1
in a standard fashion.

Now, (
1.27
) follows by summing (
8.11
) over every
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and using that, by (
2.2
),

\pi_{1}(R)\leq C(L(p)/R)^{1/2-c}\pi_{1}(L(p)).

(8.12)

We now turn to the case
p>p_{c}
.

The only additional difficulty comes from the fact that we need to force 0 and
x
not to be connected to infinity.
For the lower bound, sum over every
x\in\Lambda_{L(p)/2}
the following inequality

\phi_{p}[0\longleftrightarrow x,0\not\longleftrightarrow\infty]\geq\phi_{p}[\Lambda_{L(p)}\not\longleftrightarrow\infty]\phi_{\Lambda_{L(p)},p}^{0}[0\longleftrightarrow x]\geq c\pi_{1}(R)^{2},

where the first inequality is due to the spatial Markov property (
SMP
),
and the second inequality follows (
RSW
), (

p

-MON
) and an argument similar to Lemma
8.1
.

For the upper bound, let
A_{x}^{*}
be the event that there exists a circuit in
\omega^{*}
surrounding 0 and
x
.
The FKG inequality in the first inequality, Corollary
2.14
and (
CBC
) in the second, and a reasoning similar to Lemma
8.1
in the third (based on (
Mix
) and Theorem
1.4
) imply that

\displaystyle\phi_{p}[0\longleftrightarrow x,0\not\longleftrightarrow\infty]

\displaystyle\leq\phi_{p}[0\longleftrightarrow x]\phi_{p}[A_{x}^{*}]

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\phi_{\Lambda_{R},p}^{1}[0\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{R/2}]^{2}\exp(-c|x|/L)

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\pi_{1}(R)^{2}\exp(-c|x|/L).

Summing this inequality over
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
gives the upper bound.

8.2.3
Proof of (
1.28
) (scaling relation between
\iota
and
\nu
)

Assume
p>p_{c}
, the case
p<p_{c}
is identical. Write
L=L(p)
. Use Corollary
7.1
and integrate the derivative of

g(p):=\phi_{p}[\mathscr{C}(\Lambda_{L})]
between
p_{c}
and
p
to get

\displaystyle\int_{p_{c}}^{p}L^{2}\Delta_{u}(L)du\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!g(p)-g(p_{c})\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!1.

In the other direction, let
p_{0}\in[p_{c},p]
be such that
L(p_{0})=RL(p)
for some
R>1
.
Theorem
1.4
and the definition of
L(p)
implies that

g(p)-g(p_{0})\geq g(p)-g(p_{c})-CR^{-\varepsilon}\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!1

(8.17)

provided that
R
is large enough.
For
u\in[p_{0},p]
, Corollary
7.1
, together with the quasi-multiplicativity property Theorem
1.6
(ii), implies

\displaystyle g^{\prime}(u)\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!L(u)^{2}\Delta_{u}(L(u))\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!L^{2}\Delta_{u}(L).

(Note that the constant in
\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!
depends on
R
.) Integrating the previous displayed equation between
p_{0}
and
p
and then using (
8.17
) gives

\displaystyle 1\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!g(p)-g(p_{0})\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\int_{p_{0}}^{p}L^{2}\Delta_{u}(L)du\leq\int_{p_{c}}^{p}L^{2}\Delta_{u}(L)du.

Together with (
8.2.3
), the previous displayed equation and the stability of
\Delta_{u}(L)
given by Theorem
1.6
(iii)
show that

\displaystyle 1\asymp\int_{p_{c}}^{p}L^{2}\Delta_{u}(L)du\asymp(p-p_{c})L^{2}\Delta_{p_{c}}(L),

which concludes the proof.

8.2.4
Proof of (
1.29
) (scaling relation between
\iota
and
\alpha
)

A straightforward computation involving (
1.12
) shows that

\displaystyle f^{\prime\prime}(p)=2\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}p}\phi_{p}[\omega_{e}]=2\sum_{f}\mathrm{Cov}_{p}(\omega_{e},\omega_{f}),

where
e
is a fixed edge of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and the sum is over all edges
f
.
By Lemma
5.3
applied to
\mathscr{D}
formed of the single edge
e
, we find

\mathrm{Cov}_{p}(\omega_{e};\omega_{f})\asymp\Delta_{p}(\ell\wedge L(p))^{2}e^{-c\ell/L(p)}\asymp\Delta_{p_{c}}(\ell\wedge L(p))^{2}e^{-c\ell/L(p)},

where
\ell
is the distance between
e
and
f
.
For the second equivalence, we use Theorem
1.6
(iii).
Summing the displayed equation above over all edges
f
, we conclude that

\displaystyle f^{\prime\prime}(p)\asymp\sum_{\ell=1}^{L(p)}\ell\Delta_{p_{c}}(\ell)^{2},

as required.

Remark 8.3

The above shows that if the phase transition is of second order (meaning that
f^{\prime\prime}(p)
diverges as
p
tends to
p_{c}
),
then
\sum_{\ell}\ell\Delta_{p}(\ell)^{2}
diverges, which, using the interpretation of crossing probabilities in terms of
\Delta_{p}(\ell)
,
implies that the crossing probabilities of quads for the infinite-volume measure are not differentiable at
p_{c}
.

Remark 8.4

When
\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\ell\Delta(\ell)^{2}
converges, the computation above simply proves that
f^{\prime\prime}(p)
remains bounded uniformly in
p
. Nevertheless, it is easy to deduce by differentiating one more time that for
p\neq p_{c}
(we drop
p
from the notation),

\displaystyle f^{\prime\prime\prime}(p)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{f,g}\phi[\omega_{e}\omega_{f}\omega_{g}]-\phi[\omega_{e}\omega_{f}]\phi[\omega_{g}]-\phi[\omega_{f}\omega_{g}]\phi[\omega_{e}]-\phi[\omega_{e}\omega_{g}]\phi[\omega_{f}]+2\phi[\omega_{e}]\phi[\omega_{f}]\phi[\omega_{g}]

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\sum_{\ell\leq L(p)}\ell\Delta(\ell)\Delta(\ell^{\prime},\ell)\sum_{\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell}\ell^{\prime}\Delta(\ell^{\prime})^{2}\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!L(p)^{4}\Delta(L(p))^{3}\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\frac{1}{|p-p_{c}|^{3}L(p)^{2}}.

If one defines
\alpha
in this framework by the formula
f^{\prime\prime\prime}(p)=|p-p_{c}|^{-\alpha-1-o(1)}
, we deduce that
\alpha\leq 2-2\nu
.
The converse bound does not follow by the same computation since the summand on the first line is not of definite sign;
at the time of writing the matching lower bound on
f^{\prime\prime\prime}(p)
remains unproved.

8.3
Scaling relation with magnetic field: proof of Theorem
1.10

We insist one last time on the fact that this section is independent of the rest of the paper. Below, we work with the graph
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with the addition of the ghost.
We drop
p_{c}
and
q
but keep
h
in the notation except when it is equal to
0
, in which case we omit it as well.
We start by a lemma relating certain quantities at
h=0
with the corresponding quantities at
h\geq 0
.

Set
\tilde{\pi}_{1}(h,R):=\phi_{\Lambda_{R},h}^{1}[0\leftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{R}]
,
where connections need to occur in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
Additionally, let
A_{R}^{*}
be the event that there exists a dual circuit in
{\rm Ann}(R,2R)
surrounding
\Lambda_{R}
and

\displaystyle\beta(h,R):=\phi_{{\rm Ann}(R,2R),h}^{1}[A_{R}^{*}].

Finally, for
C>1
, define

\displaystyle h_{c}(R)=h_{c}(R,C):=\inf\big\{h>0:\,\exists\ r\leq R\text{ such that }\tilde{\pi}_{1}(h,r)>C\tilde{\pi}_{1}(r)\text{ or }\beta(h,r)<C^{-1}\beta(r)\big\}.

Lemma 8.5

For any
C>1
, there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that for every
R
,

\displaystyle h_{c}(R)R^{2}\pi_{1}(R)\geq\varepsilon.

(8.18)

Due to the definition of
h_{c}
,
crossing estimates as in Theorem
2.1
apply in the regime of
(r,h)
with
r\leq R
and
h\leq h_{c}(R)
.
Indeed, the crossing probabilities in the primal model increase with
h
, which ensures the lower bounds.
For the upper bounds, observe that
\beta(h,r)
involves the boundary conditions that render dual crossings least likely.
Theorem
2.1
applied at
p_{c}
combined with the definition of
h_{c}(R)
implies the uniform positivity of
\beta(r,h)

for
r\leq R
and
h\leq h_{c}(R)
.
The FKG inequality and the monotonicity of boundary conditions imply lower bounds for crossing probabilities in the dual model, as claimed.

As a consequence, a similar proof to that of Lemma
8.1
applies for all
r\leq R
and
0\leq h\leq h_{c}(R)
, and yields

\displaystyle\phi_{{\rm Ann}(r,2r),h}^{1}[y\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{2r}]

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\pi_{1}({\rm dist}(y,\partial{\rm Ann}(r,2r)))

for
y\in{\rm Ann}(r,2r)
and

\displaystyle\phi_{\Lambda_{r},h}^{1}[0\longleftrightarrow y,0\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{r}]

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\pi_{1}(r)\pi_{1}(\|y\|\wedge{\rm dist}(y,\partial\Lambda_{n}))

\displaystyle\text{ for \penalty\ $y\in\Lambda_{r}$},

where the constants in
\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!
depend on
C
.

Proof of Lemma
8.5

Fix
C>1
and
r\leq R
. All constants in the signs
\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!
below are allowed to depend on
C
, but not on
r
or
R
.
The differential formula
[
Gri06
, Thm. 3.12]
reads

\displaystyle\tfrac{d}{dh}\tilde{\pi}_{1}(h,r)=\tfrac{1}{1-e^{-h}}\sum_{y\in\Lambda_{r}}\phi_{\Lambda_{r},h}^{1}[0\leftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{r},\omega_{y\mathfrak{g}}=1]-\phi_{\Lambda_{r},h}^{1}[0\leftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{r}]\phi_{\Lambda_{r},h}^{1}[\omega_{y\mathfrak{g}}=1].

(8.23)

Let us analyse the right-hand side of the above. Recall that
{\sf C}
is the cluster of the origin for the connectivity in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
First we show that the vertices
y\notin{\sf C}
have a negative contribution to (
8.23
).
Fix some
y\in\Lambda_{r}
.
For a set
\mathscr{C}\subset\Lambda_{r}
containing
0
and
y\notin\mathscr{C}
,

\displaystyle\phi_{\Lambda_{r},h}^{1}[0\leftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{r},\omega_{y\mathfrak{g}}=1,{\sf C}=\mathscr{C}]

\displaystyle=\phi_{\Lambda_{r},h}^{1}[\phi_{\Lambda_{r},h}^{1}(\omega_{y\mathfrak{g}}=1\,|\,{\sf C}=\mathscr{C})\,\mathbbm{1}_{0\leftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{r}}\mathbbm{1}_{{\sf C}=\mathscr{C}}]

\displaystyle\leq\phi_{\Lambda_{r},h}^{1}[\omega_{y\mathfrak{g}}=1]\phi_{\Lambda_{r},h}^{1}[0\leftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{r},{\sf C}=\mathscr{C}].

The first inequality is due (
SMP
) and (
CBC
) since
\phi_{\Lambda_{r},h}^{1}[\cdot\,|\,{\sf C}=\mathscr{C}]
is a random-cluster measure with free boundary conditions on
(\Lambda_{r}\setminus\mathscr{C})\cup\{\mathfrak{g}\}
,
and is stochastically dominated by the restriction of
\phi_{\Lambda_{r},h}^{1}
to
(\Lambda_{r}\setminus\mathscr{C})\cup\{\mathfrak{g}\}
.

Summing the above over every
\mathscr{C}\subset\Lambda_{r}
, we find that

\displaystyle\phi_{\Lambda_{r},h}^{1}[0\leftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{r},\omega_{y\mathfrak{g}}=1]-\phi_{\Lambda_{r},h}^{1}[\omega_{y\mathfrak{g}}=1]\phi_{\Lambda_{r},h}^{1}[0\leftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{r}]\leq\phi_{\Lambda_{r},h}^{1}[0\leftrightarrow y,0\leftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{r},\omega_{y\mathfrak{g}}=1].

Plugging this inequality in (
8.23
) gives

\displaystyle\tfrac{d}{dh}\tilde{\pi}_{1}(h,r)\leq\tfrac{1}{1-e^{-h}}\sum_{y\in\Lambda_{r}}\phi_{\Lambda_{r},h}^{1}[0\leftrightarrow y,0\leftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{r},\omega_{y\mathfrak{g}}=1]\leq\sum_{y\in\Lambda_{r}}\phi_{\Lambda_{r},h}^{1}[0\leftrightarrow y,0\leftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{r}],

where the second inequality comes from (
SMP
), which implies that

\phi_{\Lambda_{r},h}^{1}[\omega_{v\mathfrak{g}}=1\,|0\leftrightarrow y,0\leftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{r}]\leq 1-e^{-h}.

Now, assuming that
h\leq h_{c}(R)
, (
8.3
) applies, and we conclude that

\displaystyle\tfrac{d}{dh}\tilde{\pi}_{1}(h,r)\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\sum_{y\in\Lambda_{r}}\pi_{1}(r)\pi_{1}(\|y\|\wedge{\rm dist}(y,\partial\Lambda_{r}))\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!r\pi_{1}(r)\sum_{k=1}^{r/2}\pi_{1}(k)\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!r^{2}\pi_{1}(r)^{2}.

The second inequality uses the fact that there are at most order
r
vertices at distance
k
from
0
or
\partial\Lambda_{r}
;
the third one is a standard consequence of (
2.2
) and (
2.9
).
Keeping in mind that
\pi_{1}(r)\leq\tilde{\pi}_{1}(h,r)
, the above implies

\displaystyle\tfrac{d}{dh}\log\tilde{\pi}_{1}(h,r)\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!r^{2}\pi_{1}(r).

A similar computation, where
{\sf C}
is replaced by the cluster of
\partial\Lambda_{2r}
implies that

\displaystyle-\tfrac{d}{dh}\log\beta(h,r)\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\sum_{y\in{\rm Ann}(r,2r)}\phi_{{\rm Ann}(r,2r),h}^{1}[y\leftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{2r}]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!r^{2}\pi_{1}(r).

We are now in a position to conclude.
Let
c_{0}
be a constant larger than the constants involved in the inequalities
\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!
in (
8.3
) and (
8.3
).
Then, for
\varepsilon>0
, integrate these two inequalities for
h
between
0
and
h^{\prime}=\min\{h_{c}(R),\varepsilon/(R^{2}\pi_{1}(R))\}
.
We find

\displaystyle\left.\begin{array}[]{r}\log\tilde{\pi}_{1}(h,r)-\log\tilde{\pi}_{1}(r)\\
\log\beta(r)-\log\beta(h,r)\end{array}\right\}\leq c_{0}h^{\prime}r^{2}\pi_{1}(r)\leq\varepsilon c_{0}.

Now, for
\varepsilon<(\log C)/c_{0}
, the above shows that
h^{\prime}<h_{c}(R)
,
which implies (
8.18
).

\square

Proof of Theorem
1.10

Fix
h>0
. Again,
\mathsf{C}
is the cluster of the origin when considering connections in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
only.
We start with the lower bound. We have

\displaystyle\phi_{h}[0\longleftrightarrow\mathfrak{g}]

\displaystyle\geq\phi_{h}[0\longleftrightarrow\mathfrak{g},|{\sf C}|\geq 1/h]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\phi_{h}[|{\sf C}|\geq 1/h]\geq\phi_{0}[|{\sf C}|\geq 1/h]\geq\pi_{1}(\varphi(1/h)),

where the fourth inequality is due to (
1.24
), the third to monotonicity in
h
(

h

-MON
), and the second to the fact that conditioned on
|{\sf C}|\geq 1/h
, there is a positive probability for
0
to be connected to
\mathfrak{g}
. This last property can be easily deduced from the finite energy property, which states that every edge connecting
\mathbb{Z}
to
\mathfrak{g}
has a probability larger than
(1-e^{-h})/(1+(q-1)e^{-h})
and smaller than
h
of being open, regardless of the states of other edges.

Let us now derive the upper bound.
Let
\varepsilon
be the quantity given by Lemma
8.5
for some fixed
C>1
.
Choose
R
to be the largest integer such that
hR^{2}\pi_{1}(R)\leq\varepsilon
.
Notice that this implies that
h\leq h_{c}(R)
. We deduce that

\displaystyle\phi_{h}[0\longleftrightarrow\mathfrak{g}]

\displaystyle\leq\pi_{1}(h,R)+\phi_{h}[0\longleftrightarrow\mathfrak{g},\,0\not\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{R}]

\displaystyle\leq\pi_{1}(h,R)+\sum_{y\in\Lambda_{R}}\phi_{h}[0\longleftrightarrow y,\,\omega_{y\mathfrak{g}}=1]

\displaystyle\leq\pi_{1}(h,R)+h\sum_{y\in\Lambda_{R}}\phi_{h}[0\longleftrightarrow y],

where the last inequality uses the finite energy property.
Now, use that
h\leq h_{c}(R)
to deduce that
\pi_{1}(h,R)\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\pi_{1}(R)
and that, similarly to Lemma
8.1
,
\phi_{h}[0\longleftrightarrow y]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\pi_{1}(|y|)^{2}
. We deduce from the above that

\displaystyle\phi_{h}[0\longleftrightarrow\mathfrak{g}]

\displaystyle\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\pi_{1}(R)+h\sum_{y\in\Lambda_{R}}\pi_{1}(|y|)^{2}\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\pi_{1}(R)+hR^{2}\pi_{1}(R)^{2}\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\pi_{1}(R),

where in the second inequality we used a computation similar to (
1.27
).
Observe that, with the notation of Theorem
1.9
,
R=\varphi(\varepsilon/h)\!{\begin{array}[]{c}>\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\varphi(1/h)
, so

\phi_{h}[0\longleftrightarrow\mathfrak{g}]\!{\begin{array}[]{c}<\\[-10.0pt]
\scriptstyle{\frown}\end{array}}\!\pi_{1}(\varphi(1/h)),\penalty\

which concludes the proof.

\square
